Viktor Schauberger

Water – The Blood of the Earth
(Sampler from important written documents)

[Full English translation of Viktor Schauberger's "Wasser — Das Blut der Erde" sampler (oekotrend.ch, 2004 revision). Translated from https://oekotrend.ch/wp-content/uploads/2022/09/Viktor_Schauberger_Wasser_Das_Blut_der_Erde.pdf — 210 chunks, German original.]

-1-
-2-
INTRODUCTION

From "OUR SENSELESS WORK"

In every drop of good spring water there are more forces present than a medium-sized power plant of the present day is capable of generating.

These energies can be obtained effortlessly and almost cost-free if we follow the paths that nature constantly shows us and abandon the wrong paths that our present-day technology pursues.

Happiness and health, as well as unlimited quantities of energy, are available to us almost cost-free, if we once recognize that in water, in the blood of the Earth, the will and its resistance, life, reside — for which we struggle so hard today because we constantly rob this carrier of all life of its most noble quality through our actions: its soul.

The will of nature is the constructive process serving the whole, proceeding through atomic decomposition and atomic transformation. Its resistance is our stubborn, atom-smashing work, the selfish exploitation of nature.

The only possible consequence of our purely categorical division of the intellect, which is forced upon us as children in school, is the loss of creative endeavor. The human being loses his individuality, the ability to look at the thing in itself and thereby the interconnectedness of nature. He approaches the state of equilibrium, which is impossible in nature and which inevitably leads to a general economic decline.

Therefore the laws upon which we base our actions are also incorrect, because they operate within boundaries that do not exist.

The work we perform is our embodied will. The soul of this work is its effect. It brings happiness when this work is done correctly, and reliably misery when this work is done incorrectly.

-3-
Viktor Schauberger
on the lawful regularity of water movement

Vienna, January 1, 1930


---


The influence of the temperature of water was dismissed as too insignificant for channel treatment — that is, flood control, bank protection, for log driving and floating, for water supply, dam construction and hydraulic engineering in general — and likewise in all hydropower technology.

The differences found yield values that are mathematically too small for any noteworthy influence on the result to be derived from them.

It must first be stated that the temperature differences within water are a consequence of the temperature differences between the water and the surrounding medium.

If the temperature differences within the water are neglected, then the significance of the temperature difference between water and air, or external temperature — that is, the cause of the water cycle — is also denied.

The importance of the water cycle for all life on the Earth's surface hardly needs a word.

Equally great, though less apparent, as will be shown later, are the effects of temperature differences within the water, which, as must be admitted, have been neglected and excluded as too insignificant for all hydraulic calculations.

Years of observation, practical experiments, and correctly conducted measurements provided evidence that taking into account the internal temperature differences of water is not only decisive but an absolutely compelling necessity. Even the neglect (exclusion is impossible) of these temperature differences makes any practical use of water practically impossible.

The recognition of the importance of the effect of these temperature differences within water alone compels a revision of the foundations of the currently valid rules for all hydraulic engineering.

In addition to previous experience, a new component neglected until now comes into play, one that is very significant: the alteration of the internal water conditions through the internal stratification of water resulting from the temperature difference within it. This new viewpoint must now be incorporated into the accustomed conceptual framework, which leads to entirely new conclusions, so that we must also learn to rethink our fundamental principles.

A further gap appears in today's theories of the origin

-4-
of certain springs. Besides the well-known seepage springs, whose water reaches the Earth's surface by following the law of gravity through water-impermeable layers, there are also springs that occur high above any possible water accumulation in the manner of artesian wells, contrary to every known law. (Example: a spring on the Hohen Priel, which emerges approximately 100 m below the mountain peak at an altitude of over 2,000 m and carries water year-round.)


---


To arrive at the actual topic, namely the influence of temperature differences within water on its movement, it must first be pointed out that through the temperature differences in water, which have been completely neglected until now, a state of rest in water is altogether excluded.

Even in apparently standing water, very significant movements take place. These movements, to give an example, are capable of setting large masses of wood in motion. If an apparently stagnant body of water is irradiated by the sun on one side, the water surface rises at the irradiated point through warming, creating an inclined plane and a current toward the colder side. This in turn results in a circulation. Thus, even without a gradient of the bed, a movement of water occurs.

If water containing layers of different temperatures flows down a bed gradient, differently tempered — and therefore differently dense — water masses flow alongside and above one another in the same channel for a long time without mixing.

Each individual water particle is bound to a very specific velocity corresponding to its specific weight in the respective gradient. If the specific weight changes due to the gradient (greater velocity, greater friction, volume increase), the water cannot adapt to the new velocity without a transition phase.

The same occurs when the specific weight changes through external influences (solar irradiation). The water breaks, or as it is called, becomes turbulent.

Here lies the triggering of the hitherto unrecognized precision brake in moving water, which operates with a wonderful automaticity and is normally activated by the external temperature. In cool weather and at night, due to the inherently greater water velocity and the resulting increased friction of the water particles against the channel and against each other, the resulting change in volume and weight is sufficient.

All water threads approach the temperature of +4° C and thereby the density of 1, which has the consequence that the specific weight would

-5-
adapt to the increase in velocity. In this case, a constant increase in water velocity would occur. But through the increase in water velocity, the friction of the water particles among themselves is increased, which results in a rise in temperature and thereby an increase in volume.

This yields the following picture:
On the one hand, water surging ahead; on the other, a decrease in specific weight. The water thread tears, the water becomes turbulent. The forward movement of the water is resolved into vortex formation.

The greater the velocity of the forward movement, the greater and stronger the vortex formation. At a certain velocity, this vortex formation takes on such a violent character that an atomization of the water within the water can occur. It appears as if a cloud has formed within the water.


---


In summary, then, it can be said:

Turbulence is the lawfully occurring interruption of the forward movement of flowing water along the axis (the point of greatest velocity increase), caused by the circumstance that a specific velocity corresponds to each specific weight.

Turbulence is therefore the automatic triggering of the compensating movement, the automatic and doubly secured brake in every flowing water and channel.

With the recognition of the source and its conditions of origin, and the clear grasp of the functional phenomenon of turbulence, the lawful — and therefore unlimited — practical utilization of water in all directions is placed in human hands.

The following guiding principles are derived from the above; and with them the compelling necessity of reorganizing the entire water management system.

Everything flows — and all processes in the atmosphere
               are reflected in the interior of the Earth.

-6-
GUIDING PRINCIPLES

The quantity of water passing through a channel profile is never a homogeneous mass, but always exhibits layers of different temperatures.

The flow velocity of water in all channels corresponding to the respective gradient depends primarily on the differences in the degree of warmth of the individual water layers relative to one another.

The profile of a channel influences the flow velocity of water to the extent that the form and condition of the profile affect the temperature differences of the individual water layers.

The profile is a product of the processes that take place within the body of water passing through it.

Viktor Schauberger

-7-
Temperature and Water Movement
Excerpt from the treatise on the lawful regularity of water movement, deposited at the Academy of Sciences in Vienna

Special reprint from: "Die Wasserwirtschaft" (The Water Economy), 1930, Issue 20.

Mr. Viktor Schauberger has transmitted to me the enclosed treatise on temperature and water movement.

Since it has aroused my full interest through the completely new viewpoints it contains, which appear suited to act not only as a stimulus but even as a trailblazing influence on river and dam construction, I consider it in the general interest to make this work known to a wider readership and the professional world. In this sense, I recommend that you publish this interesting article.

With sincere respect

Forchheimer

Contents:

The significance of the temperature gradient for water movement;
Setting of the groundwater;
Flow of water on the Earth;
Some remarks on river regulation;
Significance of groundwater for agriculture;

The catastrophic floods occurring ever more frequently in recent years on the one hand, and the increasing water scarcity becoming ever more apparent in some regions on the other, give rise to the question of whether the manner of water management practiced up to now, as well as other measures taken by human hand, may not be partly to blame for these evils.


---


There are above all two factors that should be examined from this viewpoint: today's practice of river regulation and the increasing deforestation of the forests.

Before addressing the matter itself, an important circumstance should be emphasized in the introduction that has been disregarded in all hydraulic engineering measures until now: the temperature of

-8-
water in relation to the Earth and air temperature, as well as the temperature differences within the flowing or standing water itself (temperature gradient).

If even small temperature differences suffice to bring about the obvious changes of state of water (liquid, gaseous, and solid), then it is easily understood that larger temperature differences existing within flowing or standing water must decisively influence the movement of water on and in the Earth.

In what follows, these hitherto overlooked connections between temperature and water movement will be discussed, and those errors will be pointed out that have been made through neglect of this important connection.

I. Temperature gradient — Full (see figure) and half circulation

The movement and distribution of the water returning from the atmosphere to the surface is under the influence of the inherent temperature of the water and that of the surrounding Earth and air layers.

-9-
If the temperature of the water falling on the Earth is higher than that of the Earth layers that are to absorb the water, then through temperature equalization the water, becoming specifically heavier due to its cooling, can penetrate into the interior of the Earth without difficulty. As it sinks further, after reaching the temperature of +4°C — which is simultaneously its point of greatest density — it eventually reaches higher-temperature Earth layers again and becomes specifically lighter through temperature adaptation.

The further it sinks due to the pressure of the heavy water above it, the greater its own resistance to the downward movement becomes, because of its ever-decreasing specific weight. Eventually an equilibrium state forms, and through this the setting of the supremely important elevation of the groundwater table. Under very specific pressure conditions, a water layer of 4°C temperature forms in the groundwater, which in the following shall be referred to as the middle layer.

In the case described above, we are dealing with a positive temperature gradient, if by temperature gradient we understand the arithmetically taken difference per unit length between the temperature of the falling water and the Earth temperature.

The above case also represents a full circulation of water, which


---


- 10 -
which, in repetition of what was said above, is characterized by the following phases:
Penetration of water into the Earth, passing through the groundwater middle layer of 4°C, purification at this temperature, further sinking under the influence of its own weight in underground watercourses, transition to the vaporous state under the influence of the hot interior of the Earth, rising again to the Earth's surface while simultaneously absorbing nutrients, cooling and deposition of nutrients, flowing away on the Earth's surface, evaporation, cloud formation, falling again as rain, etc.

In warm soils the groundwater layer is absent, and with it the counterweight for the pressure coming from below. If the temperature of the falling water is lower than that of the uppermost Earth layer, the water initially sinks to a certain depth, warms up, and becomes specifically lighter. Eventually it is driven back to the surface by the pressure from below and flows away — if it does not immediately evaporate again — down the bed gradient.

In this case we are dealing with a negative temperature gradient (water temperature lower than Earth surface temperature, see figure next page). A full circulation no longer forms, but only a half circulation — that is, water falling on the Earth, surface runoff, evaporation, cloud formation, falling again as rain.

For closer explanation regarding the temperature gradient, and for better understanding of what follows, the following should also be stated,

- 11 -
namely:
With an initially negative temperature gradient (warm Earth, cold rain), a simultaneous drop in atmospheric temperature can cool the Earth to such an extent that the temperature gradient returns to 0. The same case can occur with an initially positive temperature gradient when the water masses penetrating into the soil are so large that they are capable of warming the soil. In both cases, upon reaching a temperature gradient of 0, the runoff follows the bed gradient until temperature gradients are re-established through friction phenomena, etc. In both cases it is necessary to establish the required temperature gradient by means of correctly tempered supplementary water, in order to brake the water masses flowing freely and almost without resistance down the bed gradient.

II. Groundwater table

Depending on the temperature of the Earth layers, which in turn are co-influenced by those of the penetrating water masses, the height of the groundwater table is a variable one. The air temperature also plays a major role here.


---


Locally impounded and thereby deeply cooled water masses at the bed influence the temperature of the surrounding Earth layers. These are cooled and thereby a steady positive temperature gradient is produced, since the falling rain masses will always be warmer than the colder Earth. The precondition for the penetration of rainwater is thus given. Not only will a rise in the groundwater table occur, but the absorptive capacity of the soil will also increase downward and laterally.

The groundwater middle layer of 4°C mentioned earlier is pressed downward as a result of the increased pressure of the water masses resting upon it, and thereby the resistance offered by the warmer and specifically lighter water lying below the middle layer to further penetration is overcome.

This leads to the formation of a natural underground reservoir — a retention basin — which prevents the rapid surface discharge of water and brings about a full circulation of the same. The release of water from this reservoir occurs as described later, but can also occur through lateral expansion of the middle layer due to the pressures acting upon it from above and below (spring formation).

The accompanying sketch shows schematically a cross-section through a groundwater reservoir. From it one can see how the middle layer (which is under the greatest pressure of all layers) can flow laterally or also upward.

- 12 -
In contrast, as described in Section I, high Earth surface temperatures allow almost no water to penetrate. The formation of groundwater does not occur or establishes itself only to a small extent and at great depths. The soil becomes ever less receptive through the evaporation of any groundwater that may be present in small quantities. The penetrating water is brought to surface runoff through the effect of the excessively high soil temperature and thereby, in connection, to rapid re-evaporation (half circulation). Thus no groundwater formation in the sense described earlier occurs. (In such regions warm springs frequently appear, which are pressed to the surface through fissures in the Earth by the pressure acting from below, against which no counterpressure is exerted due to the absence of groundwater.)

The absorptive capacity of the soil is thus dependent on the temperature conditions which, as described above, cause the setting of the groundwater table, and thereby on the existence and height of the groundwater table.

In summary it can be said:
A positive temperature gradient is the precondition for the absorptive capacity of the soil and for the formation of groundwater, and in connection therewith: the formation of full circulations.
A negative temperature gradient permits no groundwater formation. It produces half circulations. (see sketches above)

III. Flow of water


---


A flow of water below the Earth's surface (groundwater flow) occurs, as already stated earlier, under the pressure acting from above and below, as well as through temperature differences within the Earth and water layers surrounding the groundwater. What has hitherto been understood as groundwater flow alone was only the draining away of those groundwaters that sit upon a water-impermeable layer and move along its gradient until they could rejoin the full circulation.

Water reaches the surface flow in two cases:

1. With a negative temperature gradient as written in Section I, immediately (sketch; );
2. With a positive temperature gradient, when the groundwater table — after saturation has occurred (sketch;) — rises above the Earth's surface under the influence of solar radiation.

- 13 -
This also explains a phenomenon often observed in the mountains, namely: Days of rain cause no particular rise in water flow in the receiving streams. The rainwater is almost entirely absorbed by the Earth.

Only after the onset of warm weather does flood flow occur in the receiving streams. The cold groundwater rises to the Earth's surface layer, which has now been warmed by the influence of the sun and the warm air. The previously existing positive temperature gradient transforms into a negative one. The water flows away. One commonly says: The mountain lets go.

The preconditions for the emergence of floods are far greater in case 1, all the more so because, as a result of the initially negative Earth temperature gradient, a positive temperature gradient develops during the now-direct runoff of the water masses, whereby the water gradually tends to penetrate into the Earth, loosens and carries away sediment. In addition to the bed gradient, there is also a surface gradient. A further increase in flow velocity and tractive force for sediment occurs. In the later course, and after another reversal of the now-positive temperature gradient into a negative one, curve formation occurs through turbulence falls and thereby a mechanical deceleration of the movement. The carried sediment is deposited and the following water masses are impounded.

The consequence of this: Floods.


---


If, in case 2, saturation of the groundwater basin occurs as a result of a persistent positive temperature gradient, then the groundwater (spring) water now reaching the surface is colder than the Earth layer immediately beneath it. The temperature gradient has reversed and become negative. A rapid runoff of the cold heavy water masses ensues. The heavy surface water of the Earth that flows away only gradually comes into a positive ratio due to the relatively low Earth temperature, since the heavy water warms very slowly. As a result of a bed gradient that is mostly too large in the upper third of the catchment area, turbulence falls form and thus curves in the horizontal course. The further transition from the negative to the positive temperature gradient therefore proceeds slowly and with the help of strong turbulence falls, which in turn lead to overly sharp horizontal curves and thereby to sediment deposition on the one hand, scouring on the other, and displacements of the channel bed through mechanical effects. The next consequence of this water flow is widening of the channels, throwing up of broad gravel banks, evaporation or sinking of the water thus again exposed to the external temperature influence in the churned-up channel bed (the water flow of the Alps that has already become typical; with always one-sidedly deepened bed and gravel bank on the opposite side). The water flow

- 14 -
occurs just as in the first case in a jerky manner. Erosion of the banks is the consequence. In the event of flood, mechanical braking occurs through the curves thus formed, the banks are eroded even more — an even worse situation than before. The consequences are again: Floods.

But in order to contain the flood danger at its root, it is necessary to artificially eliminate the two extreme cases 1 and 2.


---


Through the creation of impounding facilities with appropriately built-in precautions, the flow conditions — where they have been incorrectly set due to shifts in the Earth layer temperatures and the resulting Earth layer temperature gradient — become regulatable again in the above sense. Through these regulating impoundments, the flow conditions can be automatically adjusted according to the respective daily temperature, such that primarily the two extreme cases described above are reliably eliminated and appropriate intermediate temperatures automatically establish themselves in the flow conditions. With ever finer adjustment of the simple apparatuses provided in these impoundments — meant is the air-controlled diving bell from Patent No. 136214 — the temperature gradients adapted to the annual mean temperature gradually emerge, and thus it is possible to influence and gradually prevent the flood danger at its origin.

The consequence is: no flood danger, because through adaptation of the runoff water masses to the annual average temperature, the correct Earth temperature gradients are established, thereby the absorptive capacity of the soil, the correct setting of the groundwater table, and thus the formation of the extremely important retention basins is made possible. Through the correctly adjusted flow conditions, a uniformly regulated continuation of water on the Earth's surface results, and no locally limited evaporation, thereby in turn no locally limited and rapidly successive precipitation — in a word, the re-regulation of orderly full circulation conditions is achieved.

Where the drainage of areas is concerned, it is likewise possible, by bringing about the favorable temperature gradient for this case (positive temperature gradient), to make the undesired stagnant water disappear.

It is thus possible to produce a full circulation or a half circulation at will, whereas the impounding facilities built hitherto have one-sidedly produced only a half circulation.

In this connection, reference should once again be made to the significance of the full

- 15 -
and half circulation:

Full circulation means the entry of water into the interior of the Earth, the creation of the necessary groundwater masses, the deceleration of water runoff, and thereby the prevention or reduction of flood danger. Cold springs also form continuously, whose water masses, after merging with the receiving stream, create new low temperatures in it and help co-prevent too-rapid evaporation along the way.

With half circulation, by contrast, the well-known condition of repeatedly forming rising water vapor occurs — that is, a constant addition to the atmospheric water masses, and in connection therewith, frequent precipitation.

One flood thus produces the next.

- 16 -
IV. Fundamental principles of river regulation


---


Creating the correct flow conditions is, however, not only of great importance in the sense indicated above, but also for the regulation of river courses and the formation of their banks, which will now be briefly discussed.

The goal of today's practice in carrying out river regulation is, as previously mentioned, to achieve the fastest possible removal of water, which is generally attempted through bank corrections, securing the banks with artificial structures, and so on. However, this type of regulation is entirely one-sided and does not fulfill its purpose.

It cannot and must not be the engineer's task to correct nature. His task is rather to fathom the natural processes as far as possible and to imitate the examples that nature provides in healthy watercourses in those watercourses that require regulation.

Here again it becomes apparent that the relationship between water and air temperature is the most important factor that must not be neglected in any regulation.

The natural regulators of water flow are forests and lakes. Forests, by keeping the soil cool in their area, create a constant, positive temperature gradient and, as a consequence, groundwater reservoirs that have a retarding effect on the removal of precipitation water. The cold springs that issue from these groundwater reservoirs in turn flow toward the receiving waters, cool their water masses, and thus prevent their premature evaporation along the way. The cycle of water — the transition from the earth to the atmosphere — is thereby also delayed and extended spatially. The cycles do not take place within relatively small territories, so that one rainfall does not produce another, one flood does not produce another.

Natural lakes fulfill the same function.
Where forests have been cleared and natural lakes are absent, a substitute must now be created, and this is, as already stated in Section III, the artificial reservoir, which must, however, be properly constructed and properly operated. Only then can it bring about the functions cited: groundwater formation, flow delay, and generation of the correct temperature gradient. Reservoirs have indeed often been built to establish a regulated water management system, but they have not always proven successful; indeed, they have often brought about the very opposite of what was intended.

This shall be briefly explained in the following: The reservoir installations as they are built and operated today are nothing more than pure water storage. They collect water, also fulfill the effect of retention of precipitation water to a certain degree, but almost always produce a half-cycle. The water reaches the surface or soon after its outflow undergoes rapid evaporation.

- 17 -


---


Precipitation in the area of current reservoirs becomes irregular; it increases or decreases depending on the aspect of the valley (wind direction), the normal water flow in the middle and lower reaches of rivers declines, and likewise the groundwater table in the middle and lower reaches drops; the soil yield in these areas decreases conspicuously. This is solely and exclusively due to the fact that the construction of such reservoirs in the downstream river course creates an entirely one-sided temperature gradient through the constant release of heavy or light water, depending on whether the water is discharged through bottom outlets or over weirs. Both types of discharge lead to the extreme cases described earlier and thus to the formation of half-cycles with their known adverse consequences for the origin and impact of floods.

It is now the task of a properly constructed reservoir and an outflow regulation equipped with the necessary provisions to regulate the temperature gradient in the river course from the dam in such a way that the phenomena described above are reliably avoided.

With this type of regulation — namely through regulation of the temperature gradient — costly and ineffective installations in the river course itself become unnecessary.

When we have spoken so far of properly constructed reservoirs, this refers to the fact that the slight water movement present in every reservoir is intensified through the creation of a strong temperature gradient, and thus, through the installations to be provided, cool water layers repeatedly reach the surface, whereby the excessive evaporation with its adverse consequences, which has previously occurred on reservoirs, is greatly reduced.

As is apparent from the above exposition, flood hazards can practically only be contained when the water masses, through lawful, natural, and correct treatment, are no longer conveyed into the atmosphere as rapidly as possible as before, but are instead directed to their proper purpose — namely, via the detour through the earth, to the full cycle and thereby to the nutrient supply (see also: "Temp. and Water Movement 6") of the soil. One sees that two cardinal errors have been committed so far: by rapidly draining water over the earth's surface, the water masses were returned to the atmosphere too quickly, thereby causing renewed precipitation and floods, and furthermore the water was diverted from its most important purpose — namely, penetration into the earth — and the nutrient supply of the soil was obstructed through prevention of the full cycle.

- 18 -


---


River regulation that, without regard for the temperature gradient, deals exclusively with the removal of water masses through the less important bed gradient, ultimately leads to a disturbance in the proper succession of temperature gradients, or rather, to the formation of a one-sided temperature gradient, and thereby to the catastrophes and floods that, for example, must now occur with increasing severity in France and will also propagate southward, unless the evil is grasped at its root and the effects are remedied through transformation of the governing causes described here.

The movement of water in the riverbed itself and the possibilities of influencing it will be discussed in a later essay.

Many additional illustrations and constructions are found at the back in the appendix.

- 19 -

V. The Importance of Groundwater for Agriculture

Having drawn attention in the preceding sections to the errors made so far in carrying out hydraulic engineering tasks and having pointed out a way to avoid them, the following will particularly address the catastrophic consequences that incorrect water management brings about.

Through incorrect treatment of waters, not only does an immediate and acute threat to riverside inhabitants arise, but what is far worse, a creeping evil that manifests itself in the reduction of soil productivity — namely: decline of groundwater, or the other extreme: swamping.

If we merely consider the changes in cultivated land for fine produce that have taken place over the period of a human lifetime, and consider that in places where our grandfathers still had rich farmland, today hardly any grass grows, it becomes clear to us at what pace soil productivity is declining. (For example, the cultivated area for wheat and rye has fallen from 273 million hectares to 246 million hectares in the last three decades.) This decline in yield is particularly stark in mountainous regions, which naturally feel the decline of groundwater first. On alpine pastures where it once made no difference whether hundreds more head of cattle were driven up, today those with grazing rights quarrel over the feed needed for a single animal. The once almost inexhaustible pastures today do not even suffice for a fraction of the former stocking levels.

- 19 (cont.) -


---


This decline in soil yield has its cause solely and exclusively in the fact described above — that the groundwater table has receded and continues to recede steadily. The soil, which is to produce a good yield, must constantly be supplied with the substances that plants need for their development. The mediator and carrier of these substances is the groundwater, which in its internal cycle constantly brings fresh nutrient salts upward from the interior of the earth. When the groundwater recedes, the supply of nutrients by natural means ceases. Artificial fertilization and doubled diligence can only provide partial, temporary replacement for the natural supply of substances. The precipitation coming from the atmosphere is merely a moistening. Nutrients for plants are not contained in it.

Nature itself is not responsible for the ever-increasing desiccation of the earth's surface, which is a consequence of the lowering of the groundwater table. Rather, since time immemorial, everything has been done by human hand — albeit unconsciously — to steadily lower the water and with it the natural nutrients.

- 20 -

The importance of the temperature gradient for the lawful movement of water was previously unknown. Water was therefore mostly treated incorrectly. Humans have, for example, in utilizing the energy contained in water for the generation of electricity, made arbitrary installations in riverbeds and so forth, which have often had water-destroying effects. Attempts have been made to correct rivers from the banks, naturally with negative results. It has never occurred to anyone to restore the equilibrium that was disturbed through installations in the river itself, through deforestation, and so forth, by means of other measures.

The path shown here — namely, to artificially re-establish the temperature gradient that otherwise establishes itself in nature under normal circumstances, where it is needed — is the only correct solution for achieving natural water drainage, or rather, water retention in the soil. Only by following this path, or rather, by utilizing these insights, which are the result of many years of observation by the author, can a further decline of groundwater and thereby also a further decline of soil yield be prevented.

Only in this way will it also be possible to contain water catastrophes and to make of water what it always was and must always be: a giver of life.

- 21 -

Temperature and Water Movement
Foundations of River Regulation with Consideration of
Temperature Conditions in Flowing Water
by Viktor Schauberger

Contents:
Turbulence phenomena in flowing water
Temperature gradient/bed gradient and curve formation
Geographical position and Earth's rotation in their influence on the river course
The general tasks of river regulation
Regulation of the temperature gradient


---


In the following treatise, the most important factors influencing a river course will be discussed in brief outline, and subsequently means and ways will be presented that lead to a natural regulation of a river course. Detailed questions will not be addressed here.

1. Turbulence Phenomena in Flowing Water

In an ideal liquid flowing down a gradient without friction, the individual stream filaments would have to move parallel to one another, and furthermore, according to the law of gravity, the motion would have to be uniformly accelerated. This case does not occur in nature, however, because friction phenomena between the liquid and the channel walls, as well as those between the liquid particles themselves, come into play. Since energy is thereby consumed, the motion is no longer accelerated but uniform, provided pulsations etc. are disregarded. As long as the water moves in layers (laminar) — wall friction being disregarded for the moment — in a non-ideal, viscous liquid, a portion of the energy is converted into heat. At a certain velocity, which varies according to the temperature of the water, the laminar motion changes to a swirling, turbulent one. In turbulent motion as well, part of the energy is converted into heat, as shown by the experiments of Barnes and Coker, while another part of the energy is consumed through the exchange of momentum. On this subject, Prof. Forchheimer says: "....in the case of swirling motion, the mean flow can be converted not only into heat but also into vortices, and perhaps conversely an acceleration of the mean motion can occur through a decrease of the vortices; however, no experience is available in this regard."

- 22 -

From the author's observations it now follows:

1. Turbulence is smallest in water at +4°C temperature — under equal conditions in one and the same profile.

2. The turbulence and — in connection with it — the velocity reduction become greater the more the water's temperature deviates from +4°C.

3. It is possible to achieve an acceleration of the mean motion by approaching the water temperature toward +4°C.

Fig. 1 (This illustration could not be reproduced due to its poor quality) shows the extraordinarily strong turbulence and vortex phenomena at the confluence of a hot spring into the Tepl at Karlsbad. While, when the hot spring is blocked, the water of the Tepl at this point flows off with considerable velocity due to the existing steep bed gradient, this velocity decreases immediately to an extraordinary degree after the inflow of the hot spring water.


---


The great influence of water temperature on turbulence and velocity could also be observed at a log flume installation in Neuberg (see the chapter "Principle of Small Waterways") (Styria). Here, temperature and velocity measurements were carried out over a 2 km stretch during log transport. In the morning, at a water temperature of 9 to 10°C, a block of wood needed 29 minutes; at noon, at a temperature of 13 to 15°C, however, approximately 40 minutes to cover this 2 km stretch under otherwise identical conditions.

As a further example, the water supply to the turbines of the Neuwild cardboard factory in Lower Austria can be cited. The water supply is through two concrete channels of approximately equal length. One draws its water from the warmer "Stillen Mürz," the other from the so-called "Kalten Mürz" (Cold Mürz). The former runs on the sunny side, the latter on the shaded side, both flowing to the common water castle. The volume of water drawn from the Stillen Mürz at full utilization of the channel profile amounts to approximately 860 l/sec. At times when the water temperature of the Stillen Mürz approaches that of the Kalten Mürz and a positive temperature gradient establishes itself in the supply channel from the Stillen Mürz, the water volume — according to observations by the factory manager, Mr. Ing. Brückner, and the operations manager, Mr. Patta — increases to 1800 l/sec. Despite throttling of the intake openings before the turbine, the

- 23 -
output of the turbine rises, which results in an increased production of 1 wagon per day.

2. Temperature Gradient, Bed Gradient, and Curve Formation

The formulas currently in use for calculating water velocity in channel profiles take into account the geometric profile of the channel, the roughness of the channel walls, and the gradient (bed gradient, water surface gradient, or energy line gradient). Not taken into account in these formulas are the physical properties of water that change with temperature, such as viscosity and specific weight. It is, however, important to consider the course of temperature in the direction of flowing water — that is, the temperature gradient — and by this term is understood the change in temperature per unit length in the direction of flow.

The temperature gradient is referred to below as positive when the temperature of the water approaches the temperature of 4°C in the direction of flow, and as negative in the other case.


---


If, for example, the temperature at point A of a channel is t1°, at a downstream point B t2°, and t1 > t2 (positive temperature gradient), then at this location an increase in velocity occurs due to a reduction in turbulence. The rollers and vortex formations become smaller. In the reverse case, t1 < t2 (negative temperature gradient), the turbulence phenomena intensify due to the temperature increase, and the resulting loss of kinetic energy manifests itself in a reduction of velocity. The tractive force diminishes, leading to deposition of the suspended sediment being carried along.

In the section on tractive force and bedload movement, Weyrauch remarks in his book "Hydraulisches Rechnen," 4th ed., p. 68: "The critical tractive force depends on the type of bedload and is therefore constant for a river stretch that is not too long and without tributaries; on longer stretches without tributaries it decreases downstream."

The explanation for this is given by the above (case of the negative temperature gradient). Where tributaries are present, however, that again feed colder water to the main river and thereby, with a reduction in turbulence, cause an increase or at least a maintenance of velocity, this weakening of the tractive force does not occur. One can say: the tractive force is maintained or increases with a positive temperature gradient; it decreases with a negative temperature gradient.

- 24 -

This phenomenon becomes important when considering changes in the riverbed. With a positive temperature gradient — assuming uniform water flow — the bed gradient is maintained or becomes steeper; with a negative temperature gradient, it becomes flatter.

When the water volume increases and the temperature gradient is negative, the bed profile does not change significantly; instead, bank erosion occurs due to lateral shifting of the main current. When the water masses increase and the temperature gradient is positive, the riverbed is attacked and deepened. The watercourse straightens: earlier sediment curves are evened out.

Under certain circumstances, falling water masses (recession after a flood) with a simultaneous temperature drop, especially at night, can become more dangerous than quantitatively larger water masses during warm rainy weather under a negative temperature gradient.

The mean bed gradient that establishes itself over time corresponds to the temperature gradient associated with the annual mean temperature and the mean water flow during the year, whereby annual mean temperature and precipitation volume are also related to a certain extent. In those years in which larger fluctuations in the annual mean temperature occur, relatively larger changes in the riverbed will also take place.


---


Temperature measurements at various points within the same river cross-section show that temperatures also vary within it, and the point of greatest water velocity (river axis, main current) also changes its position in the profile — both laterally and vertically — over the course of a day. The lowest temperature is always found in the main current, while it increases more or less toward the banks. During the day, the main current lies closer to the shaded bank, since the heavier water establishes itself there, while the lighter water flows along the sun-exposed bank. At night, the river axis migrates toward the center of the channel due to the enlargement of the heavy-water side. With a negative temperature gradient, the main current lies near the water surface; with a positive temperature gradient, it lies deeper.

During log transport, the following phenomenon is observed: when the temperature of the surroundings is lower than that of the water (i.e., the temperature gradient is entirely positive), log transport proceeds most easily. The logs remain in the middle of the channel and float away in the distinctly pronounced main current. On warm days, particularly toward noon, deposits of logs occur. Knot formations easily arise, since the main current wanders (cross-currents of the water masses due to turbulence phenomena) and does not maintain its position uniformly over longer periods as it does with a positive temperature gradient.

- 25 -


---


In Fig. 2, a portion of a river stretch is depicted. In cross-section 1-1', the main current is still in the middle of the river. If one determines the mean velocity in all verticals of the cross-section and draws the energy line for these, it becomes apparent — as is to be expected — that there is a more or less steep energy decline toward the riverbank. If this exceeds a certain limit, it is self-evident that this state can only be a labile one, and small causes suffice to change this state. If, for example, the bank at point 1 is shaded (see sketch), while the bank at point 1' is exposed to solar radiation (see Fig. 2), the water at 1' will warm up, become specifically lighter, and, due to increased turbulence, flow more slowly than at 1. The consequence is that the heavy water flowing along the left bank (see Fig. 3) will surge ahead, whereby the beginning of the circular movement indicated in Fig. 3 is already created. The center of rotation lies outside the river profile. A new equilibrium position is formed (section 2-2'). This circular movement continues until heavy and light water have equalized in temperature and velocity. The temperature gradient in the cross-section itself, which was previously negative from the left to the right bank (in cross-section 2-2', see Fig. main current), reverses and becomes negative from the right to the left bank, since, as the main current bends increasingly to the right (see Fig. 3), lighter, higher-temperature, and slower-flowing water forms to the left of it. At the point where the temperature gradient in the cross-section reverses, the ford (cross-section 3-3') is formed through the weakening of the tractive force (through the transfer of energy from the heavy water to the light water on the right bank).

If one compares the river profile with the corresponding energy line, one recognizes that both lines are similar to each other.

Curve formation in the river course usually occurs where, favored by climatic position, larger temperature fluctuations occur within short time periods. (Where a river emerges from the mountains onto the plain, etc.) A straight stretch with regular, bilateral deposition of sediment, on the other hand, forms where the temperature gradient remains positive over long stretches during most of the year.

Fig. 4 (note: This illustration could not be reproduced due to poor image quality) shows a stretch shortly before

- 26 -
the confluence of the Tepl into the Eger. On this stretch, the temperature gradient is always positive, since the water warmed upstream by the inflow of the hot spring cools along the way. On this stretch, the Tepl shows a consistently straight course with bilateral, regular bank formation.

3. Influence of Geographical Position and Earth's Rotation on the River Course

For the development of a river course, in addition to the influence of the terrain and that of the temperature gradient described above, the influences of geographical position and the Earth's rotation are also decisive.


---


The influences arising from geographical position are essentially expressed through the development of the temperature gradient.

In Sweden, for example, the uniform climate favors the development of a positive or only weakly developed negative temperature gradient; the water flow of rivers is uniform, as is the sediment transport. The riverbed is entirely regular, mostly trough-shaped (Fig. 5). The adaptation of the heavy water masses to the climatic conditions of the valley lowlands proceeds slowly, and the water temperatures are maintained for a long time. Such conditions are also found in other mountain streams that flow through cool gorges or forests. In these, despite widely varying water volumes and mostly steep gradients, moss settles on the stones. The moment these channels are exposed to open sunlight (through clear-cutting, etc.), the moss covering of the stones disappears; the stones become scoured. Bank erosion sets in: the channel immediately takes on the character of those whose temperatures constantly change.

The earlier the watercourse is exposed to free solar radiation (through clear-cutting, etc.), the more rapidly and over a shorter distance the temperature equalization takes place. The resulting deceleration of the water masses occurs abruptly in sharp braking curves, and premature deposition of the bedload being carried along takes place (loss of energy and velocity during the rapid transition from a positive to a negative temperature gradient). Very wide channel beds are formed, so that the water flowing through in normal times is increasingly exposed to temperature influences. The next consequence is excessive evaporation and overloading of the atmosphere with water vapor, which favors prolonged or suddenly catastrophic precipitation when low temperatures break in.


---


- 27 -
The rivers of Veneto emerge from the sharply and almost immediately descending high mountains onto the North Italian plain and thereby experience extraordinarily high and abrupt temperature fluctuations in their immediate surroundings during most of the year. As long as the river flows through the mountains, its water temperature and that of its surroundings remain uniformly low. Fluctuations occur only within narrow limits. The bed formation shows no particular deviations. This changes immediately upon the river's entry into the plain, which is warm for most of the year, occasionally hot, and exhibits strong, suddenly occurring temperature fluctuations. Day and night temperatures also show fluctuations of up to 10°. The flow profile takes on a very characteristic form: a very shallow bed with deeply incised thalweg (or, in cases of very great bed widths, two or more thalwegs) — a pronounced double profile! (Fig. 6) The thalweg of the torrente, as these river courses are called, is as a rule very deep. Since the bed gradient is not significant, the water velocity in the thalweg remains within normal limits. Because forestry in the Italian Alps, as a result of centuries of neglect, is at a very low level and entire stretches are karstified, during snowmelt large cold-water masses enter the hot plain without transition. The almost sudden reversal of the temperature gradient causes large gravel deposits through mechanical displacements of the floodwater masses in the riverbed, and where this is not sufficiently wide, considerable flooding occurs.

The rivers in the west of the North Italian plain present an entirely different appearance, although the terrain conditions are the same as in Veneto. These rivers do not exhibit torrente character, but flow in a regular profile with uniform velocity toward the Po. This regularity is brought about by the large water reservoirs of the North Italian lakes, which retain the water from snowmelt and release it into the plain at a temperature that is already more adapted to it, so that the formation of such extreme negative temperature gradients as in the case of the torrenti can no longer occur.

For the Alpine rivers flowing toward the north, conditions are similar to those described above, but not as extreme as with the torrenti, because the descent of the Alps toward the north is gentler and the temperature differences are not as great. Here, after leaving the mountains, the riverbeds show one-sided deepening with thrown-up gravel banks on the inner curve (also a double profile), likewise a consequence of the negative temperature gradient that establishes itself in the longitudinal and cross-section during most of the year (Fig. 7).


---


- 28 -
In the above, two extreme cases (Sweden and Northern Italy) were discussed. Between them there are naturally a whole series of intermediate stages, whose detailed discussion would go too far. It should only be briefly mentioned that rivers that flow into the sea with a positive temperature gradient (e.g., rivers flowing into the Arctic Ocean) carry their sediment far out into the sea (formation of spits, etc.), while rivers that flow into the sea with a negative temperature gradient deposit their sediment before the confluence (delta formation).

The former, when flowing in a west-east direction, migrate broadside toward the north through the constant enlargement of the heavy-water side and the shifting of the river axis toward the northern bank; the latter widen perpendicular to the river direction in proportion to the decrease in tractive force.

Through the formation of the heavy- and light-water sides described earlier, centrifugal effects arise due to the screw-like rearrangement of the water masses (see Section 2, alternation of heavy- and light-water sides in ford formation), which are strengthened or weakened by the Earth's rotation — depending on the direction of flow. East-west channels show a different character than west-east or north-south or south-north channels. In a west-east channel, the sediment transport will distribute itself uniformly over the entire cross-section. In south-north and north-south channels, sediment transport is mostly one-sided. West-east and east-west channels will generally be seeded on both banks (in the latter case possibly also bare on both banks), while south-north and north-south channels mostly show one-sided seeding with typically one-sided deepening of the riverbed.

4. The General Tasks of River Regulation

For the profile formation, the design of the longitudinal profile, and the horizontal course of a river, the following have been recognized as the primarily governing factors in accordance with the above exposition:

1. The terrain configuration
2. The temperature gradient
3. The geographical position
4. The Earth's rotation

The terrain configuration is given by nature. Certain improvements can be made through minor installations where these become unavoidable for the protection of cultural assets, but it would be wrong to attempt to regulate the river from the banks — that is, to combat only the effects, not the causes. In particular, bank corrections consisting of smooth, straight walls are often dangerous, because the velocity increase that occurs at the smooth wall creates the beginning of the circular movement described in Section 2 (see Fig. 3), which promotes bank erosion at a downstream location. Truly promising in carrying out river regulation is the regulation of the temperature gradient with only light assistance from the banks, since with this — namely the regulation of the temperature gradient — the geographical position can also be taken into account within certain limits.

- 29 -


---


In carrying out river regulation, the primary aim is the safe discharge of water masses, so that human life and cultural assets are reliably protected from the effects of flooding. The following aspects must be taken into account in every regulation:

a) The longitudinal profile and the horizontal course must be brought into harmony.
b) The river profile must be designed so that it is capable, depending on the locality, of carrying a certain maximum water volume without difficulty.
c) Provisions must be made to ensure that water from catastrophic precipitation in the catchment area does not immediately become surface runoff.
d) Efforts must be made to regulate sediment transport so that deposition or erosion occurs where it is desired.

Regarding a): As previously mentioned, a river will, over the course of time, establish that bed gradient which corresponds to the temperature gradient associated with the mean annual temperature as well as the mean annual water flow. The preservation, or rather the establishment, of this mean bed gradient can be achieved through the regulation of the temperature gradient adapted to the prevailing temperature conditions.

Furthermore, when comparing the longitudinal profile with the situation, care must be taken that the succession of curves is correct, and that, for example, a left-hand curve does not exist where nature demands a right-hand curve.

Regarding b): The channel profile must be adapted to local conditions and be capable of carrying both low water and flood water in a regulated manner. When it is said "adapted to local conditions," the following is meant: In those river stretches that are naturally


---


- 30 -
which are favored by nature and exhibit a natural, positive temperature gradient for most of the year, a simple, preferably trough-shaped profile will be appropriate. Where strong temperature fluctuations occur, however, a profile must be chosen which, through its shape, favors keeping the temperature in the flowing water as low as possible for as long as possible. A profile that possesses this property is the double profile, strictly adapted to the respective conditions (see Fig. 6 and Fig. 7). In this, a natural separation of heavy and light water takes place. Through this, the water flow becomes orderly, fluctuations of the stream line are reduced to a minimum, since this is moved away from the surface into the depths. The water flow in the curve, effected by depth rather than lateral weight distribution, is adapted to that of healthy channels and prevents a change of the temperature gradient within the cross-section, as described in Section 2. The heavy water flows in the lower part of the profile, adapted to the local conditions, the light water in the upper part. Between the faster-flowing heavy water and the slower-flowing light water, a roller running opposite to the direction of flow forms with a horizontal axis (sketch). This roller equalizes the sediment carried along to the right and left of the heavy water core. The light water flowing above the heavy water protects the latter from too strong direct heating and thereby causes the temperature gradient in the flowing water to remain positive for as long as possible (sketch). The braking of the leading cold water core occurs mechanically: with increasing velocity of the heavy water core, the water roller described earlier enlarges and reduces the cold water core, whereby its forward movement energy is inevitably reduced. With decreasing bed gradient, on the other hand, the velocity of forward movement decreases, and with it the size of the water roller and its braking effect.

The correct position of this roller is extremely important. The mechanical profiling of the cross-section depends on it. In healthy river courses, the axis lies horizontally, apart from minor fluctuations in the curves, while under abnormal conditions this axis is strongly inclined or even vertical, which gives rise to irregular profile formations. The occurrence and force of such rollers at points of contact between different velocities is described by Forchheimer ("Hydraulik", 1914, page 499): "When a shallow strip borders a deep bed, as is frequently the case with overbank flooding, the unequal velocities create eddies with vertical axes. These eddies can carve longitudinal trenches in the higher bed not far from the deep bed edge, which sometimes look almost like pipe trenches." Thus


---


- 31 -
during the flood of July 14, 1913, in the Leonhardbach in Graz, at a distance of about 30 cm from the deep bed edge, a longer trench of 0.3 to 1.5 m width and 0.2 to 1.5 m depth was formed by the action of a vertical water roller (Fig. 9).

If the execution of the double profile is not possible for special reasons, e.g., excessive costs, one can — through correctly functioning water reservoirs, which will be discussed in more detail in Section 5 — inevitably shape the water feed of the channel in such a way that the temperature gradient becomes positive or only slightly negative on the river stretches in question. In this case, the heavy water always flows in the middle of the channel and the sediment and suspended matter deposition occurs uniformly on both banks, as explained earlier in the case of the Tepl. The water works out the appropriate profile for itself in this case, and over time the correctly positioned double profile with the properties described above, which are favorable for water flow, will establish itself on its own — a process that naturally takes a longer time.

ad c) Regarding measures to prevent rapid surface runoff of heavy precipitation, the essential points have already been stated in the essay "Temperature and Water Movement," Part 1, so that a discussion of this topic here is unnecessary.

ad d) The dragging force and sediment transport of a river and their connection with the temperature gradient have already been discussed several times in the course of this treatise. Through correctly introduced inflows (so to speak, through the supply of lower-temperature energy water), the positive temperature gradient and thus the dragging force can be maintained, a purpose that can also be achieved by supplying low-temperature bed water from a dam. The intensity of the effect will depend on the ratio of the supply water to the water in the main channel that has lost its dragging force.

The temperature gradient at the confluence of a tributary into the main river must be correctly adjusted, otherwise unpleasant phenomena may occur in the main river, just as incorrect regulation in the tributary can lead to catastrophic devastation in the main channel.

In this connection, the following phenomenon, related to the dragging force of water and occurring in rivers with unhealthy conditions, should be noted. If, as discussed in the case of the Torrenten, the cold water masses of the river suddenly


---


- 32 -
flow into the warm valley plain, the following longitudinal profile results when the temperature gradient becomes negative. From A to B the temperature gradient is negative, with strong sediment deposition at B, the point of greatest loss of dragging force.

The water has reached its highest temperature here. Due to the damming caused by sediment deposition at B, there is an overfall with roller formation and a scour behind B, in which clearly measurable low temperatures form (heavy water pockets). As the light water flows over this colder heavy water, the light water is cooled from below, the temperature gradient becomes positive again for a short stretch up to C, and from here the process begins anew.

For a regulation to be carried out successfully, these changes in the temperature gradient must be extended over a longer stretch, whereby the sediment movement also becomes more uniform and the bed transformations occur in a gentle wave form.

View object; for river regulation

5. Regulation of the temperature gradient.

In the above explanations, the regulation of the correct temperature gradient has been discussed, and this process shall now be explained in more detail. There are two possibilities for such regulation:

a) Regulation of the temperature gradient through the construction of a reservoir;
b) Maintenance of the temperature gradient through correct profile design

ad a) Where the terrain conditions permit and no water law difficulties exist, it is expedient to proceed with the construction of a reservoir in the uppermost catchment area of the river. With sufficient water depth, the water stratifies in the reservoir according to its specific weight: the lower-temperature water will settle at the bottom, the higher-temperature water at the top. The dam wall; (see Patent No. 136214) can be designed at the extraction point so that water of the respectively required temperature can be withdrawn from the reservoir, whereby this tempered water is obtained through automatic mixing of the differently tempered water layers contained in the reservoir. This is made possible by a floating gate, which is automatically moved by a floating bell; exposed to solar radiation and external air temperature, and thus automatically releases a larger or smaller cross-section of the bottom outlet.

- 33 -
In this way, more or less bed water — as needed — can flow to mix with the surface water. The floating bell is set once and for all as deemed necessary based on the author's experience after examination of the climatic and other conditions, so that the water leaves the reservoir at a temperature corresponding to the prevailing air temperature.


---


Taking this factor into account, the temperature gradient in the channel will establish itself as positive on that stretch which is decisive for the overall regulation of the river course (normally the upper reaches), with only a gradual, unavoidable transition to the negative temperature gradient. This transition point and the development of this change can be achieved at the desired location, which is chosen so that mechanical influences there have no adverse effects. The reversal of the temperature gradient discussed in earlier sections does not take place over short stretches but over a desired, larger stretch, and the sediment is no longer deposited suddenly but distributed evenly over this larger stretch.

Through the uniform leveling of the temperature gradient thus achieved, the previously discussed abrupt displacements no longer occur, but rather gentle changes in the river bed, and conditions are created that closely approach the annual average with regard to temperature gradient and water flow.

ad b) Where reservoirs cannot be constructed for any reason, one must strive — through correct choice of the river profile — to maintain the low temperature of the water, which is decisive for the positive temperature gradient, for as long as possible. Such a profile has already been described in Section 4b. The details have already been stated there, and it may be added here by way of supplement that the horizontal development of the river course (the sequence of bends) must be most carefully observed. Here, the lower part of the double profile in the bend must be correctly adjusted in terms of lateral and depth position in order to maintain the river axis (stream line) and the axis position of the roller described earlier in the respectively correct position. If the lower, decisive part of the profile is correctly laid out, it also maintains itself in its shape and position in loose gravel, as the troughs of the Torrenten prove.

Concluding word.

With the above explanations, an attempt has been made to present the difficult problem of

- 34 -
river engineering and river regulation, including the decisive factors and the previously unheeded temperature gradient, in concise brevity. Detailed explanations could naturally not be entered into here, since rigid forms cannot be established; rather, the details must adapt to the special and always varying conditions in each individual case.

The recognition that mathematical developments alone are not sufficient for carrying out hydraulic engineering work has been expressed by the hydraulic engineer Robert Weyrauch in apt words, namely that for carrying out hydraulic measures "a special hydraulic aptitude, a pronounced feeling for what is hydraulically possible or impossible" is necessary. "That is difficult to acquire; even the most experienced person experiences disappointments again and again."

- 35 -
TEMP. AND WATER MOVEMENT 1 (t4)

Temperature and Water Movement


---


Excerpt from the treatise on the laws of water movement, deposited at the Academy of Sciences in Vienna

Special reprint from: "Die Wasserwirtschaft" (The Water Economy), 1930, Issue 20.

To the editorial office of Die Wasserwirtschaft, Vienna.

Mr. Viktor Schauberger has transmitted to me the enclosed treatise on Temperature and Water Movement. As it has aroused my full interest through the completely new viewpoints contained in it, which appear suitable not only to have a fruitful but even pioneering effect on river and dam construction, I consider it in the general interest to make this work known to a wider readership and the professional world. In this sense, I recommend that you publish this interesting article.

With high regard,

Forchheimer

Contents:

The significance of the temperature gradient for water movement;
Setting of the groundwater;
Flow of water on the Earth;
Some aspects of river regulation;
Significance of groundwater for agriculture;

- 36 -
The catastrophic floods, which have occurred with increasing frequency in recent years on the one hand, and the growing water scarcity becoming more apparent in many regions on the other, give rise to the question of whether the manner of water management practiced hitherto, as well as other measures taken by human hand, may not partly be to blame for these evils.

There are primarily two aspects to be examined from this viewpoint: today's practice of river regulation and the increasing deforestation of forests.

Before addressing the matter itself, an important circumstance should be emphasized by way of introduction, which has been neglected in all hydraulic engineering measures up to now: the temperature of the water in relation to the Earth and air temperature, as well as the temperature differences within the flowing or standing water itself (temperature gradient).

If even small temperature differences are sufficient to bring about the obvious aggregate changes of water (liquid, gaseous, and solid), then it is easily understood that larger temperature differences existing within flowing or standing water must decisively influence the movement of water on and in the Earth.

In the following, these previously overlooked connections between temperature and water movement will be discussed, and those errors will be pointed out which have been made hitherto through neglect of this important connection.

I. Temperature gradient — Full and half cycle

The movement and distribution of the water returning from the atmosphere to the surface is influenced by the inherent temperature of the water and that of the surrounding Earth and air layers.


---


If the temperature of the water falling on the Earth is higher than that of the Earth layers which are to absorb the water, the water — becoming specifically heavier as a result of its cooling — will be able to penetrate into the interior of the Earth without difficulty. As it sinks further, after reaching the temperature of 4°C, which is simultaneously its greatest density,

- 37 -
it finally reaches higher-temperature Earth layers again and becomes specifically lighter through temperature adaptation.

The further it sinks due to the pressure of the heavy water above it, the greater its own resistance to the downward movement becomes, due to its continually decreasing specific weight. Eventually, an equilibrium state emerges, and thereby the setting of the extremely important elevation of the groundwater table. Under very specific pressure conditions, a water layer of 4°C temperature forms in the groundwater; this shall be referred to below as the middle layer.

In the case described above, we are dealing with a positive temperature gradient, if by temperature gradient we understand the arithmetically taken difference per unit length between the temperature of the incident water and the Earth temperature.

The above case also represents a full cycle of the water, which, in repetition of what was said above, is characterized by the following phases:
Penetration of the water into the Earth, passage through the groundwater middle layer of 4°C, purification at this temperature, further sinking under the influence of its own weight in underground river courses, transition to the vaporous state under the influence of the hot interior of the Earth, re-ascent to the Earth's surface with simultaneous nutrient absorption, cooling and deposition of nutrients, flow on the Earth's surface, evaporation, cloud formation, falling again as rain, etc.

In warm soils, the groundwater layer is absent, and with it the counterweight for the pressure coming from below. If the temperature of the incident water is lower than that of the uppermost Earth layer, the water initially sinks to a certain depth, warms up, and becomes specifically lighter. Finally, it is driven back to the surface by the pressure from below and flows away — if it does not immediately evaporate again — down the bed gradient.

In this case, we are dealing with a negative temperature gradient (water temperature lower than Earth surface temperature). A full cycle no longer forms, but only a half cycle; that is: the water falling on the Earth, surface runoff, evaporation, cloud formation, falling again as rain.


---


- 38 -
For further clarification regarding the temperature gradient, and for better understanding of what follows, the following should be noted:

With an initially negative temperature gradient (warm Earth, cold rain), a simultaneous drop in atmospheric temperature can cool the Earth to such an extent that the temperature gradient returns to 0. The same case can occur with an initially positive temperature gradient when the water masses penetrating the soil are so large that they are able to warm the soil. In both cases, upon reaching a temperature gradient of 0, the runoff follows the bed gradient until temperature gradients are re-established through friction phenomena, etc. In both cases, it is necessary to establish the required temperature gradient through correctly tempered supply water, in order to brake the water masses flowing freely and almost without resistance down the bed gradient.

II. Groundwater table

Depending on the temperature of the Earth layers, which in turn is co-influenced by the penetrating water masses, the groundwater table height is a variable one. The air temperature also plays a major role here.

Locally dammed and thereby low-temperatured water masses at the bed influence the temperature of the surrounding Earth layers. These are cooled and thereby a constant, positive temperature gradient is produced, since the falling rain masses will always be warmer than the colder Earth.
Thus the precondition for the penetration of rainwater is given.

Not only will a rise in the groundwater table occur, but also the absorption capacity of the soil will increase downward and laterally.

The groundwater middle layer of 4°C mentioned earlier is depressed downward due to the increase in the pressure of the water masses resting on it, and thereby the resistance offered by the warmer and specifically lighter water lying below the middle layer to further penetration is overcome.

This leads to the formation of a natural underground reservoir — a retention basin — which prevents rapid surface runoff of the water and causes a full cycle of the same. The release of water from this reservoir takes place as described later


---


- 39 -
but can also occur through lateral expansion of the middle layer due to the pressures acting on it from above and below (spring formation). The accompanying sketch shows schematically a cross-section through a groundwater reservoir. From it, one can see how the middle layer (which is under the greatest pressure of all layers) can flow laterally or also upward.

In contrast, as described in Section I, high Earth surface temperatures allow almost no water to penetrate. Groundwater formation does not occur or only to a small extent and at great depths. The soil becomes less and less absorbent due to the evaporation of whatever small quantities of groundwater may be present. The penetrating water is led to surface runoff and, in connection with this, to rapid re-evaporation by the effect of the excessively high soil temperature (half cycle). Thus, no groundwater formation in the sense described earlier takes place. (In such regions, warm springs frequently occur, which are pressed to the surface through fissures in the Earth by the pressure acting from below, against which no counterpressure from groundwater exists.)

The absorption capacity of the soil is thus dependent on the temperature conditions which, as described above, cause the setting of the groundwater table, and thus on the existence and height of the groundwater table (sketch).

In summary, it can be said:

Positive temperature gradient is the precondition for the absorption capacity of the soil and for the formation of groundwater, and in connection with this: formation of full cycles.

Negative temperature gradient does not allow groundwater formation. It causes half cycles (sketch).

III. Flow of water

A flow of water below the Earth's surface (groundwater flow) occurs, as already stated earlier, under the pressure acting from above and below and through temperature differences within the Earth and water layers surrounding the groundwater. What has hitherto been understood as groundwater flow alone was only the draining away of those groundwaters that sit on a water-impermeable layer and move in its gradient until they could rejoin the full cycle.

- 40 -
Water reaches the surface flow in two cases:

1. with a negative temperature gradient as written in Section I, immediately (sketch);
2. with a positive temperature gradient, when the groundwater table — after saturation has occurred (sketch) — under the influence of solar radiation, rises above the Earth's surface.


---


This also explains a phenomenon often observed in the mountains, namely: days of rain do not cause any particular rise in the water flow in the relevant receiving waters. The rainwater is almost entirely absorbed by the Earth. Only after the onset of warm weather does flood flow occur in the receiving waters. The cold groundwater rises to the Earth's surface layer, which has now been warmed by the influence of the sun and the warm air. The previously existing positive temperature gradient transforms into a negative one. The water flows away. One commonly says: The mountain lets out.

The preconditions for the occurrence of floods are far greater in case 1, all the more so because, due to the initially negative Earth temperature gradient, a positive temperature gradient now forms during the direct runoff of the water masses, whereby the water gradually tends to penetrate the Earth, loosens and carries away sediment. In addition to the bed gradient, there is also a surface gradient. A further increase in flow velocity and dragging force for sediment occurs. In the later course, and after another reversal of the now positive temperature gradient to a negative one, curve formation occurs through turbulence and thereby a mechanical deceleration of movement. The carried sediment is deposited and the following water masses are dammed. The consequence of this: floods.
