If, in case 2, saturation of the groundwater basin occurs due to a continuing positive temperature gradient, the groundwater (spring water) now reaching the surface is colder than the Earth layer immediately below it. The temperature gradient has reversed and become negative. A rapid runoff of the cold heavy water masses occurs. The heavy excess water of the Earth that reaches the runoff only gradually enters a positive ratio due to the relatively low Earth temperature, since heavy water warms very


---


- 41 -
slowly. Due to a mostly excessive bed gradient in the upper third of the catchment area, turbulence occurs, and thus curves in the horizontal course. The further transition from the negative to the positive temperature gradient therefore takes place slowly and with the help of strong turbulence, which in turn leads to overly sharp horizontal curves and thereby to sediment deposition on the one hand, scouring on the other, and displacements of the channel bed through mechanical effects. The immediate consequence of this water flow is widening of the channel, throwing up of broad gravel banks, evaporation or sinking of the water thus exposed again to external temperature influence in the churned-up channel bed (the now typical water flow of the Alps, with always a one-sidedly deepened bed and a gravel bank on the opposite side). The water flow occurs in the same way as in the first case, erratically. Erosion of the banks is the consequence. In the case of flood, mechanical braking occurs through the curves thus formed, the banks are eroded even more, an even worse situation than before. The consequences are again: floods.

In order to dam the flood danger at its roots, it is necessary to artificially eliminate the two extreme cases 1 and 2.

Through the creation of dam facilities with appropriately installed provisions, the runoff conditions — where they have been incorrectly adjusted due to shifts in the Earth layer temperatures and thereby the Earth layer temperature gradient — can be regulated again in the above sense. Through these regulating dams, the runoff conditions can be automatically adjusted according to the respective daily temperature, so that primarily the two extreme cases described above are reliably eliminated and appropriate intermediate temperatures are automatically established in the runoff conditions. With ever finer adjustment of the simple apparatuses provided in these dams (meaning the air-controlled diving bell from Patent No. 136214), the temperature gradients adapted to the annual mean temperature gradually emerge, and thus it is possible to influence and gradually prevent the flood danger at its origin.

The consequence is: no flood danger, because by adapting the runoff water masses to the annual average temperature, the correct Earth temperature gradients are established, thereby the absorption capacity of the soil, the correct setting of the groundwater table, and thus the formation of the extremely important retention basins is enabled. Through the correctly adjusted runoff conditions, a uniformly regulated continuation of the water on the Earth's surface results, and no locally limited evaporation, thereby no locally

- 42 -
limited and rapidly successive precipitation — in a word, the re-regulation of orderly full cycle conditions is achieved.


---

Where drainage of areas is concerned, it is likewise possible, by bringing about the favorable temperature gradient for this case (positive temperature gradient), to make the unwanted stagnant water disappear.

It is therefore possible, at will, to produce a full circuit or half circuit, whereas the dam structures built up to now have one-sidedly produced only half circuits.

In this connection, the significance of the full and half circuit is once again pointed out:

Full circuit means entry of water into the Earth's interior, creation of the necessary groundwater masses, delay of water runoff and thereby prevention or reduction of flood danger. There is also continuous formation of cold springs, whose water masses, after union with the receiving waters, produce new low temperatures in them and help prevent too rapid evaporation along the way.

With half circuit, on the other hand, the known condition of the repeatedly forming rising water vapor occurs, thus a constant addition to the atmospheric water masses, and in connection with this, frequent precipitation. One flood thus produces another.

IV. Fundamental Principles of River Regulation

The creation of proper drainage conditions is not only of great importance in the sense indicated above, but also for the regulation of river courses and their bank formation, which will now be briefly discussed.

The goal of today's practice in carrying out river regulations is, as already mentioned earlier, to effect as rapid a removal of the water as possible, which one seeks to achieve overall through bank corrections, securing of banks through artificial structures, etc.

This type of regulation is however entirely one-sided and does not fulfill its purpose.

It cannot and must not be the engineer's task to correct Nature.

- 43 -

Rather, his task is to investigate the natural processes as far as possible and to imitate the examples that Nature offers in healthy watercourses in those watercourses that require regulation.

Here too it again shows that the relationship between water and air temperature is the most important factor that must not be neglected in any regulation.


---


The natural regulators for water flow are forests and lakes. The forests, by keeping the ground cool within their domain, produce a constant, positive temperature gradient and, as a consequence, groundwater reservoirs that act in a retarding manner on the drainage of precipitation water. The cold springs issuing from these groundwater reservoirs hurry toward the receiving stream, cool its water masses, and thus prevent premature evaporation along the way.

The cycle movement of water — the transition from the Earth to the atmosphere — is thereby likewise decelerated and extended spatially. The cycles do not play out over relatively small territories, so that one precipitation event does not produce the next, and one flood does not produce the next.

The same function is fulfilled by natural lakes.

Where forests have been cleared and natural lakes are absent, a substitute must now be created — and this, as already stated in Section III, is the artificial reservoir, which must, however, be correctly constructed and correctly operated. Only then can it bring about the cited functions of groundwater formation, runoff deceleration, and production of the correct temperature gradient.

Reservoirs have indeed often been built to establish an orderly water management, but they have not always proved effective — indeed, they have often brought about the very opposite of what was intended.

This shall now be briefly explained: The impoundment facilities as they are built and operated today are nothing more than pure water storage. They collect water, also fulfill the function of retaining precipitation water to a certain degree, but almost always produce a half cycle. The water reaches the surface or soon after its outflow undergoes rapid evaporation.

The precipitation in the area of present-day reservoirs becomes irregular; it increases or decreases depending on the compass direction of the valley (wind direction), the normal water flow in the middle and lower reaches of the rivers declines, and likewise

- 44 -

the groundwater table in the middle and lower reaches sinks, the soil yield in these areas decreases conspicuously. This is solely and entirely due to the fact that the construction of such reservoirs creates a thoroughly one-sided temperature gradient in the river course downstream, through the constant release of either heavy or light water — depending on whether the water is discharged through the bottom outlet or over the spillway. Both types of discharge lead to the extreme cases described earlier, and thus to the formation of half cycles with their known adverse consequences for the origin and impact of floods.

It is now the task of a correctly constructed reservoir and an outflow regulation equipped with the necessary provisions to regulate the temperature gradient in the river course from the dam in such a way that the phenomena described above are reliably avoided.


---


With this type of regulation — namely through regulation of the temperature gradient — costly installations in the riverbed itself that do not serve their purpose become unnecessary.

When we have spoken so far about properly designed reservoirs, this refers to the fact that the slight water movement present in every reservoir is intensified through the formation of a strong temperature gradient, and thus cool water layers automatically reach the surface again and again through the installations to be provided, whereby the excessive evaporation and its detrimental consequences that have previously occurred on the reservoirs are greatly reduced.

As is evident from the above explanations, flood hazards can practically only be contained once the water masses, through a lawful, natural, and correct treatment, are no longer channeled into the atmosphere as quickly as possible as has been the case, but are instead directed to their actual purpose, namely through the detour through the earth into the full cycle and thus the nutrient supply of the soil. One can see that two cardinal errors have been made so far: by rapidly draining the water on the earth's surface, the water masses were returned to the atmosphere too quickly, thereby causing renewed precipitation and floods, and furthermore the water was diverted from its most important purpose, namely infiltration into the earth, and the nutrient supply of the soil was blocked by preventing the full cycle.

River regulation that, without regard for the temperature gradients, concerns itself exclusively with the removal of water masses through the less important bed gradient, ultimately leads to a disturbance in the correct sequence of temperature gradients, or rather to the formation of a one-sided temperature gradient and thereby to the catastrophes and floods that, for example, must now occur increasingly in France and will also propagate southward, as long as the evil is not grasped at its root and the effects are not remedied by transforming the described governing causes.

The movement of water in the riverbed itself and the possibilities of influencing it will be discussed in a later essay.

V. The Importance of Groundwater for Agriculture

When in the preceding section attention was drawn to the errors made so far in carrying out hydraulic engineering tasks and a way to avoid them was indicated, the following shall particularly point out the catastrophic consequences that incorrect water management brings about.

Through incorrect treatment of waters, not only does an immediate and acute threat to riparian residents arise, but what is much worse, a creeping evil that manifests in the reduction of the soil's productivity, namely: decline of the groundwater or the other extreme: waterlogging.


---


If we merely consider the changes in the area under cultivation for quality crops that have taken place over the period of a human generation, and realize that in places where our grandfathers still had rich farmland, today scarcely any grass grows, it becomes clear to us at what rate the productivity of the soil is declining. (For example, the area under cultivation for wheat and rye has fallen from 273 million hectares to 246 million hectares in the last three decades.) This decline in yield is particularly stark in mountainous regions, which naturally are the first to feel the effects of declining groundwater. On alpine pastures where it once made no difference if hundreds more head of cattle were driven up, the grazing rights holders today quarrel over the fodder needed for a single animal. The once almost inexhaustible pastures today do not even suffice for a fraction of the former stocking rate.

This decline in soil yield has its cause solely and exclusively in the described fact that the groundwater table has fallen and continues to fall steadily. The soil that is to produce a good yield must constantly be supplied with the substances that plants need for their development. The mediator and carrier of these substances, however, is the groundwater, which in its internal cycle constantly brings fresh nutrient salts up from the interior of the earth. If the groundwater recedes, the supply of nutrients by natural means ceases. Artificial fertilization and doubled diligence can only provide partial temporary replacement for the natural supply of substances. The precipitation coming from the atmosphere merely represents moistening. Nutrients for plants are not contained in it.

Nature itself is not responsible for the steadily increasing desiccation of the earth's surface, which is a consequence of the lowering of the groundwater table. Rather, since time immemorial, everything has been done by human hand — even if unconsciously — to steadily lower the water and with it the natural nutrients.

The significance of the temperature gradient for the lawful movement of water was previously unknown. Water was therefore mostly treated incorrectly. For example, in harnessing the energy contained in water to generate electricity, humans have made arbitrary installations in riverbeds and so forth, which have often had water-destroying effects. Attempts have been made to correct rivers from the banks, naturally with negative results. No one has ever thought of restoring the balance that was disturbed through installations in the river itself, through deforestation, and so on, through other measures.


---


The path indicated here — namely to artificially restore the temperature gradients that would otherwise establish themselves in nature under normal circumstances, wherever necessary — is the only correct solution to achieve a natural water drainage, or rather water retention in the soil. Only by following this path, or rather by utilizing these insights, which are the result of many years of observation by the author, can a further decline of the groundwater and thereby a further decline of soil yield be prevented. Only in this way will it also be possible to contain water catastrophes and to make water what it always was and must always be: a giver of life.

TEMP. AND WATER MOVEMENT 4, Atmospheric Cycle (t7)

Temperature and Water Movement

- 47 -

By Viktor Schauberger, Vienna

The lawful movement of water on the earth's surface
(Atmospheric Cycle) and its relationship to river regulation.

from "Die Wasserwirtschaft" Year 1931, Issue 10, 2nd Part

Contents:

The lawful movement of water on the earth's surface
Trout — food intake;
Trout — principle of movement;
Aviation;
Timber floating;

- 48 -

The lawful movement of water on the earth's surface

Under the protection of the forest, the watercourse can maintain its moderately even temperature. The external temperature exerts only indirectly as much influence as is necessary for the successive decrease of the drag force through weak vortex phenomena. Healthy forest, or cold tributaries that accompany the water along its course, provide the guarantee for a slowly progressing heat absorption and thus for an even release of nutrients in favor of the surrounding environment with its animal and plant life. (The Amazon River of Brazil and the rivers of Java.)

Without healthy forest, no healthy water, no healthy blood, thus a deterioration of the most fundamental conditions of life through the methods of today's customary forestry and water management.

The longer the water is withdrawn from the influence of the sun (through the protection of the forest, through properly designed lakes and tributaries), the longer it retains its energy and above all its drag force, and the more even the release of its substances will be, its general drainage pattern and direction.

Properly treated water — i.e., water of the correct temperature adapted to the respective climatic conditions — can, as will be shown later, not attack the banks.

Incorrectly treated water, however, which mostly exhibits excessively high temperatures, has the ability to tear at the banks and for this reason assumes the characteristic properties of a wild mountain torrent.


---


It must already be emphasized at this point with special emphasis that all efforts to maintain watercourses in equilibrium through correction structures from the bank are futile. The best testimony to this fact is also all the regulation works carried out on this principle, which, as is well known, repeatedly cause new damage and further costs despite constantly necessary repair work.

A special significance naturally also attaches to the distribution of the water masses according to their various states of aggregation. Premature evaporation of the water masses (warm soils) during their flow on the earth's surface results in one-sided accumulations of water vapor in the atmosphere.

- 49 -

This causes too rapid a shift of the temperature gradients interacting with each other in the atmosphere, which in turn must lead to heavy precipitation and cyclones.

Interactions of the earth's temperature create unfavorable temperature gradients in the flowing water masses, whereby the full cycle of water is again adversely affected. In such cases, instead of entering the earth, the water passes too quickly into the atmosphere through the ever-widening channels due to excessively high temperatures. This not only results in the prevention of important nutrient acquisition and its supply, but must also inevitably cause ever larger and more dangerous flood catastrophes.

Applying the above concepts of the temperature gradient, we have before us a case where, due to excessively strong external temperature influence, a transverse positioning and thereby widening of the channel occurs through too rapid departure from +4°C, and in such a case one can therefore speak of an excessive negative temperature gradient, which must lead to the loss of the equilibrium state in the flowing water and to displacements.

The flowing water increasingly approaches the second (turbulent) energy form, is driven out of its internal equilibrium by ever more strongly occurring counter-movements, and finally begins to scour in order to brake itself. The channels become wider, shallower, the external temperature influence more intensive. Ever larger water masses already return to the atmosphere along the way. The apparent shrinking of the water, the ever-increasing dry channels, and the ever-increasing storm catastrophes are the lawfully correctly occurring consequences of previous river regulations.

From the following observation results it will become clear that today's treatment of water must gradually lead to the complete destruction of all vegetation and thus of all cultures, and that the economic decline now playing out before our eyes is only a first stage on this path, which no customs barrier, but only a return to nature, may perhaps still be able to halt.


---


A frightening example of the consequences of such water treatment is the deserts, which were once in part sites of high civilizations. Excavations give us news that attempts were made to retain the receding water through large-scale, but unfortunately also incorrect, water installations of every kind. We need only cast a glance at the map of the Gobi Desert, whose rivers dry up at the edge of the ever-widening wasteland. Careless treatment of vegetation and excessive external temperature influence of these latitudes created today's image of the desert.

After fully grasping the causes of the current phenomena of destruction and their progression, it can be claimed with complete justification that through a reversal of this gradual decline — that is, through a reconfiguration of the energy forms in question — proceeding just as gradually from the very beginnings, literally making cultural sites out of deserts again and creating new possibilities for life is possible.

The concept of temperature has not been evaluated at all until now.

The water flowing downhill is subject to two influences: the direct influence of solar radiation and the indirect influence of the earth in the wetted area. These two influences, through changes in energy forms and thereby changes in the gradient, produce the labile equilibrium state in the flowing water.

It should now be readily clear that the influence of the sun must be greater than the influence of the earth. This overriding influence will make itself felt primarily at the upper edge of the channel body. At this point, the water will exceed the so-called critical velocity due to the influence from outside and become more strongly turbulent. If the external influence is, for example, indirect due to the protection of the forest, then the influence occurs more or less evenly, provided the same bank formations on both sides.

If the external influence acts directly (direct solar radiation), it produces an irregularity (day and night alternation). The logical consequence of these temperature phenomena is the curve in the stronger or weaker sense. (For more detail, see "Die Wasserwirtschaft," Issue 24 of 1930.)

The resulting deposits and erosions as well as other displacement phenomena are to be regarded as material deposition sites of the existing equilibrium disturbances.

Once again, it must be pointed out with special emphasis that deposits in the interior of the earth (under exclusion of air) occur as the temperature approaches +4°C, while on the

- 51 -

earth's surface (under the influence of air) these deposits occur as the temperature departs from +4°C.


---


In addition to this heavily weighing external influence, the influence of frictional heat (mechanical action) must also be taken into account. The cold, healthy mountain water flowing rapidly over the bed and along the walls (edges) of the bank generates, at the aforementioned contact surfaces, due to local exceeding of the critical velocity, light vortices through frictional heat and thereby counter-currents.

The precondition for this counter-current is a difference in the influence of temperature from outside and from the earth. The lower the temperature of the core water masses, the greater the relative movement velocity. This in turn triggers, in the same proportion, the reaction in the form of counter-currents through mechanical and physical impulses at the bank and bed. If temperature, mass, and bed gradient were in a correct ratio, the labile equilibrium state of the water masses would be achieved.

In practice, however, this state is impossible. If the bed gradient is too small for the respective temperatures of the water, erosion occurs; in the opposite case, deposition occurs.

The bed gradient thus adjusts itself according to the temperature gradient, whose constancy in turn is not disturbed as long as the protective forest is maintained in the right extent and in its correct composition (tributaries).

The preservation of watercourses is therefore exclusively dependent on the correct maintenance of the forest, and today's necessity for watercourse regulation is already a consequence of incorrect forest management.

If the objection should be raised here that the forest had to be pushed back to gain cultivable land, the answer is that this can certainly be done up to a certain ratio without further ado, provided that instead of the forest, appropriate substitutes (properly designed reservoirs and river profiles) are created that, in the absence of the forest, are capable of permanently maintaining the labile equilibrium state that is important here.

Under these conditions, the observation picture described above will also result. If the aquatic plants lean upstream or downstream, this is nothing other than the

- 52 -

effect of slight fluctuations and shifts in the temperature gradient. The calm moss tip standing perpendicular to the direction of flow thus indicates, like the balance pointer standing at the zero point, the presence of correct equilibrium conditions.


---


The vortex formation (counter-current) described above persists in the correct measure as long as correct temperature conditions prevail in the main water mass. If, after removal of the forest, the direct influence of the sun takes effect, strong vortex formations (turbulences) arise at the upper lateral contact surfaces. The middle core water masses rush ahead and exceed the critical velocity. the fluttering up of turbulence in these core water masses at the point of greatest velocity is the consequential phenomenon whose cause of origin has not been clarified until now. It is, so to speak, the emergency brake for the water masses departing too rapidly downhill, and the hitherto scientifically unexplained preservation of the continuity of the water flowing down the inclined plane (gradient).

With a jolt, the reaction sets in in the form of a sharp counter-movement. The entire water mass is flung around the braking curve either to the right or to the left. The scour point arises as the effect of an excessive and too abrupt deceleration of the through-flowing water, which now leads to the well-known destruction of banks and bed.

Through today's protective measures, these phenomena are not improved or eliminated, but rather worsened many times over. At this point it should be noted that similar processes also give rise, for example, to the avalanche, in combating whose damage — as with water — one always sees only the mechanical effects and, without taking the physical causes into account, seeks to remedy only the mechanical effects.

The corresponding purpose of this vortex formation, which proceeds by mechanical and physical means, is the possibility of aeration of the water masses, the temperature equalization and change of kinetic energy, whereby the bank and bed are also reshaped as a direct consequence.

The bed gradient is thus a secondary phenomenon of the temperature gradient.

- 53 -

The transverse positioning of the water masses, the widening of the watercourses, the thereby ever more pronounced external influence, the eventual evaporation of the water along the way (drying up of the rivers), the emergence of deserts on the one hand and excessive water catastrophes on the other hand are the known results of treating water according to current principles. This end result, however, is the entirely correct lawful consequence of the current way of treating water. Thus, precisely in proportion to the financial resources sacrificed for today's river regulation according to customary methods, the catastrophes and devastations must inevitably increase in strength and number in the same ratio.

Movement of the trout in flowing water with regard to food intake


---


In the foregoing, the influences to which the medium of water is exposed have been described in broad outlines. In the following, we shall proceed further and show how dependent the creatures living in water are, and how every error made by humans takes its revenge on them.

In healthy water, we find the healthy, well-known and palatable mountain trout just below the spring. This resident trout, living a comfortable and peaceable life in healthy water conditions, when viewed under the magnifying glass of the attentive observer, very much resembles the gentle oscillating movement of the moss tips described earlier.

For those who know how to observe, a wealth of highly interesting perceptions now presents itself. One learns to understand the purposefulness of even the smallest movement of the trout, and begins to realize that the human intellect, in theory and practice, diligently tends to always take the wrong path, although nature unceasingly demonstrates the correct path in countless repetitions and nuances.

With the exception of the breeding season, food intake is the sole reason for every movement. Slight positional changes in height and depth counterbalance the changes in the food supply caused by external influences. If the trout is startled, it flees at lightning speed upstream, only to return to its old position after a certain time.

Through simple means it can be determined that the trout as a rule

- 54 -

stands in the axis of the core water layers, at the point where the water particles that are closest to the temperature of +4°C, and thus least turbulent, are moving. The water particles moving in this axis have, due to the most ordered forward movement, the relatively greatest velocity, and in this energy line, the actual river axis, all foreign bodies that are heavier than water — and thus also the food for the trout — travel.


---


As long as healthy conditions exist — that is, as long as the core water masses are in the correct ratio to the bed gradient and the curves — stronger vortex phenomena will occur at the upper edge of the bank, becoming ever weaker toward the bottom. Under such conditions, the river axis is also subject to virtually no changes.

The vortices running counter at the edge create small scours that, in proportion to the necessary widening and shallowing of the channel, loosen soil components from both banks and thus also the food (the worms that prefer to dwell at the moist edge) for the fish. The external influence, which becomes ever stronger through this widening, ensures an ever-diminishing drag force of the water, and thus the consistent deposition of the salts — which also become ever inferior — is guaranteed. Toward evening, the temperature gradient again approaches the positive energy form, and during the nighttime the removal of the sediment takes place. At the same time, higher-quality water also arrives in the lower reaches at night (less deposition).

At the appropriate temperature, the watercourse regulates itself completely. Noticeable sediment transport, displacements, or bank erosion are unknown phenomena in healthy waters.

In correctly tempered watercourses, the water flow will not only widen in the correct proportion but also deepen in the lower reaches to such an extent, thus automatically forming the longitudinal profiles adapted to the changing cross-sectional variations that are necessary for the removal of the sediment masses — which are in themselves small under regulated forest conditions.

If flooding occurs in such watercourses untouched by humans, then due to the usually low external temperatures at this time, the influence from below becomes stronger with the increasing water mass. As at night, the temperature gradient approaches the positive energy form, and the water masses flow at greater velocity — because of lower temperatures — without exceeding or attacking the banks. The temperature gradient acts primarily and the bed gradient becomes secondary when, through correct temperature adjustment, the

- 55 -

mass removal is regulated in the correct proportion through a corresponding increase in velocity.

If the river course did not shallow, no decrease in drag force could occur either. The release of nutrients could also not take place due to the consistently low temperatures in deep waters.

In correctly treated — or better said, untreated water conditions under correct forest conditions — with increasing water quantities in the lower reaches, a correct profiling will form, and with the mass increase the corresponding influence from below will develop, whereby the overriding influence from above is weakened, thus making it almost impossible for sediment to remain lodged or for flooding to occur in the lower reaches.


---


The larger the water masses, the greater in this case, due to the correct adjustment of the temperature gradient, the flow velocity will be under otherwise equal conditions (gradient). With subsequent correct profiling, the temperature gradient automatically releases the energies needed to transport even the largest water masses. The energy charges necessary for the removal of sediment in the lower reaches are provided by the laterally adjoining, lower-temperature tributaries to the main waters.

Through the current methods of river regulation, the external influence must make itself ever more directly and thereby ever more strongly felt. The edge vortices become stronger, the core water masses rush ahead more violently, and the exceeding of the critical velocity by the core water masses is carried out to an ever greater extent through constant repetition. The river thalweg appears ever less pronounced through the scour formations, the watercourse becomes ever wider, and the sediment deposits stronger. The trout, too, must increasingly fetch its food, which no longer flows so centrally, through more frequent repositioning.

Slowly, just as with humans, here too — caused by their lack of reason — the hard struggle for daily bread begins, and the struggle for existence that becomes pointless in this further development. This also brings about the rule of the strongest even in the otherwise peaceable trout. Individual predatory natures are indeed enabled to exist, but the duration of their existence is already very limited at this stage, because the river course and the water are already sick and drying up.

If one observes the microscopic small life that arises in the water just below the spring under the influence of

- 56 -

light and sun (warmth), then it also becomes clear why fish seek out certain spots in the spring area of a river during spawning season.

It becomes ever more difficult for the parent fish to find the suitable spot for its offspring, where the young, clumsy fry in their food intake can be offered the possibility of beginning life. The reasons for the decline of our former abundance of fish are in many cases also attributable to the healthy water that has become rare even in the upper reaches, which, instead of being laden with nutrients from noble springs, comes empty from the earth through seepage springs. This water, already unhealthy at the spring, cannot produce healthy flow conditions downstream either. Instead of an always briefly preceding rush of the core water masses and a converging direction of the water particles, the entire middle water becomes excessively turbulent, the energies direct themselves against the banks due to the displacement phenomena, whereby bank erosion and island formations arise.


---


At this point, a great error should be pointed out. This rushing ahead of the water masses has been regarded as a continuous acceleration of the water. And yet this supposed acceleration is in reality the precondition for its internal braking through the occurrence of intensified turbulence at the point of greatest water velocity. Thus every mathematical calculation of this process made so far must undoubtedly lead to precisely the opposite result (reaction effect).

Eventually, not only does the curve form in the horizontal direction, but the water even begins to roll up. The water strongly warmed at the surface becomes strongly turbulent and lags behind the deeper, colder layers. The less warmed lower layers rush ahead, change their consistency, and fall behind again. At a certain velocity, this time by mechanical means, water rollers running upstream arise, which throw the food floating on the water upward and toward the spring. Strong evaporation suffocates the insects dancing above the steaming water surface, which, fallen into the water, form the food for the trout and swallows.

As long as healthy water conditions prevail, the trout has abundance and choice. In the cold, clear water, the resident trout leisurely examines each morsel of food that swims by and, with the discerning eye of a connoisseur, avoids the hook. At most, it is the small trout standing outside the river thalweg that bite, as they hungrily try to seize

- 57 -

every morsel of food that strays from the lawful path.

When sultry weather sets in and temperatures shoot sharply upward (negative temperature gradient), the entire core water becomes turbulent. Restless from hunger, the resident trout dashes about, jumps at the midges dancing above the surface, and, made careless by hunger, easily falls prey to the angler, who well knows that in such weather the large trout bite, but until now had no idea why.

The same naturally also applies after a rain, when the newly arriving water must mix with the body water and becomes strongly turbulent during this process — that is, disordered water conditions occur that make every orderly food supply impossible and make the trout hungry.

Principle of movement of living beings in the media of water and air

In the preceding examples, the influence of the medium of water through temperature changes was shown. In the following examples, the behavior of living beings or other floating bodies at various temperatures shall be demonstrated.

The principle of movement of fish in water or the movement of birds in air is the same in both cases. There are merely structurally obvious differences that are attributable to the different properties of the medium. Each can be influenced in its own way through reaction phenomena in order to achieve the desired effect, which is the most efficient locomotion.


---


The bird, like the fish, possesses the ability to eliminate the resistances that arise during locomotion in the medium through physical processes — that is, without significant energy expenditure — or at least to reduce them at the right moment. Through this perception, what was previously considered impossible — namely, great performance with small means — is brought within the realm of fact, and thereby the cornerstone of the energy laws established so far is called into question.

The resistances and frictions that, according to natural law, grow with the square of velocity are canceled out by the aforementioned physical phenomena in the same proportion. After utilizing these new viewpoints in practice, at the appropriate moment, only the resistance-free medium will remain to be overcome.

Once again, it is necessary to study the energy forms in question here in the greatest detail, in order to artificially imitate and exploit this ideal movement here or there for our purposes when applied.

If it is already a curiosity that the trout, considering its small strength, moves upstream at such great speed, then it is certainly also highly puzzling at first glance that, for example, the trout is able to stand still in sharply flowing waters in which scarcely any human can remain standing, without significant exertion.

The resident trout in healthy, clear, cold mountain water stands in the river thalweg — that is, where the water threads closest to a temperature of +4°C are flowing.

It influences, through its smooth, slippery skin surface — thus initially through a purely mechanical energy form — the velocity of the water flowing past. The water displaced by the body volume of the trout first comes under pressure and glides along the slimy body at a greater velocity than the water particles flowing farther away. The next consequence is an exceeding of the highest possible flow velocity of the water for the momentary temperature, whereby an intensification of turbulence occurs. In the neutral zone that forms through this counter-current of the water, the trout is able to stand still effortlessly (see sketch).


---


If the water becomes too warm through external influence, the reaction also becomes weaker, but in particular, movement and counter-movement fall out of proportion and the trout is easily pushed downstream.

In the opposite case, however — that is, when the temperature suddenly drops — it is pushed upstream. The trout also immediately responds to gross changes in the labile equilibrium point with a positional change adapted to the altered conditions, in order to restore the rough equilibrium between water movement, reaction (counter-movement), and body shape. The adjustment of the labile equilibrium state described above takes place solely through turbulence intensification by purely mechanical means.

With the help of gill respiration, however — that is, through purely physical impulses — the trout is able to neutralize not only the large and rapid movement processes but also smaller irregularities.

- 59 -

The effects triggered in this way stand in contrast to the comparatively small exertions of force by the trout and can therefore be increased to unusually high performance.

The trout is driven away by the flowing water as soon as gill respiration ceases. If the trout swims rapidly upstream, respiration begins with great intensity. If the trout flees, it gives, so to speak, full steam with its gills.

To explain these processes, the following:

The formation of the counter-vortices described above, produced by mechanical means and adapted to body size and type of movement, gives the trout, so to speak, the rough equilibrium position.

The balance pointer of the labile equilibrium state, however, is the ability to increase the water velocity through physical phenomena, whereby, with the help of gill pressure, an intensification of turbulence (counter-vortices) takes place. Gill respiration serves, as we shall see below, not only for air supply but also for locomotion — an arrangement that once again demonstrates nature's entire superiority.

The trout increases the water velocity through rapid and deep respiration insofar as correspondingly large water masses, from which oxygen has been extracted, are guided along the body through the gills, whereby the turbulences intensifying in the potential sense can be produced as counter-movements in almost any desired form and strength (sketch).

In this way, the counter-currents originally produced by mechanical vortex formation are multiplied, and the trout really only needs to adjust the caudal fin correctly in order to exploit the reaction effects.

The end effect is the springing of the body against the direction of the flowing water. During the breathing pause, the mechanical vortices act alone. Weak screwing movements with the fins destroy the vortices produced by mechanical means (vortex destruction through vortex formation) — and the trout drifts. With the first breath, the trout stands still again in the sharpest water jet.


---


The secret of the theory of fish movement in water, which has been puzzling to us until now, lies merely in the exploitation of the laws of water movement that were previously completely unknown. Thus, after all that has already been said here, it will no longer sound strange when the claim is now made that not only the current water power machines (turbines), but also the ship in water, as well as the airship or airplane, acting against the laws of the medium — air or water — are neither economical nor reliable.

- 60 -

Aviation

One of the next practical examples of an artificial influence on the medium carried out in a lawful manner will be the proof that it is possible to bring any aircraft down from any altitude.

The air pockets and vertical gusts so feared in aviation, which cause the machines to crash, are nothing other than interacting temperature gradients that, as a rule, shift lawfully in the early morning, forenoon, or toward evening. Most aircraft crashes, insofar as they are attributable to these causes, are known to always occur around these times, in which question the season of course also plays a major role.

These shifts in temperature gradients can be produced artificially in the simplest way, and with the help of this knowledge, one of the newest and supposedly greatest achievements — military aviation — can be effortlessly eliminated.

Although not belonging here, it should already be briefly touched upon that the current type of propulsion of aircraft also does not correspond to the natural law arrangements, and that current flying represents a pronounced game of chance, to which a — one could almost say — incorrect ray of sunshine can already bring a sudden end. The justification for this, and in particular why the glider pilot can only stay aloft at certain hours, shall be discussed in forthcoming treatises.

If aviation is not yet at the desired level, this cannot, in view of the little experience gained in the few years of its existence, be considered a reproach.

- 61 -

Timber floating

If, on the other hand, one considers perhaps the oldest technique — the floating of timber — and considers that despite the enormous destruction and the resulting damages that can no longer be calculated at all, this method of timber transport is still practiced today, then no word can be too harsh to condemn this simply incomprehensible thoughtlessness.


---


Through the removal of the forest, the transport medium, water, is released. The consequence is the previously described, lawfully occurring destruction of the channel. At this stage, the log driving manager does even more by using large additions of water from the so-called driving dams.

What is achieved is precisely what is not wanted, or rather what should be avoided. Instead of the wood being moved forward, it is thrown sideways and washed ashore by the supposed auxiliary measure. To prevent this, the forester created his bank reinforcements, which, by the way, were later also adopted by the watercourse regulator. Battens, stone boxes, guide and cross-works, etc., now serve to keep the wood away from the banks.

The effect, in short, is the following: The wood slides along with the water at the now smooth, steeply set bank wall, similar to how water slides off the slippery body of a fish. Significantly greater turbulences arise than before. The stone box wall, etc., is primarily lawfully undercut and collapses after a short time. As long as these walls hold, the turbulent water masses are forced into a completely incorrect flow form, and the water is deprived of all guidance due to the wood rushing ahead too quickly.

The increasing counter-forces bypass and destroy the end of the reinforcement. After leaving the wall, the stored forces also attack the now unprotected banks with reinforced violence, to destroy them completely.

The next necessary consequence is renewed bank reinforcement. An endless chain of bank works arises, which not only make any reasonable downstream transport of wood impossible at enormous cost, but these bank works are precisely the dangerous guide works during floods. At these guide works, the potential forces already discussed above in many examples are generated and stored. Far down in the valley, where there was never any danger, destruction phenomena are suddenly triggered, which act as unexpectedly as they are unfamiliar, destroying valuable cultivated land that is already too scarce even without this forestry destruction technique.

- 62 -

In this way, things have already progressed so far that all once-healthy and fish-rich Alpine streams have almost without exception become veritable fishless watercourse ruins, which carry enormous masses of debris downhill during floods, cause unprecedented devastation, and are again waterless in normal times.

The rest is done by the hydroelectric plants now springing up in every corner. If our water engineers continue in the methods practiced so far and eventually reach for the last, partially still healthy water remnants located at high altitudes (Tauern power plant), then we will experience within a few decades that all the power plants built with enormous means will stand without water, and we will additionally lose the last remnants of fertile soil that is still available to us, however sparsely.


---


As long as the forester stays within the bounds of what is possible, the stream flowing from almost every forest will deliver the forest's interest, the wood, almost free of charge.

But if the forester (in this case already a forest devastator) goes further and changes the basic form under which the forest can thrive at all (clear-cutting), then nature protects itself. The destruction of the forest immediately leads to the destruction of the watercourses, to the destruction of the only profitable means of transport. Felling a forest cut off from the water is no longer worthwhile even at very short distances, and this forest is saved at the expense of human labor. Replacement transport means, especially forest railways, have rarely proven themselves in practice in the long run, because they require too large a volume to amortize themselves. Attempts by entrepreneurs in these cases to come out ahead through forced exploitation conjure up such dangers for the entire national economy that the state, once it has fully recognized the true connections and the associated dangers, will have to proceed with the sharpest coercive measures.

Treating water with mathematical formulas alone is unthinkable. Proper treatment of water requires above all great interest and much feeling, similar to that of a good doctor.

In water treatment there are infallible symptoms, which shall be briefly summarized once more:

As long as a watercourse delivers the wood itself, i.e., free of charge, the forester may use his axe. The deterioration of the waterways means a warning of dangers that, it is no exaggeration to say, threaten our own existence most severely.

- 63 -

As long as the trout stands quietly in the watercourse because the food flows into its throat without its own effort, economically favorable conditions for humans will also exist. If the water shows signs of destruction, the trout becomes restless, wood begins to wash ashore, then in the same proportion as the animals in the water lose quality, the conditions of life diminish, which the man bound to the soil cannot possibly do without.

The current methods of torrent control, river regulation, and hydropower engineering in general will have to change fundamentally.

The increasing karstification in the upper reaches due to the ever-further sinking of the groundwater table, destruction and devastation of cultures in the lower reaches, the ruleless and undisciplined routing of floodwaters into the valley, the increasingly sharply expressed inequality in the distribution of precipitation, the increasing swamping in low-lying areas, the natural catastrophes that strengthen locally from year to year, the decline of agriculture, etc., are largely also to be charged to the account of a completely incorrect handling of watercourse regulation.


---


Without even a faint suspicion of the working principles that prevail in nature, without any inkling of even the most fundamental laws of water movement, far-reaching regulation works were undertaken that fundamentally alter the natural landscape and work in direct opposition to the laws prevailing here. Instead of holding before one's eyes, as would have been obvious, that vegetation at high altitudes, the forest, is just as necessary as, to give one example, the skin on the body, everything was actually done to destroy the wonderfully interconnected relationships existing in nature, which are almost indestructible.

In the belief that the forest exists only to be exploited for every conceivable purpose, everything was done not only to exploit what is necessary for life in nature and for the preservation of the soil as an object of base speculation, but also to destroy it through completely wrong measures into the bargain.

The most remarkable thing, however, is that despite all the bad experiences and the economic collapse we have actually already brought about by these means, there is still adherence today to these regulation and forest treatment methods that are practically simply impossible, when the forest, the primary precondition for any culture, is provably doomed to die through the measures of its authorized organs, and not a single case can be proven where the regulation of even a small stream has been practically and flawlessly successful.

Millions of workers and employees are already unemployed. Thousands of farms stand on the brink of collapse. Even the most ingeniously constructed machines are no longer able to work the exhausted soil in such a way that the labor expended stands in proper proportion to its yield. Through the destruction of the forest and the completely misguided regulation of our watercourses, the state of equilibrium in the natural economy has been disturbed. Such an agricultural and forestry crisis, which naturally must have all other economic upheavals as its immediate consequence, could never have occurred if forest and water had been treated even halfway sensibly.


---


No better than the forester treated the forest has the water engineer treated the watercourses of all kinds, and it is no further wonder if, in view of the connections between forest and water, the channels are relatively in an even more deplorable condition than the forest.

At a time when thousands of people are languishing in unemployment and in reality twice the number of people would be at least necessary to halt the general economic decline threatening us, which can only happen if the mistakes made are remedied as quickly as possible, i.e., the forest is rebuilt as it once was and must be, the channels are re-balanced through the creation of appropriate reservoirs so that they can at least for the time being return more or less to their lawful paths, produce healthy water again, and thus again supply healthy blood for plants and living creatures — at such a time, people idly stand by and watch as individuals who have already lost all connection with nature destroy and smash not only further livelihoods but literally the last possibilities of recovery.

TEMP. AND WATER MOVEMENT 5, Influence on Structure Formation (t8)

- 65 -

Temperature and Water Movement

Influence on Structure Formation, Forestry and Water Management.
By Viktor Schauberger, Vienna.

from "Die Wasserwirtschaft" (The Water Economy), Year 1931, Issue 3

Contents:

Influence on Structure Formation
The Dying Forest;

The lifespan, i.e., the structural safety of a dam, is, as can be seen from the treatise published in Issue 35 of "Die Wasserwirtschaft," Year 1930 (The Movement of Temperature in Gravity Walls), not only a question of statistically correct calculation; it also depends on the temperature influences exerted on the wall by the surrounding layers of air and water.

If a negative temperature gradient establishes itself inside the dam wall, caused by external influence, i.e., if the water particles inside the dam wall move away from their temperature of +4°C with their temperature, then a cavitating effect occurs, in contrast to a sedimentating effect that occurs in the dam wall when the water particles inside the dam wall approach their temperature of +4°C. (Positive temperature gradient) (Die Wasserwirtschaft, Issues 20 and 24, Year 1930).

Cavitating effect means enlargement of the pores, thus loosening of the wall structure through dissolution and leaching of certain salts at certain temperatures, whereas sedimentating effect means densification of the wall structure (pores) through deposition of certain salts inside the dam wall at a certain temperature movement.

Deterioration or improvement of the wall structure is therefore dependent on the ratio of the duration of the positive to the negative temperature gradient inside the dam wall.

The type and duration of the respective temperature movement is naturally


---


- 66 -

naturally again a question of location, of the general climatic conditions, and thereby mainly a question of the compass direction in which the wall faces (direct or indirect solar influence).

The same phenomena thus appear in the dam wall as in nature during the degradation of mountains under the influence of the sun (negative temperature gradient) and their reformation under the exclusion of the sun and the influence of the low temperatures found partly in the sea and in the interior of the earth (positive temperature gradient).

Becoming and passing away in nature are therefore a question of the hitherto completely neglected or disregarded type of temperature movement in bodies, the lawful consequence of the anomaly of water, the significance of which has not been appreciated until now, but which plays the main role not only in the formation and destruction of rock (dam walls and the like).

Through this anomaly of water, the structure is altered or created in both inorganic and organic formation. Thereby, there (in the wall) the lifespan, and here moreover the very possibility of life itself, as well as the reproductive capacity of living beings, which of course also includes the plant world, is influenced by the correct or incorrect formation of structure.

Being or non-being of all bodies and natural products is therefore the question of the lawfully correct or incorrect type of water movement in the inner circulation of the earth. (Issue 20 — see "Temperature and Water Movement 1" — Year 1930 of Die Wasserwirtschaft.)

Thus the energy form of water movement, which in one case means the affirmation of life and in the other the negation of life, is a function of the hitherto neglected temperature gradient of water.

A = f (tg)

Water is known to be the carrier of certain nutrient salts. These nutrient salts are dissolved at certain temperatures of the water, i.e., under a certain temperature gradient, transported at a certain temperature gradient, and deposited again at a quite specific temperature gradient.

The type of temperature movement, the temperature gradient, and thus the consequence of the respective energy of the water in the interior of the earth, of bodies, or of living beings, plays a greater role in the supply of nutrients from the earth's interior, which is indispensable for vegetation,

- 67 -

than has hitherto been assumed.

The type of temperature movement of the water in the interior of the earth and of bodies, etc., is logically again only a question of the possibility of direct or indirect irradiation by the sun, and consequently a question of the resulting general climatic conditions, and thereby naturally also a question of the direction of the mountain ranges and the valleys lying between them.


---


Through the aforementioned temperature processes, the supply of nutrients to the earth's surface and thus the very possibility of the existence of vegetation is created. The vegetation in turn enables the mitigation of extremes necessary for the respective altitude (direct solar radiation, low temperatures caused by strong evaporation).

The balance, which becomes especially necessary in the mountains to an increased degree, and the groundwater retention on steep slopes, which is particularly important here, can only be created by the healthy forest, properly graduated according to tree species, area, altitude, and age.

Without these preconditions, i.e., without consideration of the general climatic conditions and the location, without consideration of the forest conditions correctly distributed according to area, altitude, tree species, and age, there can be no healthy, i.e., no regulated water conditions and thus no productive agriculture in high altitudes. This question naturally becomes more important the higher the location and the harsher the climate.

Severe winters with a frost belt reaching deep into the ground enable the storage of high temperatures in the earth's interior, which is important in these locations. Contrasts and thus tensions arise in the ground. The water is driven upward through a highly simple condensation process, which must strengthen upward in spring. In summer, it is the properly graduated forest that, through its deep soil temperatures, keeps the water and thus the nutrients within the reach of a naturally correctly distributed root system.

Unfortunately, this natural process has not been heeded in forestry to this day, and so it had to come about that through exactly contrary measures (clear-cutting economy) a relaxation of the soil occurred through interruption of forest protection, and thus a sinking of the water. Instead of cultural development, cultural decline results, as a consequence of a forest management that qualitatively ruins the forest, the first and most important precondition of culture, instead of building it up.

The main reasons for our cultural decline are:

1. The forestry economy, now consolidated into large economic complexes, which destroys itself as well as other cultivated land.
2. The completely misguided river regulation and torrent control.
3. The incorrectly managed energy economy of water.

The Dying Forest

For decades, the young plant, the healthy offspring, once lived in the healthy, natural forest, uninfluenced by humans and their science, under the protection of the mother trees in uniform conditions of temperature, humidity, and light.


---


Only with the dying of the mother trees did the young stand, by then mostly already mature, come into direct enjoyment of light and warmth, i.e., only at a time when the youth period, which reacts to extreme weather influences with extremely wide annual rings, was already past, and the surplus of light and warmth served not only uniform further development but as a necessary addition also benefited reproduction. It should be expressly noted here that the trunk itself remains protected from the direct influence of the sun as before, and only the crown of the tree is exposed to the sun. The great significance of this arrangement will become clear in the following.

The forester, to whom the phenomenon of so-called light growth naturally could not escape, saw here the possibility of scientific activity and the opportunity offered to him to correct nature. He established new laws, which, although contradicting the natural order, were in his opinion better and more correct, and exploited the moment in question for his new forestry at a time when the young plant reacts to too much light and warmth with widening, i.e., excessive growth of annual rings.

These newly blazed paths now also enabled clear-cutting operations and a supposedly more rational management through concentration of operating areas.

Already with the introduction of this system, a disappearance of certain understory species showed itself. This was not felt as a disadvantage, however, but rather as an advantage, because it seemed to avoid unnecessary draining of the soil through unusable understory.

Unavoidable with this form of management, however, was also the premature exposure of all shade-tolerant tree species sensitive to frost, which had accumulated under the protection of the old growth through natural regeneration, e.g., the silver fir. If a large part of this young growth perished as a result of freezing, the remaining stock nevertheless showed a striking light growth increment.

The silver firs, which often set centimeter-wide annual rings through sudden exposure, produce at this point a spongy wood, inferior in its consistency, which separates in ring-like fashion after felling, indeed often already in the standing stand. After drying, these spongy areas naturally contract differently from wood of healthy structure, and such wood is from the outset no longer suitable as utility timber.


---


The ring-shake condition known in silver fir, the internal ailing that begins with it, the inheritance of these detrimental effects on the again poorly treated offspring, had to lead for qualitative reasons to a slow and imperceptible, but sure, dying of this tree species — which, as will be seen later, is necessary for the very existence of the forest.

The decline of the silver fir, which naturally also did not escape the forester, could in his opinion be tolerated, however, because silver fir produces inferior wood that is not particularly sought after in trade and can be much better replaced by spruce wood.

This opinion is yet another and unfortunately even more far-reaching error than the one mentioned at the beginning, which for reasons of unfavorable regeneration methods had to lead to the extinction of certain tree species.

It is known that with the onset of forest management conducted according to forestry science principles, the qualitatively most valuable wood occurring in our area, the so-called resonance wood, disappeared at a single stroke.

This wood, which even in the primeval forest occurred only rarely, was not only dependent on the most extensive protection of the mother trees, but this wood could only develop in extremely sheltered locations, in hollow-like depressions, where, safe from all external influences, it grew under truly poor soil conditions in deep shade under precisely the opposite preconditions from those created by the forestry practiced today.

This slowly growing wood exhibits, in contrast to the fast-growing wood produced according to modern principles, annual rings that are almost invisible to the naked eye. Moreover, this wood shows a truly wonderful uniformity in its organic structure.

Resonance wood (the noble spruce, also called hazel spruce) is used primarily in instrument making. The wonderful tone color of the instruments made from this wood (from which even Stradivari built his famous violins) points not only to the healthiest, because most natural, form of development; this wood also has an almost unlimited durability.

If one now compares the structure of the wood raised according to current silvicultural practice with this quality wood, which has unfortunately already become legendary in the closer homeland, the almost irretrievable loss we have suffered through failure to recognize the facts stated above comes fully to consciousness.

Should the objection now be raised that one cannot dispense with the other great advantages of modern forestry for the sake of the resonance wood, which even in the primeval forest occurs only rarely, it should be emphasized that the above example is naturally only intended to point to the qualitative differences between wood regenerated and raised by natural and by artificial means.


---


The deterioration of wood quality, increasingly felt from decade to decade, and the growing difficulties in re-establishing cultures on steep southern slopes, will probably very soon make the question acute whether it is really worthwhile, for the sake of a relatively small light growth increment, to accept the catastrophic quality losses already showing themselves after barely a hundred years of activity of our modern forestry, indeed even to risk the existence of our entire high forest.

A closer study and, above all, a return to nature, which will become urgently necessary very soon, will show that the forest

- 71 -

is not, as has hitherto been supposed, an object of exploitation, but, especially in the mountains, a pronounced precondition of every culture, and that the ever-growing social misery is the consequence of today's forest destruction.

What at first glance thus appeared to be a great advantage, indeed almost a scientific achievement, turned out in practice to be a perhaps irreparable disadvantage, indeed a cultural decline.

The forester believes he can surpass nature. What was achieved was the decline and dying off of certain tree species through the premature application of a physical influence whose actual effects were not recognized, and which, through the forester's mode of action described above, was incorrectly applied, influencing the structure and thus the organic formation in the worst possible way.

These grave errors of today's clear-cutting economy have, unfortunately, still further serious consequences, which shall now be discussed in more detail.

The composition of tree species in the forest untouched by humans was and is by no means random, but rather one tree species was necessary for the other in the existing proportions.

The preservation of the forest is not solely a question of stocking, but also a question of the distribution of tree species according to root systems. This question becomes more important the higher the forest stands. (Mountains.)

Closer observation cannot fail to notice that the dying out of one tree species creates a gap in the medium of nutrition (soil), and thus the destruction of one tree species causes the disappearance of the other. This concerns the interruption of the water and thus nutrient supply, which will be discussed in more detail later.

The silvicultural principles of today's forestry practiced thus far (clear-cutting economy) with artificial regeneration thus lead to a qualitative and thereby general decline.

In place of the former coniferous high forest, whose unweakened natural power provided for its continuation without human assistance, we now have the sad result of a management system before us — the wood of our modern forestry — which after a further rotation will be unusable as quality wood and, moreover, will in all probability also lose the germinative capacity of its seeds.

- 72 -


---


The recent protest against the import duty on Polish noble wood for piano and violin making, indeed even the concern about using domestic wood for better furniture manufacturing, shows the sensitive decline that, if the current mode of operation continues, must within a short time lead to a complete collapse of this economically important branch of industry.

We thus stand literally before the dying forest, and the counter-arguments from forestry circles against the warnings recently made by the cultural engineer Dr. Ing. Kaltenbrunner, Vienna, are irrelevant insofar as it is not the quantity of any remaining old-growth timber that is decisive here, but the quality of the young stands replanted by artificial means, which are in turn supposed to become mother trees.

If the spruce disappears, the next main tree species in our latitudes is the beech.

Even if it would go beyond the scope of what is to be said here to explain the decline of springs in beech forests, this fact shall nevertheless be referred to, even if only briefly.

As an example of the unfavorable influence of a type of water movement through temperature effects that does not correspond to the organic structure of the body, it should also be mentioned that in the vicinity of large beech forests with water-rich soils, tuberculosis occurs in humans and animals, a phenomenon attributable to the abnormal development of water vapor (chemically pure water), i.e., nutrient-free water.

Chemically pure water has the property of greedily absorbing nutrients at certain temperatures, whereby the structure of the respiratory organs is attacked and thus the precondition for this dangerous disease is created in the lungs.

This phenomenon occurs primarily in humans and animals whose organic structure (structure of the respiratory organs) is not equal to this abnormal supply of water vapor.

The great mortality of the black soldiers brought from the hot climate to the West during the World War through hemorrhages and tuberculosis is not least attributable to the aforementioned phenomena.

A sick forest does not die alone, but in dying kills its destroyer — the human.

- 73 -

Reference should be made at this point to the tuberculosis in large closed beech forest districts, to which young foresters fall victim when they are transferred there from pure coniferous forest regions, if their stay in these beech areas lasts too long.

Similar, but even much stronger effects than on the respiratory organs occur in the digestive organs, in the stomach, when one permanently consumes chemically pure water (cistern or snow water) below +4°C or spring water of 4°C.

Just as humans and other living beings, plants too are attuned to a specific internal temperature corresponding to their species and the climatic conditions of their location, below which they can live, maintain themselves, and reproduce without weakening.


---


The maintenance of the correct internal temperature is, however, not only a question of location, but also naturally mainly a question of the formation of the structure, in which we now find water everywhere, albeit in different forms and types.

Again it is the irregularity — the anomaly of water — which causes a single ray of sun, under the circumstances and conditions described above, to bring about a change in the state of equilibrium between body and water, i.e., a movement through the change of the causes of movement (change of local temperatures), to a pulsation, and thus to an independent circulation, i.e., to life.

The more direct the solar influence, the more uneven the course of development through the change of day and night and seasons, and the greater the water requirement becomes. The structure becomes looser, and in the same proportion the quality of the wood deteriorates.

Moderate warmth influence (indirect solar radiation) under the protection of the mother trees (natural regeneration) leads to a dense and uniformly structured formation throughout. Quality wood can thus only thrive under the protection of the mother trees, i.e., only as nature has intended it.

The more and the earlier the young plant is exposed to the direct light of the sun, the earlier the plant, if it is a shade-tolerant species, must decline in qualitative structure due to incorrect structure formation.

For this reason, all light-demanding tree species are also able to protect their internal structure from the sun through strong bark formation.

- 74 -

This does not apply to the shade-tolerant species, however, and so the silver fir must decline first and foremost, but ultimately even the spruce, which is able to protect itself through thicker bark formation, under today's forestry treatment — if more slowly than the silver fir, then ultimately also hopelessly. This disappearance of the spruce naturally begins first in the higher southern slopes, where the shallow-rooted spruce, after the disappearance of the silver fir, is no longer able to retain and draw up the water it needs because of the strong solar radiation (expanded structure).

The spruce wood growing on good soils under full light influence, however, in turn produces such spongy and soft wood that red rot becomes noticeable after barely a year of storage of the felled wood, indeed in some cases already in the standing stand.

Such wood performs moderately in the processed state and is naturally no longer suitable for better quality goods and thus for export.


---


The influence on natural forces through conventional forestry has in any case achieved only very short-term and then only apparent successes. Nature reacted lawfully with a sensitive decline in quality, so that we have already experienced our somewhat far-sighted business world very energetically wishing to preserve the possibility of obtaining foreign quality wood from forest stands on which modern forestry has not yet exerted such a profound influence as is unfortunately already the case in the forests of the closer homeland.

In dam construction, under certain preconditions, by disregarding the extremely important type of temperature movement (temperature gradient) in the wall, one can create an enormous danger for those living downstream; moreover, through an incorrect temperature gradient in the impounded water, climatic changes can be brought about through changes in soil temperature, which, like everything in nature, do not show themselves immediately, but then all the more severely.

In forestry, after barely one rotation, the consequences of severe interventions in nature are now showing, which can only be remedied again with enormous sacrifices — if it is not already too late for this.

The irregularities in water distribution that have been showing themselves ever more frequently in recent years

- 75 -

(flood catastrophes, droughts, landslides, indeed even the rising of poisonous gases from the earth, so-called death mists, the appearance of diseases when warm vapors rise from the earth in winter), the negative temperature gradient showing itself everywhere and in various variations and ever more strongly intensifying, are the lawful reaction phenomena to our current, as will soon be proven, completely misguided water management, whose main fault, however, is to be sought only in today's forest management.

Today's forest management can be called without exaggeration one of the greatest dangers to culture, and if we are to put a halt to the cultural decline already showing itself everywhere, we must urgently return to the methods of natural regeneration, to the former natural forest, which never built itself side by side, but for millions of years one layer above another.

Individual remnants of this natural forest we still find in smaller farm forestry operations, where clear-cutting and the associated area regeneration method were impracticable from the outset because of the too small area size.

In this natural forest, age class upon age class (overstory and understory), tree species next to tree species, build themselves up seemingly without rule and in the most colorful mixture, under the protection of the mother trees, while in the supposed cultural forest, forest forms and age classes appear arranged as a homogeneous mass (in clear-cutting economy) side by side (horizontally) without regard to climate and altitude.


---


This, to put it mildly, nature-contrary treatment of the forest (systematic juxtaposition of clear-cut area next to clear-cut area) leads through the alteration of soil temperatures to a systematically progressing relaxation in the soil.

The inevitable consequence of a systematic elimination of the temperature contrasts in the earth's interior, which, as will be seen later, are indispensable for the thriving of vegetation, is now the beginning sinking of the groundwater table on the slope and the swamping in the plain.

This means, however, the interruption of the hitherto equally unknown nutrient supply from the earth's interior, which is only a function of the temperature gradient whose significance has not hitherto been recognized.

- 76 -

The temperature gradient, or as one might also say, the correctly adjusted or purposefully graduated energy form of the water moving in the inner circulation, is the precondition of a nutrient supply in the root zone of plants.

Decline of nutrient supply from the earth's interior, through disturbance of the water circulation in the interior of the earth, means decline of vegetation, and that is the decline of culture, the decline of agriculture, which no machine in the world, no rationalization, but only an immediately and correctly initiated water management can perhaps still halt.

- 77 -

TEMP. AND WATER MOVEMENT 6, Circulation of Water (t9)

Temperature and Water Movement

The Circulation of Water in the Interior of the Earth and the Related Nutrient Supply for Vegetation
By Viktor Schauberger, Vienna

from "Die Wasserwirtschaft" (The Water Economy), Year 1931, Issue 5

If one considers the consequences of the neglect of temperature influences in the field of forestry, which have occurred after barely one rotation, the question inevitably forces itself upon us whether the structural decline of the tree discussed above does not today or tomorrow find an analogue in the dam (gravity wall).
