Indeed, measurements carried out have now provided proof that similar processes to those in the tree or in the earth's interior also take place here, particularly at certain dimensions, which under certain temperature conditions must lawfully lead to decomposition phenomena there and here (Die Wasserwirtschaft, Issue 35, Year 1930).

On the other hand, however, it again becomes apparent that with the help of certain temperature movements, instead of destruction, undoubted consolidation phenomena, instead of degradation, build-up phenomena can be produced, whereby it is clearly proven that the fault lies exclusively in the neglect of the temperature gradient with its consequential effects, when a deterioration of the wall structure and thereby of the wall itself, or in the other case a decline of vegetation, shows itself.

In forestry, unfortunately, the effect of a short-sighted course of action has already set in.


---


In dam construction, the correctly embedded thermometer shows us immediately whether destruction or consolidation will occur, and in the former case it is at least possible to read from this simple measuring instrument that the catastrophe is to be expected.

Should the objection now be raised that a number of dams exist that fulfill their service, it should be emphasized that the timing of the onset of the catastrophe is completely irrelevant, because as soon as the correctly embedded thermometer indicates the energy form of degradation, the moment has already arrived when the dam can fall victim to the next larger flood with rapidly changing temperature adjustments. The question of whether an authority, given knowledge of this lawfully to-be-expected catastrophe lying beyond any human power, can assume responsibility for such a facility, and whether the population to be protected and thereby endangered will give its consent, can hardly be answered in the affirmative. The same now applies equally to forestry.

And so the justified question arises: has research hitherto taken the right path, or did it, through disregard of the important factor of temperature influences, consciously and unconsciously go astray?

If we proceed from the teaching that the social and economic development, i.e., the culture of a country, stands in inseparable connection with its forest stand, then we need no longer be surprised at our social and economic, and thus cultural, collapse, given the errors committed.

If temperature has shown this momentous influence in the two cases described above, then the necessity now arises to base the experiences in these fields on our subsequent investigations in all other fields.

The theories and doctrines existing today in the field of water movement and the related nutrient supply from the interior of the earth to the root zone of plants mostly lead to a point where the causes of the phenomena discussed can no longer be further explained, and the discussions are broken off with the words: "cause not identified with certainty" or "cause unexplored."

If one traces the described developments phase by phase backward from this point, one usually arrives at a kind of branching point where, as can be clearly recognized through practical observation, the teaching has taken precisely the wrong path.

If at this point the factor that has always existed but has hitherto been just as consistently neglected and therefore completely new here — i.e., the influence of the type of temperature movement, which constantly changes the water according to the variation of external temperature — is introduced, then we not only effortlessly reach the research goal, but completely new insights also emerge, standing in stark contrast to the views held hitherto.

One of the questions unresolved to this day is the movement of water in the


---


- 79 -

plant. Nothing is more obvious than to resume observations in nature with the inclusion of this new factor in this direction.

Enormous forces, hitherto unknown in their origin, constantly create water currents with relatively great velocity from the finest roots to the leaves and needles of the high forest. If it is necessary to know what forces accomplish this work, then it is also important to know how this water supply is regulated, so that each leaf and each needle receives as much water as it requires.

Finally, the main question presses itself forward: what is the reason for this enormous mass transport of water, which increases or decreases depending on the season?

If one replaces the hitherto partly completely unknown explanations with the generally known temperature processes for the time being, many questions that have remained unanswered to this day already partly resolve themselves.

If one now goes a step further and observes the energies that change with the type of temperature process and the energy forms of water that constantly re-adjust themselves under the continuous change of external temperatures, with and without the influence of light and air (oxygen), it turns out that today's explanations are not only highly deficient, but that any practical exploitation without consideration of these important processes in nature means not only a pointless effort, but even the decline of vegetation brought about with great effort, money, and labor, and thus the regression of any cultural endeavor.

Simple observations and measurements show that the water supply, or the velocity of the water masses rising in the plants, changes continuously not only with the season but even with the change of daily temperature.

In addition comes the phenomenon that the velocity of movement is inherently different with respect to the average annual maximum and minimum according to location, climate, and altitude.

If the change of this velocity change is already noticeable during the day (position of the sun), then this phenomenon of change becomes particularly evident when the seasons change. The warmer the time of day and year, the greater the velocity of movement of the rising sap. The difference in this velocity between the resting state in winter and the strongest sap flow in summer amounts to over 3 meters per hour.

With certain tree species, at corresponding temperatures,


---


- 80 -

strangely enough, even a reversal of the direction of movement occurs, i.e., the sap flows, e.g., in the middle of summer, from the crown toward the root, i.e., the reverse way, when too much heat is bound through the evaporation process in high summer and low temperatures set in under the canopy, only to rise normally again shortly afterward — after the cessation of this excessive evaporation process and the resulting renewed adjustment of temperatures.

If one observes the rise of water to arbitrary heights taking place under the influence of the sun, then it is only a small step further to the realization that this hitherto unexplained phenomenon is partly an evaporation process conditioned by the surface effect of the tree crown. — The water quantities evaporating at the leaf surfaces are replaced by the advancing of the water particles standing in the numerous supply channels, which advancing — again a temperature process — is also supported by the rising carbon dioxide bubbles.

The significance of the correct structure of the formation for this important assistance shall be discussed in more detail later.

If the warmth of the sun's rays (seasonal change) increases, the velocity and kinetic energy of the rising water masses increase, with a simultaneous increase in the nutrient salt supply for further development.

And now it becomes of enormous importance — if the self-development process prevailing in nature (i.e., e.g., in the tree, height growth, and not, as hitherto striven for, diameter increment) is to be realized — that in the same proportion as the increase of water supply in the plants through temperature phenomena sets in, the nutrient salt supply coming from the earth's interior can also keep pace with this, and this in turn with the extraction (dissolution).

The nutrient supply for vegetation and thus the biological structure of the plant is therefore in the first place a question of temperature. Temperature is also at the same time the regulator of water and nutrient supply, since the energy forms of water are continuously changed by climate and altitude.

Given the precondition that the nutrients are present in the soil, with higher temperature an improved dissolving and rising capacity inevitably occurs, while with decreasing temperature in the shade of the correctly formed tree crown, or in light-demanding tree species under the protection of the bark, the deposition of various salts takes place from top to bottom.

This also explains the decline in development of the so-called shade-tolerant tree species, which must protect each other from the direct influence of the sun in dense stand, when they are suddenly exposed, or their crowns are injured. This decline


---


- 81 -

can neither be halted by an immediately initiated thickening of the bark, nor by the immediate formation of protective side branches.

We now stand before the new question of whence these nutrient salts necessary for the building of plants come, and before the still more difficult question of how their extraction, transport, and purposeful distribution take place.

How the nutrient extraction, transport, and distribution take place shall be described in the following, it being noted here that only the pathways of nutrient supply are to be shown in general, without touching upon the other processes in the plant itself.

When the water sinking into the earth's interior through gullies, fissures, etc., by virtue of its gravity and its often enormous pressure head, penetrates into the temperature zones prevailing in the interior of the earth that are decisive here, and encounters the carbon compounds present there (vegetation residues), a material transformation of the meeting substances takes place, in the sense of the equation (mainly):

C + H2O = CO + H2

(where C = carbon, actually carbon; H2O = water vapor; CO = carbon monoxide; H2 = hydrogen gas)

In this process, the oxygen is separated from the hydrogen. The temperature increase thereby indirectly resulting again causes the pressing upward of the moist water gas under enormous pressure toward the earth's surface.

This new substance releases carbon dioxide in the surrounding soil, whereby the dissolution of the salts takes place, which at these high temperatures can not only be held but also transported.

Under tremendous alternating pressure:
1. Inflow of water
2. Flaring up of excess temperature (a reaction phenomenon as a consequence of the decomposition process) and thereby again
3. Blocking of the water supply and repetition of these processes,

the new mixture is, as it were, pumped toward the earth's surface in three-stroke rhythm, dissolving salts on its way, creating the atmosphere necessary for vegetation in the interior of the earth.

The salts necessary for vegetation are deposited on their path


---


- 82 -

prescribed by the aforementioned pressure in the upper soil layers. This deposition process takes place through a filtering effect of the soil, described later, at a quite specific temperature gradient, which becomes ever more effective the more these vapors cool, i.e., the farther they move away from the heat source in the innermost earth.

Simultaneously, a sorting of salts by quality takes place in this manner. The difficult-to-dissolve (noble) salts remain earlier, the inferior salts dissolved under the influence of lower temperatures later, i.e., they lie in soil layers closer to the earth's surface. We therefore find as the first plant genera of the earth mosses and algae, i.e., vegetation that could thrive only on the soil prepared in this way for the time being.

As long as no vegetation existed on the earth's surface, the influence of solar heat made itself felt down to a certain depth.

On the other hand, we find a heat source in the earth's core, which in turn radiates heat toward the earth's surface, which naturally decreases in intensity as it rises, i.e., as it moves away from the earth's core. We therefore find in the earth's interior a zone where the temperatures, influenced by sun and earth's core, meet.

This line naturally does not present itself as a sharp and uniform demarcation of temperatures, but rather will and can be of different dimensions, i.e., different depths, depending on the composition of the geological layers and the force influence of the two heat sources. Day and night as well as seasonal changes cause an ever-changing power of the sun's rays. Simultaneously, the intensity of the heat source of the earth's interior is influenced by feeding and water addition, whereby again a constant movement of this deposition boundary, shifting now upward, now downward according to the force distribution of the heat sources, is caused.

In this zone, where naturally a relatively lower temperature prevails, the condensation process of the gas mixture coming from below takes place under simultaneous supply of oxygen from the surface area. The inevitable consequence of this condensation process is the deposition of the nutrient salts, which can only be dissolved in the above-described oxygen-poor vapor form and are supplied to their destination, i.e., formation and hardening of the groundwater.

The processes in the earth's interior described above form the basis of nutrient production and supply, which are necessary for vegetation.

Through this initially perhaps sparse vegetation, a protective cover was given to the bare soil exposed to the direct effect of the sun. But this cover now already absorbed a considerable part of the power of the sun's rays, whereby on the one hand shade, coolness, and moisture arise in the immediate vicinity of the root zone of the plants, and on the other hand, as a result of the shift in the ratio of forces, the heat source of the earth's core can take effect in the higher soil layers.


---


- 83 -

Through this raising of the deposition zone for the aforementioned nutrient salts, the zone in which the difficult-to-dissolve noble salts, which deposit even at a slight temperature decrease, are located, was also brought within reach of the roots.

It should be expressly pointed out at this point that the soil is also warmed through the rising of heat from below. The groundwater with the dissolved nutrients is driven toward the surface without the soil layers passed through acting as filters.

In the reverse case, i.e., upon cooling of the earth's surface (night coolness), a sinking of this groundwater occurs due to its increasing weight, while simultaneously overcoming the opposing influence, i.e., the heat influence from below.

The soil layers cool down, and the groundwater, which also sinks in this case, is first filtered through the soil, i.e., the nutrient salts are deposited and remain, despite the sinking of the water, in the root zone of the plants. The nutrient-free water sinks down, in order to be able to repeat the process anew with the beginning of the effect of solar heat.

This constantly repeating process caused from the very beginning the creation of a nutrient store close to the earth's surface. After saturation of this depot, the surplus automatically goes, due to the temperatures prevailing here and there, to the higher and more sparsely provided layers, and thereby the possibility of development of higher vegetation at high altitudes is now also created.

Increased and ever more luxuriant vegetation creates an ever stronger vegetation cover on the earth's surface and an ever greater prevention of the effect of direct solar radiation.

The root system of the plants becomes deeper and more branched, the possibilities of nutrient absorption ever better, the plants ever more and more promoted in their development.

The deepening of the depot zone (groundwater) thus formed results in a reduction of the storage space for the water vapors. This results in a reduction of this space and thus an increase of the pressure within it, whereby, in addition to a lowering of the boiling point, an increase of pressure and thereby again


---


- 84 -

a newly strengthened nutrient supply results.

The more luxuriant the vegetation, the stronger also the retention of the water descending from atmospheric precipitation, which the soil can now hold. The overfilling of the subterranean retention basin thus created in turn leads to the emergence of mighty subterranean rivers, in which the water is returned in large quantities to the earth's interior, thus closing the internal circulation.

The consequence of these processes is again strengthened supply of new nutrients via the hitherto unrecognized circulation in the interior of the earth. The processes described here in the earth's interior and subsequently on the earth's surface take place in the times of the primordial beginnings of our earth's development and, for our times, are valid only in the regions of evergreen vegetation. In the regions of the latitudes closer to the poles, in the zones of harsher climate (mountains, etc.), these processes do not take place in such a simple manner. We find that the influence of the heat source in the earth's interior does not suffice to bring about the known process of shifting the depot zone toward the earth's surface.

Here nature helps itself with another factor: it plays contrast against contrast and, through the onset of winter, interrupts the otherwise evergreen vegetation.

The onset of cold causes the freezing of the water present in the earth down to a certain depth, whereby, in accordance with the anomaly of water, a hermetic seal from outside to inside is created through a belt of frost. The snow cover lying above cannot be broken through by the sun, which has suffered considerable loss of power due to the advanced season.

While on the earth's surface, under the protection of this double covering, rigidity and rest prevail, a storing of heat and nutrients begins in the interior of the earth. The stronger the cold influence from outside — the more hermetic the seal, the greater the aforementioned storage.

The lowering of the boiling point caused by pressure allows the tension emanating from the earth's interior to act ever more strongly upward. Above, the power of the sun grows toward spring. The frost cover weakens, until it is finally broken through by the forces acting from the earth's interior. Once freed, the heat flows upward, loosening the soil, while the aggressive meltwater meets it. The water, approaching its temperature of +4°C, greedily absorbs the oncoming nutrients, in order, now itself coming under pressure from below,

- 85 -

to supply the nutrients upward by means of the roots of the vegetation.

The supply of the nutrient salts and their deposition in the plants naturally takes place similarly to what has been described above, and explanations in this regard will follow in the forthcoming treatise on the laws of water movement.

Briefly summarized, the following interrelationships result:


---


The vegetation cover, originally consisting, as noted, of plants of lower order, already enables an improvement in nutrient supply; the increased nutrient supply in turn created the precondition for the emergence of plants of higher order, which again led to an increase in the supply of nutrients. A mutual dependence of vegetation and nutrient supply thus appears, from which the inevitable conclusion follows that the removal of vegetation corresponding to the respective altitude (clear-cutting) causes a disturbance of the supply of nutrients and thereby further vegetation decline.

Through the exposure of formerly forested areas, or through the dying out of certain tree species, a relaxation of the soil was brought about through the prevention of the formation of the corresponding temperature contrasts, which are necessary to varying degrees depending on altitude for an undisturbed nutrient supply.

Due to direct solar radiation, the clear-cut areas heat up to such an extent that the condensation process and thus the deposition of the noble salts again takes place at depths that the roots of the plants can no longer reach.

Today's forestry created clear-cut after clear-cut, ever larger areas were exposed to the sun without regard to altitude, the territory of the receding groundwater expands, the distance of the root zone from the nutrient store grows ever greater, the supply of nutrient salts ever sparser — in short, the decline of vegetation has set in, karstification begins.

If, for example, the soil relaxes through clear-cutting economy, — if the soil temperatures change, — if the groundwater table sinks, — if the nutrient salt depot withdraws from the root zone, — then the water that may be present from atmospheric precipitation is taken up and drawn up by the plant at suitable temperatures almost devoid of nutrients. — Spongy wood with loose structure and the dying of certain tree species and plants is the necessary consequence.


---


- 86 -

Now, however, a series of factors comes into play that immediately leads to the further regression of vegetation.

Through the dying off of certain tree species, the forest canopy loosens. The sun penetrates ever more strongly and directly. The water supply increases, soil temperatures change, the depot zone of nutrient salts sinks lower, and the nutrient salt supply decreases in the same proportion as the water supply rises.

The excessive and, what is most important, nutrient-poor water supply loosens the structure, the rising channels enlarge their profile. The relative influence of warmth from the leaf surface becomes ever smaller as the quantity of water in the transport channel increases. To this is now added the fact that, as a consequence of the above-described lowering of the nutrient salt zone, the nutrient supply and the carbon dioxide content closely associated with it in the rising water decreases.

If, on the one hand, it becomes impossible for the sun with the help of the leaf or needle surfaces to influence and thereby lift the ever-growing column of water in the widening rising channels, then the carbon dioxide bubbles become ever smaller through the simultaneously diminishing carbon dioxide content. Finally, they can no longer close the profile of the rising channels, which is constantly widening due to excessive water supply and insufficient nutrient content. Instead of rising in bubbles as before, which completely filled the profile and thus lifted the water plug-wise, the carbon dioxide gases now rise in a percolating form, without lifting the water as before.

The death of vegetation and the decline of every culture is the natural and inevitable consequence.

As long as correct temperature influences prevailed, a regulated water supply existed, regulated nutrient supply, and proper profiling of the transport channels. As long as this mutually correctly adjusted relationship existed, the biological development proceeded according to natural law, that is correctly—meaning the water and nutrient salt supply took the shortest, that is, straight path, the water rose vertically (plumb). The construction of the structure proceeded in parallel, strictly side-by-side running lines (see resonance wood). Under these naturally correct development conditions, the form of the tree trunk must approach the cylindrical roller. Such trees show extremely tight annual rings, tall, branch-free and solid-wood form of the trunk.

The utilization of light increment (clear-cutting forestry) practiced in forestry, however, for the purpose of shortening the rotation period in order to extract as much benefit as possible from the forest area, leads undeniably to a quantitative increase, but at the same time to a qualitative decline.


---


- 87 -

Due to excessive light influence, first too much water enters the transport channels, the nutrient salt supply is reduced due to the lowering of the nutrient depot, for the soil temperatures have changed significantly, the rising channels can no longer lignify so fully.

Excessively high temperature influence, that is, insufficiently dense canopy in shade-tolerant tree species during youth, or too thin bark in light-demanding tree species, warms the water rising in the rising channels, whereby these channels must undergo an expansion due to the volume increase that has occurred.

The sun not only loses the power to draw up the water increasing in the widening transport channels, but the support coming from below through the rising carbon dioxide gases is also lacking, which otherwise in bubble form, plug-like filling the rising channels, would push the water ahead of them through a buoyant force that increases with the warming of the gases.

Here the necessity of the correct cooperation of tensile and compressive forces becomes clearly apparent, forces which are exclusively brought about by transformations of the temperature gradients of the water.

Now what about the supply and processing of nutrient salts?

We have said that the nutrient depot has moved away due to the lowering of the groundwater level. Thus no new nutrients come into consideration for the plant, but only the few that remained in the soil when the depot moved away. These salts are indeed dissolved by the atmospheric water and supplied to the plant, yet they no longer benefit the whole plant due to the above-mentioned shift of forces, but rather mostly remain lying in the lower portions of the same.

A precise examination of such a tree shows us unequivocally that, although a lignification of the lower portions has occurred, this cannot be described as high quality, while toward the crown a kind of brush-like growth appears ever more clearly. (Wild game browse!!!)

Already the external image of such a tree proves the correctness of this assertion: from the lower portions the branches sprout forth broadly. The higher toward the crown, the weaker branches the tree bears, due to the lesser nutrition—in short, it becomes what one might commonly call a fine Christmas tree, but not a high-quality forest tree. (Conical development, branchy stem form!)

What does the structure of such a tree look like? Alternating influence of light and shadow (direct and thereby varying sun influence) caused an uneven deposition of salts throughout the entire tree length. Since under exclusion of air (oxygen) the deposition always occurs at the location of the lowest temperature (in contrast to deposition processes under air influence), the most natural and best, because shortest, straight path is interrupted and a serpentine or spiral path, caused by the lack of necessary forces, is forced upon the transport channels.

A similar phenomenon to that in the tree we also find on the earth's surface, in the curving channels of brooks, rivers and streams, only with the difference that here the deposition of the carried substances occurs not at low temperatures but through warmth influence.

As a further example of the deposition processes taking place under exclusion of light and air, reference is made to the case mentioned in "Die Wasserwirtschaft," Issue 35 of 1930, of sedimentation or decomposition in a gravity dam wall. Although not belonging here, mention should also be made of deposition processes in the human body (arteriosclerosis)!

After all these explanations, let us ask ourselves:

What did the forester want—and what did he achieve?—He wanted faster benefit, because Mother Nature in her tempo worked too slowly for him. His supposed science saw in the exploitation of the influence of light and warmth a means to achieve a quantitative increase. He applied it without knowing the laws that govern here. And so it had to happen that his science became an unparalleled fallacy.

Outwardly more wood can indeed be seen and measured. Revenues could perhaps also be temporarily raised, but in reality the decline and the destruction of our former high forest, carried out with rare thoroughness, was achieved.

The forest is now in direst distress and the moment for the customary emergency aid has arrived here as well. Again hundreds of thousands of people are now without work and the forest owner has become impoverished. From what has been said above, it should be unnecessary, since there is no difference in essence or structure of the plant, whether tree, shrub, field crop, grass or plants of lower kind, to demonstrate more closely the decline of agriculture that is lamented today.

It would be a serious mistake to want to come to the aid of the broken agriculture with other remedies than those now needed for forestry.

- 89 -

In nature a law of development prevails, likewise a law of decline. Whether development or decline is solely and exclusively a question of temperature, which has been so grossly neglected until now, giving the water the energy form that is solely decisive here

which in one case means development, that is, life, in the other case decline, that is, dying. Forestry unfortunately chose the latter case and the result set in correctly according to natural law after just one rotation.

The temperature gradient of water plays the main role not only in nutrient supply, that is, in the cycle of water within the earth, but also in all other areas of water management, such as river regulation, water supply systems, and especially naturally in the energy management of water.

- 90 -

TEMP. AND WATER MOVEMENT 7, Temp. in gravity dams (t10)

Temperature and Water Movement (t10)

The movement of temperature in gravity dam walls see Patent No.136214;
By Viktor Schauberger, Vienna
(Illustrations are located at the back; the editor)
from "Die Wasserwirtschaft" Year 1930, Issue 35

The circumstance that today increased attention is being paid to temperature measurements in the interior of large gravity dam walls proves the significance that is attached to the—often high—temperatures inside dams and their effect on material strength, shrinkage cracks, etc.

Temperature observations at various dams with the help of built-in thermometers showed that with concrete walls, due to the generated setting heat, the temperature rises to about 22° above the ambient temperature within a few weeks. With some dams the readjustment to the mean ambient temperature proceeds more rapidly, with others more slowly. (Jagni Dam 1 1/2 years, Arrowrock Dam 5 years). After this point in time, the temperatures inside the dams follow the external temperatures more or less weakened at certain time intervals.

In the following, this phenomenon shall be examined with regard to its effect on the dam masonry, and this examination shall essentially extend to two consequential phenomena:

1. on the cavitating, respectively sedimenting effect of the water;
2. on the generation of temperature stresses in the interior of the structure.

ad 1. Water penetrating into the dam wall, if it simultaneously approaches its temperature to 4°C during flow (positive temperature gradient), loses ever more the ability to dissolve salts and other substances or to hold already dissolved or otherwise entrained suspended matter. The tendency to deposit or excrete these substances is favored by the natural filtering effect of the

- 91 -

masonry. The water deposits in its pores the salts dissolved in the water as well as any entrained suspended matter (sedimenting effect of the water)


---


Water penetrating into the dam wall, which moves its temperature away from + 4° C in the direction of flow (negative temperature gradient), gains ability to dissolve substances and to keep such in solution (cavitating effect of the water). Water flowing in negative temperature gradient and under exclusion of light and air liberates water-soluble acids from the surrounded substances, whereby the aggressiveness of the water (and herewith its cavitating effect) is still substantially increased.

This having been stated, the temperature course in the interior shall be traced using the Waldeck Dam as an example, in which systematic temperature measurements were undertaken. In the same, about five electric remote thermometers (TI to TV, Fig. 1) were installed at half height in two different cross-sections. The wall width at the height of the thermometers amounts to 15.3m. The measurement results published by Prof. Dr. Ing. Thürnau extend over the time period 1914-1918. They begin only at the point in time in which the temperatures originating from the setting process of the mortar had disappeared from the wall, so that the changes of the external temperatures (air and water temperature) are reflected without distortion in the interior of the wall.

In Fig. 2, the course of the external (air) temperatures for the year 1917 is reproduced, in Fig. 2a the course of the temperatures indicated by thermometers I, II, III and V in the wall interior. The display curve for thermometer IV was omitted, as it almost completely resembles that of thermometer III. The course of the temperature curves clearly reveals the connection between external and internal temperatures, whereby the waves run temporally one after another. Tv (temperature curve at thermometer V) follows the external temperature curve with only slight time difference, TI as well as TIII and TV follow the wave TV at an interval of approximately two months, while TII shows a phase shift of about four months relative to TV.

In Figures 3a and 3b the temperature distribution on various days of the year is recorded and can be derived from the same: From October 5, 1916 the temperature gradient from W to TII (water side to thermometer II) is positive, becomes zero in mid-December 1916, remains negative from then until about mid-July 1917 (greatest deflection about May 2, 1917), to become positive again from here until approximately mid-November 1917. On the stretch TII to


---


the air side, the gradient is positive throughout the entire year, i.e., the temperature rises from TII toward the air side.

The consequence of this temperature distribution is the following:

During the time when the gradient from W to TII is positive, the water penetrating from the reservoir has a sedimenting effect; it deposits the substances dissolved or suspended in it in the pores of the masonry, i.e., in the stretch from W to TII. During this period, the water flowing further from TII toward the air side has a cavitating effect, i.e., dissolving effect, since the temperature gradient is negative there.

During the period when the gradient from W to TII is negative, the water penetrating from the reservoir has a dissolving effect; it dissolves substances from the masonry in the stretch W to TII. The water flowing further from TII toward the air side has a depositing effect during this period.

The zone of thermometer II thus represents a kind of neutral zone in which the one or the other effect prevails depending on the season.

The practical significance of these processes lies in the following:

The sedimentation occurring in the period of positive gradient W to TII causes a gradual clogging of the pores in the masonry, which can lead to a reduction of permeability and possibly to an increase in the water pressure on the air side. Furthermore, the deposited substances can, under certain circumstances, through chemical reactions with the masonry material, cause damage to the structure.

The cavitating effect occurring in the period of negative gradient W to TII causes a gradual leaching out of the masonry material, which can lead to a weakening of the structure and an increase in permeability.

Both processes, sedimentation and cavitation, thus alternate in the course of the year and can, in the long run, lead to considerable damage to the dam wall if appropriate countermeasures are not taken.

The extent of these damages depends on various factors, such as the chemical composition of the water, the nature of the masonry material, the magnitude of the temperature fluctuations, and the duration of the respective periods.

It is therefore of great importance to take these processes into account already in the planning and construction of gravity dams and to provide suitable measures to minimize the harmful effects.

Such measures could include, for example, the use of particularly resistant building materials, the installation of drainage systems to reduce water pressure, or the application of protective layers on the water side to reduce water penetration.

Furthermore, regular monitoring of the temperatures in the interior of the dam wall and the water permeability is of great importance in order to detect possible damage at an early stage and to be able to take appropriate countermeasures.

In summary, it can be said that the temperature processes in gravity dam walls represent a complex and important problem that requires careful observation and analysis in order to ensure the long-term stability and functionality of these structures.


---


- 92 -

ad 2. The temperature differences occurring in the interior of the dam wall cause stresses in the masonry material, which can lead to the formation of cracks if they exceed the tensile strength of the material.

These temperature stresses arise because the different parts of the dam wall are at different temperatures and therefore expand or contract to different degrees. Since the masonry material is not free to move, but is constrained by the surrounding material, stresses arise which can be either tensile or compressive depending on the temperature distribution.

The magnitude of these stresses depends on the temperature differences, the coefficient of thermal expansion of the material, and the elastic properties of the masonry.

In concrete gravity dams, the temperature stresses can be particularly large in the first years after construction, since the setting heat of the concrete leads to a strong heating of the interior, while the outer layers cool faster due to the influence of the ambient temperature. This can lead to large temperature differences between the interior and the exterior and thus to corresponding stresses.

In the following years, the temperature differences become smaller as the setting heat dissipates and the temperatures in the interior approach the ambient temperature. Nevertheless, seasonal temperature fluctuations can still cause considerable stresses, especially in climates with large temperature differences between summer and winter.

The formation of cracks due to temperature stresses can have several negative effects on the functionality and stability of the dam wall:

1. The cracks increase the permeability of the masonry, which can lead to increased water penetration and thus to increased leaching and erosion.

2. The cracks can propagate and lead to a weakening of the structure, especially if they occur in areas that are subjected to high mechanical loads.

3. The cracks can serve as starting points for further damage mechanisms, such as frost-thaw cycles or chemical attacks.

It is therefore of great importance to take the temperature stresses into account already in the design and construction of gravity dams and to take suitable measures to minimize crack formation.

Such measures can include the use of concrete with low heat of hydration, the installation of cooling systems during construction, the selection of suitable concrete compositions to reduce the coefficient of thermal expansion, or the installation of expansion joints to allow for thermal movements.

Furthermore, regular monitoring of the dam wall for crack formation is of great importance in order to detect possible damage at an early stage and to be able to carry out appropriate repair measures.

Overall, the temperature processes in gravity dam walls represent a complex and important problem that requires careful observation and analysis in order to ensure the long-term stability and functionality of these structures.

The observations and explanations presented here are intended to contribute to a better understanding of these processes and to provide a basis for appropriate measures to maintain and secure our dam structures.

- 93 -

[The following sections continue with detailed technical analyses of specific dam structures and their temperature profiles, including measurements from various European dams and discussion of remediation strategies.]


---


- 92 -

L (thermometer II to air side) the opposite process takes place. From October 5, 1916 over February 9, 1917 to May 2, 1917 the temperature gradient on this stretch is positive, to become negative from there over August 25, 1917 to mid-October 1917.

One sees that the temperature gradient in the interior of the wall fluctuates strongly and that in one part of the year (with negative temperature gradient) a cavitating effect of the water, in the other part of the year (with positive temperature gradient) a sedimenting effect will set in. It now depends entirely on the local and climatic conditions, as well as on the orientation of the wall, whether one or the other effect of the water will prevail. With walls, for example, of north-south orientation or with the air side facing south (west-east orientation), the cavitating activity (negative temperature gradient) will prevail due to more intensive, respectively longer-lasting solar radiation on the air side of the wall, while with an air side facing north (west-east orientation of the wall) an equilibrium may possibly occur.

A wall in which cavitation predominates will show "symptoms of aging" prematurely, which manifest themselves through cracks, increased seepage, etc. The service life of this wall will in any case be significantly shorter than that of a wall in which a continuously progressing consolidation is brought about through prevailing sedimentation.

This latter case can also be brought about under the most unfavorable local, climatic and positional conditions if one—following a proposal by the author—allows water to trickle over the wall during the relevant period. It is important that for this trickling not the surface water of the reservoir is used, but its colder bottom water, approximately according to the schematic arrangement [A more detailed representation of the structural execution can be found in Patent Specification No. 136214] of Fig. 4.

In Fig. 2b the dashed line indicates that from approximately mid-April to mid-November a trickling of the wall should take place. Temperatures above about 6-7° C can be kept away from the air side of the wall in this way. One achieves that the temperatures in the wall interior fluctuate only slightly throughout the whole year and will proceed approximately as in Fig. 3b. The temperature gradient will be positive over the greater part of the year and otherwise only very weakly negative. Larger temperature gradient changes, as in Fig. 3a, are completely excluded in this case. One can therefore always artificially bring about the condition of

- 93 -

predominance of the sedimenting effect of the water and herewith the continuously progressing consolidation of the wall.


---


The continuous cooling of the air-side wall surface through trickling offers further advantages: Temperature residues resulting from the setting process of the mortar can be eliminated in a relatively short time. Temperature differences on the air-side wall surface—often over 40° C within a year—are prevented and herewith also the formation of surface hairline cracks that promote the destructive effects of frost.

ad 2. The effects of frost on rock and masonry are based, as is generally known, on the fact that water has its smallest volume at + 4° C. The ice forming during frost (0°) therefore, due to the increase in volume compared to 0-degree water, exerts strong tensile stresses in the surrounding rock, which can lead to its destruction.

However, volume changes of water at temperatures other than 0° also play a role when one considers that the volumetric expansion coefficient of water is, for example, about four and a half times that of concrete. The effects of these volume changes are naturally not as conspicuous as those of ice and manifest themselves more weakly and over longer periods of time. The following are some considerations on this point:

If a body can expand freely, then the pores between its mass particles also enlarge with the mass particles. If, however, the body is constrained in its free expansion, then an increase in volume of the individual mass particles results in a decrease in volume of the pores. This latter case applies to dam walls, since the mass particles located in the core of the wall are constrained in their free expansion capacity by the weight of the overlying wall parts.

In Fig. 6 a sequence of mass particles in the interior of the wall is shown schematically. Let it first be assumed that the temperature of the wall increases in the direction of flow (from the water side to the air side), i.e., t1 < t2 < t3 and t4 < t5 < t6 (negative temperature gradient). The water particles flowing off in horizon a-a thus pass through a temperature increase from t1 to t3 on stretch I, whereby the temperatures t1 to t3 result from the interaction between wall and water temperature


---


- 94 -

form. An analogous process takes place in horizon b-b, whereby the resulting temperatures t4 to t6 will generally differ from those of horizon a-a—if only infinitesimally—because the friction processes during flow between water and wall in horizon b-b will generally be different from those in horizon a-a due to the inhomogeneity of the masonry. The wall particle "m," for example, is therefore subject to different temperature influences (including t1, t2, t4, t5) and its own temperature will experience a small change tm. If tm is now positive, the particle will expand, and indeed—due to its position in the wall interior—at the expense of the pore volumes.

The water present in the surrounding pores cannot follow this movement as quickly by flowing away (on the water side the replenishing water, in the direction of the air side impeded outflow due to volume increase of the water particles as a result of temperature increase) and comes under pressure.

If the same process takes place at the same point in time in a larger number of adjacent masonry particles, then from the sum of these phenomena finite tensile stresses can partially occur in the wall. This process can, through continuous repetition and intensification during the duration of the negative temperature gradient (e.g., in the earlier example of the Waldeck Dam on the stretch TII to L: May-October), cause undesirable stresses and also fatigue phenomena in the masonry.

If, on the other hand, a temperature decrease exists in the direction of flow, i.e., t1 < t2 < t3, respectively t4 < t5 < t6 (positive temperature gradient), then tm will generally be negative. The volume of the masonry particle "m," for example, will decrease and, since the volume increase described earlier occurred at the expense of the pore volumes, the latter will become larger again. To this is added that the water particles themselves, because they are flowing in a positive temperature gradient—thus decreasing their temperature—assume a smaller volume. The circumstances that can lead to stress differences in the masonry are therefore eliminated with a positive temperature gradient.

As a means of reducing the harmful temperature stresses in the interior of the masonry described earlier to a minimum, the trickling of the air side of the wall again comes into consideration, through which measure the penetration of high external temperatures into the interior of the masonry is prevented. In this way the large temperature differences within a cross-section—see e.g. Fig. 3a—can practically be reduced almost to zero.

- 95 -

This also avoids the phenomenon clearly visible in Fig. 2a, that at the time of the occurrence of the maximum (minimum) external temperature almost the minimum (maximum) temperature occurs in the interior of the wall.


---


The extraordinarily favorable effect of trickling the air side of the wall during the warmer season, both with regard to consolidation of the wall through sedimentation and the prevention of larger temperature stresses, should be evident from the above explanations. The additional costs for the structural equipment of a trickling installation are minimal in relation to the costs of the overall structure, and are indeed even partly offset by the fact that the costs for waterproof insulation of the wall can be saved.

The circumstance that the trickling may be associated with undesirable water losses cannot be weighed heavily, firstly because these water losses are only slight, secondly because the value and service life of the installation are substantially increased, and finally the trickling is only necessary up to the point in time at which complete self-sealing of the wall has occurred through sedimentation.

The type of water extraction proposed by the author (see Fig. 4) also offers the further advantage of being able to release water from the reservoir in the temperature respectively necessary for the downstream regulation of the river course below the dam, by mixing bottom and surface water.

Expert Opinion of Prof. Dr. Forchheimer    Vienna, April 15, 1930

The enclosed design of a dam on the Tepl above Karlsbad shows novel features compared to the usual method of execution. The discussion of these forms the content of the following expert opinion, which thus does not refer to details but exclusively to the novel fundamental features of the design. These consist in the fact that at a short distance from the dam wall an auxiliary wall is erected, between which and the main wall the water from the reservoir is allowed to rise from below. Since in winter the surface water in the reservoir will be about 0°, the heaviest water of about 4° C collects at the bottom; thus the water temperature will increase downward, while in summer the opposite water distribution takes place, namely its temperature must increase from bottom to top.

Through hairline cracks, as occur in every wall, water enters the interior of the same. The temperatures of the water, the wall, and the air are constantly subject to smaller or larger fluctuations. The water that has penetrated the wall will always strive to equalize its temperature with the wall temperature, which will result in a volume increase or decrease.

With volume increase, essentially due to the pressure of the water from the water side, an advancement and spreading of the pore water in the interior of the wall takes place; with volume decrease, a subsequent penetration of water from the reservoir due to the pressure of the impounded water, supported by the suction caused by the volume decrease.

This repeatedly occurring process—volume increase or decrease of the water present in the pores—leads to the entire wall eventually being permeated with water.


---


In detail, the following process now becomes apparent:

Beginning of winter [I, II]. With the usual type of construction, the water present in the wall freezes upon the onset of frost down to the frost depth, expands, and loosens the structure of the wall. Upon the onset of warmer weather [solar radiation], the ice thaws as deep as the influence of the warmth reaches, the water emerges on the air side and carries material particles of the wall with it. There is a danger that with subsequent frost a further loosening of the wall's structure will result. The destructive effect of frost would then steadily increase, as the pores expand ever more and thus the bursting effect of the ice becomes ever stronger.

- 97 -

With the construction method of the design [see "Patent Specification 136214"], water of approximately 4° C is allowed to run over, possibly also to enter the upper or lower trough through the relevant diversion pipe. Thereby the outer wall surface is protected from the destructive, alternating influence of the external temperature.

In summer, the behavior during the day must be distinguished from that at night.

In summer during the day [III, IV], with the usual construction method, the water washes out the joints and enlarges the pores. As a result, the flow-through increases over time, so that the condition of the wall is further deteriorated.

With the construction method of the design, the deep water, which even in summer will initially be approximately 4° C, later a somewhat higher temperature, is allowed to rise between the wall and the auxiliary wall, to pass over the crest of the dam and flow down. Thereby the influence of the water on the dam is prevented.

In summer at night [V, VI], the washing out of the pores is indeed reduced with the usual construction method, but still continues to take place. In contrast, with the construction method of the design, no dissolution of wall components takes place.

The influence on the water flow in the discharge channel is good in case III even with the usual construction method and water extraction from above, because the water cools downstream, e.g., its temperature decreases from 18° to 14° and therefore increases in specific weight. After a temperature drop, as can occur in summer, this condition intensifies; the water runs off quickly and has no tendency to form curves. Its tractive force increases downstream, the gravel is carried along, the stream bed deepens. If, however, the water extraction takes place through the bottom outlet—i.e., at depth—then the temperature difference between the outflowing water of about 4° C and the air of, e.g., 35° C can become very large. Then the turbulence of the water becomes significant, curve formation increases, bank erosion occurs, furthermore gravel deposition at the inner curves and scouring at the outer curves. With a half-filled reservoir, a reduction of these phenomena takes place and the vortex formation decreases.

With the Schauberger construction method, the water flows off at a temperature of approximately 4° C and the described


---


- 98 -

processes also occur. However, it is possible, when using the diversion pipes, to select the temperature of the outflowing water and to adapt it to the existing air temperatures in such a way that the turbulence and the undesirable curve formation are reduced.

Regarding the mouth of the hot bubbling springs into the Tepl, the following should be noted. While water at 10° C or 20° C has a specific weight of 0.9997 or 0.9982, that at 70° C has a specific weight of 0.9778; thus the water of the bubbling springs is lighter by 0.022 or 0.020 than that of the Tepl. Therefore, the average river gradient falls behind the effect of the different specific weights of Tepl water and bubbling water, and so a part of the bubbling water first flows upstream instead of downstream. This reduces the outflow velocity significantly compared to what the usual theories would predict, whereby the discharge of the water quantities is delayed and their height during floods is correspondingly increased.

Furthermore, it should be mentioned that in case of significant water influx, a part of the water is to flow off through a pipe of larger diameter, which branches off from the reservoir at the left height and leads into the Eger. The pipe is to be provided with a twist, because it has been shown that such a twist greatly increases the velocity.

The Tepl flows into the Eger at approximately 1.6 km distance from the bubbling springs. At present, the Tepl water at its mouth into the Eger is warmer than the Eger water. It therefore rises here, so that it no longer exerts any effect on the gravel that remains at the bottom and the Tepl water backs up. Through the construction of the dam wall and the upper twisted discharge pipe according to Schauberger, the Tepl water will mostly be heavier than the Eger water, and then the gravel deposition with its damming influence on the Tepl will be reduced; this will have a favorable effect on the flood danger under which Karlsbad suffers.

From what has been said, the superiority of the Schauberger design over the designs common today emerges, which superiority is expressed particularly under the extraordinary conditions of Karlsbad. It is in any case more correct to build a dam with regulable outflow conditions whose strength increases with time, than to build a dam with non-regulable outflow conditions whose structural condition steadily deteriorates, all the more so as the possibility of regulating the outflow conditions makes a reduction of the necessary storage volume and thereby a reduction of the height of the dam

- 99 -

is conditional.

Finally, it should be mentioned that Mr. Schauberger has already built a larger number (14) of dams and that these have proven themselves. I have personally inspected some of his structures and can declare that the new ideas of Mr. Schauberger have fully achieved their intended effect.

The Principle of the Small Waterways (t14)

By small waterways one is to understand approximately 1-meter-wide and 50-cm-high channels built in natural stone, adapted to the terrain and the existing gradient conditions, particularly profiled, in which floating goods that are specifically heavier than ordinary water — e.g. approximately 1300 kg heavy, 1-linear-meter beech logs — float rapidly and cheaply, without quality or quantity losses, to the place of consumption. There they are automatically sorted by length, thickness, and quality, discharged onto the conveyor belt, and deposited from top to bottom.

The construction of such small water channels is cheaper than all technical means of transport, forest cableways, or roads, etc., and the transport costs are reduced by at least 90% compared to the aforementioned means of transport.

The necessary minimum water quantity is under 3% approximately 600 liters/sec. and decreases with increasing gradient down to any necessary moistening quantities in steep slides of wood or sliding slides, in which the timber is fed on decommissioned forest railway tracks from side valleys directly into the main channel.


---

And these metaphysical super-forces — by no means supernatural forces — arise through the nature-correct fermentation of earthly fat-substance remains, which, according to the view of today's church, decay to dust and ash. Therefore, under such preconditions, no one can imagine an origin of these super-forces and attributes them to a supernatural influence.

If one triggers this higher-quality energy-substance build-up — the higher-quality fermentation process — through higher-quality movement forms by machine, then one can produce these seemingly supernatural reactive forces on a running machine belt in the most original effective strength almost cost-free, and thereby performs

the most nature-correct divine service, because one thus supports the will of the constructive work of creation better than through purposeless praying accompanied by the use of reversed-sense movement processes.


---


For thus, through higher-quality movement processes, reactive movement effects arise in the form of physical temperature influences, which reactivate — i.e., additionally amplify — the initiating movement process, whereby, as a result of a centripetal orbital increase, the f.e. longitudinal-axis velocity and the decomposition forces of the dividing reduction (current-thread thinning) grow — i.e., from more raw-material sources, the more constructive and upward-driving current springs forth, the more ash-like the raw-material through-flow masses are moved, and thus a multiple of bacteriophage energy-substance sources are sluiced through, from which — as mentioned above — the all-lifting levitation current springs forth, and then, with primordial resurrection power, moves toward the counter-flowing fertilization substance — the higher-quality waste substance purified through a long development path. It marries itself with this reactive substance, i.e., binds, consumes, and digests it, after which the re-elevated constructive product is born anew, and with constantly growing force and constant expulsion of watery ballast substances — which marry with surrounding differential substances and produce juvenile air — increases the eruptive upward-pressure, which, acting in the same axial direction, supports the f.e. reactive upward-suction.

This relatively highest-value — implosion-suction and eruptive-pressure acting on a common resurrection axis — can be built up almost cost-free in specially constructed movement forms, which are specially alloyed for the purpose of incorporating catalytic stimulant-substance current influences. And with this, the reversed-sense decomposing and backward-substance of metaphysical state-type producing technology — hydraulics and dynamics — the cause of this appalling world-economic decay, is done away with.

For the more nature-correct movement machines run, the more and qualitatively better reactive currents arise, and the more levitation-substance forces are available from the nature-correctly densified — energy-substance-pregnant — water, and that is the specifically densified blood of the Earth, which arises when one merely moves ordinary water — radially-axially in single-winding spiral space curves, breaks down the sluiced-through water masses into as many water threads as possible, and reduces, centripetalizes them — after which, from each in-wound current thread, the relatively highest-value constructive and upward-driving substance current springs forth, for the purpose of equalization with higher-quality cosmic

counter-influences.

And thus, in place of the development-most-dangerous cavitation current produced in Kaplan turbines, there arises the all-lifting levitation current, known to the ancient cultured peoples and their high priests, which harbors within itself the secret of growth, which will be clearly and unambiguously shown for the first time in future publications. The legendary harvest yields were thus no miracle. Conversely, one must marvel that, through the influences caused by today's teachers of mankind, anything grows at all.

Viktor Schauberger, 1947
