PREFACE


---


It is undoubtedly a sign of healthy, non-degenerate humanity when our youth resists with all their strength the symptoms of decay already appearing everywhere and refuses to continue stupidly trudging along the army road that has led us all into a cultural and economic dead end.
Let others philosophize, pointing to their deductively acquired knowledge in empty phrases, let them, misled or deliberately, spew venom and bile over such developments — our youth is ultimately right not to trust such a capability of their forebears, which yields such fruits, and to refuse to continue down the wrong path that our intellectual leaders have led us into misery.
But rebellion alone accomplishes nothing yet. The counter-efforts of our youth will only bear practical success once the causes are recognized and the errors are found that were committed by us and by earlier generations, and that have indeed plunged a world into misfortune.
But even finding the causes of the evil is only a very small step, because the army of so-called experts will stand in the way of the systematic correction of the errors committed, since they represent the previous direction and must continue to endorse it, because they live from it and want to be provided for until their blessed end.
But even this obstacle would be easy to bridge if the errors made could at least be localized to a specific branch of the economy.
Upon thorough examination and investigation of the serious errors committed for centuries, however, there is shown such an enormous spread and ramification of the disease caused by incorrect advice and false working methods, and such grave cultural, technical, and economic failures, that no branch of the economy remains untouched by it and no expert, wherever he may work, can extricate himself from complicity, even if partly unconsciously.
With the clarification that under the given circumstances almost every expert is threatened in his existential security, it would also be completely pointless to expect support from these circles, and one must reckon from the outset with a massive opposition, which is however necessary and will contribute much to clarification. But even this obstacle must by no means be daunting, because it is not about the existence of individuals, but, as can be clearly seen from the following, about the being or non-being of misguided humanity, about everything.
And for this reason it becomes a sacred duty for people who recognize the full scope of the errors committed to stand steadfastly and even at the expense of their own advantage for the final correction of the many errors. The same duty arises for all those to whom their inner feeling only allows the possibility of error to appear given.
The most effective means is to make the broad public aware of the great dangers arising from incorrect advice and the pointlessness of the


---


- 174 -
pursuit of the previous goals. Poor and rich, high and low must be seized by doubt and justified mistrust, which, drawing ever wider circles, ultimately produces the inner feeling of resistance in the broad masses, and which, once awakened, must not come to rest until it has become a judgment of the people and thus of God, and then begins to work and help from within.
The purpose of the following exposition is to awaken this inner feeling of resistance in the broad masses.
If it succeeds in rousing the mistrust slumbering in every human being and the instinctive presentiment of the enormous danger threatening here, then neither questions of prestige nor the existential worries of the salaried officials — justifiably trembling for their bread — will be able to constitute a significant obstacle to the final remediation of human self-destruction.
The experts and scientists severely criticized in the following may objectively examine everything and refute the many accusations brought forward below.
But if our water experts cannot refute what is said below in its essential meaning, then the broad public should no longer pass by the danger increasing with every day as indifferently as before, but should take to heart the following exposition, which, if it is irrefutable, can at least not be incorrect.
Whether it is well-intentioned, the peasants already struggling so hard for their soil everywhere will be best able to judge. But also all those who, forced by their profession, must earn their bread in the great cities, should seriously consider what must happen when, in addition to the bread becoming ever scarcer, ever more expensive, and ever worse in quality, the water also disappears.
But this danger becomes even more terrible because the remaining remnants of water become an inexhaustible source of the most dreadful disease, cancer, which is spreading ever further and against which, when this disease has progressed too far, there is presently no truly effective help.
Therefore, may all those people who are not in the fortunate position of drawing the cooling drink directly from a healthy spring consider where it comes from, how it is conveyed, and by what artificial additives it is made palatable.
But those people who are forced to drink nothing but sterilized water year in and year out should finally consider how water, from which its natural ability to generate life has been violently taken away by purely chemical additives, must affect the organism.


---


- 175 -
Sterilized and physically destroyed water not only lawfully brings about physical decay, but also causes mental symptoms of decay and thus a systematic degeneration of human beings and other living creatures. The same naturally applies to all other forms of vegetation and other prerequisites of all life in nature.
The reason why people confuse their cultural and economic decline with a temporary crisis and constantly strive in vain to master the ever-spreading impoverishment lies largely in the mental decay of humanity, which must lawfully follow — or precede — every physical decay.
Our science regards the primal organism "water," which forms the blood and influences the character, as a chemical compound and administers to millions of people a fluid prepared according to these viewpoints, which is anything but healthy water.
All efforts to move our science to acknowledge the serious errors it has made are pointless from the outset, since it would condemn itself with such a concession.
It must therefore necessarily remain with its current doctrines. But all other people who still possess sound common sense should categorically refuse to continue drinking water prepared in such a way, because with constant consumption of such drinking water they must degenerate into cancer-sick, mentally and physically decayed, that is, physically and morally inferior individuals.

The Achievements of the Twentieth Century

We have already become quite accustomed to the fact that today millions of people can no longer earn their daily bread through honest work and must procure their most basic necessities partly by digging through garbage heaps like animals, through begging, through theft, through fraud, or even through murder; that our children have no more trust in such working methods and regard study only as a senseless and pointless occupation, band together, and are ready to conquer with weapon in hand the place that is due to them in their homeland.
That under such conditions the hospitals and homeless shelters are overcrowded, the reform schools and insane asylums become popularly frequented institutions, suicides mount, no rationally thinking person any longer believes the empty promises of our leaders, and everyone has long since become clear that, if


---


- 176 -
things continue like this, the whole structure must collapse.
What is to become of a humanity that can no longer feed itself, that destroys its own foundations of life, that poisons its water and its soil, that cuts down its forests and thus robs itself of the most vital sources of energy?
What is to become of a humanity that regards everything only from the standpoint of immediate utility and has no understanding of the great interrelationships of nature, that with brutal force subjugates everything that does not submit to its will, and that thereby destroys precisely that which alone could guarantee its continued existence?
The answer is: this humanity must perish, and it will perish, unless it finds its way back to the understanding that water is not a chemical compound, but a living organism, the carrier of all life, the blood of the Earth.
This understanding must take hold in the broad masses, not in the ivory towers of science, for there it will never take root, because science has committed itself to a path from which it can no longer turn back without destroying itself.
Therefore, it is up to each individual to inform himself, to examine, and to judge for himself what is right and what is wrong. The following exposition is intended to serve this purpose. It does not claim to be complete or perfect, but it claims to point out the errors that have been made and to show the ways in which they can be corrected.
If the reader is willing to follow this exposition with an open mind, he will come to understand why our water is deteriorating, why our soils are becoming barren, why our forests are dying, and why our youth is rebelling against a world that can no longer offer it a future worthy of human beings.
He will also come to understand what must be done to halt the decline and to restore the natural order that alone can guarantee the continued existence of humanity.
This is the purpose of this book. It is not a scientific treatise, but a call to reason, a call to all those who still possess the capacity for independent thought, to free themselves from the spell of a science that has lost its way, and to return to the sources of knowledge that lie not in the intellect but in the observation of nature itself.


---


- 177 -

...no radical change takes place in the economic measures pursued so far, only a violent decimation of the masses of people who have become superfluous, a well-organized mass murder or, expressed more aesthetically, a modern war, can perhaps still cut this Gordian knot, is as comprehensible as it is true.

What is strange about the matter, however, is that both the instigators of the seemingly unavoidable clash of arms, anxious for their sinecures or their very existence, and our children, who are resolved to throw their futureless and therefore worthless lives into the breach, overlook the fact that this well-prepared sacrifice of other people's lives, or the voluntary surrender of one's own young life — in a word, the horrific self-destruction of the despairing masses of people by poison gas or other weapons — is no longer necessary at all.

For both sides overlook that these occurrences, which cannot possibly be brought into accord with a cultural development, are only quite natural concomitant phenomena of the intervention of a much higher power, one that has entirely different means at its disposal — means that work far more thoroughly, and above all far more incisively, than all the weapons of war devised by human brains put together, from which, if we are honest with ourselves just once, everyone hopes for a way out of this chaos.

It is the lawful order prevailing in nature and its balancing justice that will give this culture, erected by senseless human beings, the end it deserves, if humanity does not come to reason and to the insight that it has been badly advised and badly led by its spiritual leaders.

Civilized humanity has, despite its supposedly high technical culture, reached such an ethical low point that it is no longer able to recognize that this physical and moral decay is nothing other than a continuously ongoing collapse of culture — a collapse that, among other excesses, also leads the spiritually blinded masses of people to be deceived about the facts of their entrenched errors through the pretense of seemingly most humane measures of assistance.

People's most sacred possession, their freedom in subjective action, feeling and thought, is literally trampled underfoot by people who were never in a position to intervene in a truly helpful way. In this it is entirely irrelevant whether these leaders sail under this or that flag; everywhere dwells the same drive toward violation, because there as here the inner recognition of the true causes — and with it the last possibility of genuine assistance — is lacking.

To this comes the fact that our men in power, unable to form a judgment of their own, must constantly rely on the advice of the so-called experts, who, having themselves become victims of the general miseducation, can no longer recognize either that


---


- 178 -

...it is precisely their advice and the actions resulting from it that must inevitably transform this Earth, which could after all be a paradise, into a hell.

Unfortunately, terrible catastrophes or scandalous revelations must always come first before people realize that it is only their own errors that lead them into misery. These errors can then be made good again only with such difficulty because they are mostly committed by authorities who will not judge themselves, but would rather let millions of their fellow human beings perish before they would admit their mistakes for the sake of protecting their own interests.

It may be a very thankless task to alert the public to coming dangers that it does not see or does not wish to see; it may perhaps even be wasted effort to show it the terrible writing on the wall that hovers over it — the attempt shall at least be made. At the very least, the people perishing helplessly in the hospitals and our children shall know that they have become the victims of the culture of the past and of the present culture that grew out of it.

If we wish to influence our own life — which is constantly threatened by the awakening of other life — in a desired direction and protect it from decay, then we must either let nature take its course, or, if we insist on intervening, first of all come to clarity about the simplest principles of life.

It shall not be the task of these generally held explanations to discuss here in greater detail the many pointers that the worldviews, thousands of years old, offer us. Where they are briefly referred to, this is done only because they often carry very deep meaning, and their correct understanding is necessary for grasping the whole.

Naturally, much will also have to fall away here, because once people recognize the wonderful lawfulness and the unity that prevails in nature, they will gain in ethics and thereby renounce outward appearances.

Just as every individual living creature is ultimately a bridge for the construction of the whole, so the various religions and worldviews represent only spiritual bridges which, in their often primitive form of construction, must give way to better ones once the ethical ascent of humanity has overtaken them.

The mightiest bridge for the development of all being is undoubtedly water as an entity.

Only a deep study by intuitively gifted people can fathom the innermost essence of the life-substance "water." Only with the tireless investigation of the primordial substance "water" will it be possible to show the ways that lead humanity — declining in spirit and in body — upward again.

- 179 -

The deep-sea water


---


If our scholars were to examine deep-sea water more closely, they would recognize that the air absorbed by the water at great depth deviates from the air contained in pronounced surface water both qualitatively and quantitatively, in essential ways.

To this fact must also be attributed the strange phenomenon that deep-sea fish are luminous, or are even capable of delivering electric shocks.

The air absorbed by deep-sea water fundamentally exhibits a composition similar to what we can still find in certain high mountain springs. Above all, it is the high content of physically dissolved carbons and the lack of oxygen, together with protection from the influence of light, that lend this water its peculiar character.

Where the seawater at greater depths receives no gases either through diffusion or through convection — and the oxygen is additionally consumed by living organisms — the oxygen in the seawater can even be locally absent entirely, and such seawater can therefore even be fresh.

From the fact that the carbonic acid content of the atmosphere above the sea is lower than above the land, the conclusion can be drawn that the ocean surfaces also absorb carbonic acid directly. (See Dr. M. P. Rudzki, "Physics of the Earth.")

The creatures of the deep sea stand out, in comparison with their related representatives in shallow water, by their size, by their strangely constructed eyes, by their varying bodily firmness, and often by a particularly original form of build.

The outer surroundings stamp upon each individual the seal of its distinctiveness, and so we find everywhere certain contradictions that can be explained only if we understand the nature of the water in which these organisms live.

One would think that an organism living in the deep sea, on account of the mass of water pressing upon it, would have to display an correspondingly strongly built body. But in contrast to the fish found at the surf zones, with their robust skeletal forms and strong muscles, deep-sea fish have extremely delicate, paper-thin, nearly weightless bone structures. It is to this circumstance that people also attribute the fact that these animals tear apart when brought up. But this purely mechanical explanation is also a grave error.

Just as organisms hauled up from the deep sea literally explode, so it is also with what is brought up from such depths


---


- 180 -

...water raised from such depths, which — when corresponding quantities of oxygen and highly organized carbon substances, such as oil, are added — becomes warm relatively quickly, or, when sealed, bursts its container.

Many a natural event playing out in the depths of the oceans would effortlessly find its explanation if the experts were acquainted with the inner nature and character of deep-sea water. This applies in particular also to the phenomenon of ebb and flood, the true cause of whose origin will be described in a later chapter.

In the same way, our energy engineers would abandon generating electrical energy in the manner customary today if they knew that it can be obtained by the most simple of apparatus directly from the deep sea.

But these instruments and apparatuses that lift the world off its hinges would quickly wander into the museums as obsolete, because man does not need to go that far at all in order to obtain light, warmth, and other forms of energy in any desired quantity, effortlessly and almost costlessly.

The quantitative and qualitative decline of water

For roughly a decade now, the groundwater in many regions has been sinking so rapidly that one can count on one's fingers when people will be forced to abandon their settlements and dwellings at higher elevations, because they will no longer be able to procure the necessary water — or only at great cost.

With the sinking of the groundwater table, the springs run dry, the watercourses parch, and the soil that is to give us our daily bread dies of thirst. In other places again, water rises out of the earth, the rivers burst their banks, and the lands turn to swamp.

But in addition to these in themselves already frightening quantitative shifts in the water balance of, on, and above the Earth, there comes a far greater danger — the qualitative decay of the already scanty remnants of water — whereby both the drinking water and the wash and bath water are already directly harmful to health.

How far this latter danger has already progressed is shown clearly by a publication in the Daily Mail of 23 August 1933 concerning the water investigations of London's tub- and swimming-baths prompted by the press. In these investigations, more than a million bacteria per cubic centimeter were detected in the water of the public bathing establishments. This, then, at sites where thousands of people seek recreation, but instead expose themselves to severe

- 181 -

infectious diseases.

If this danger already exists in the establishments under constant supervision, then it must be estimated as still greater where such controls are lacking.

Besides this fact, the investigations yielded a further surprise, namely the finding that wherever an attempt was made to forestall this danger through corresponding chlorination, dangerous inflammations of the eyes and nasal mucous membranes appeared in the bathers.


---


The Sterilization of Water

Among the most difficult tasks in the treatment and processing of
drinking water is the sterilization of surface water, or immature
groundwater, which is in itself harmful to health and unsuitable for
drinking purposes. This water is generally drawn from rivers, lakes and
reservoir ponds or, where these are absent, raised from deep wells by
pressure or suction pumps and made drinkable through chemical
additives.
The bad taste of mechanically filtered water, not freed of
microorganic substances, and artificially spoiled by the addition of
chlorine, by irradiation, or by other sterilizing additives, is probably
known to everyone who is forced to live in cities where the water is
germ-free thanks to chemical additives or other measures.
But the consequential effects arising from this, which are to be briefly
explained below, are not known.
If even the technician, already conditioned by his schooling, cannot
become aware of the terrible consequences that must arise from the
continued consumption of sterilized drinking water, then at least the
physicians cannot be spared the reproach that they fail to recognize the
causes of the decay occurring everywhere. This weighs all the more
heavily since it is precisely they who are called upon to continually
observe and study the organic structure of the body and its stages of
development.
Even if today's physician, before beginning his actual studies, must
acquire certain technical preliminary knowledge and various chemical and
physical basic concepts — which, however, often rob him of the
connection to reality — then at least the physician engaged in practical
life should once become clear about how the constant consumption of
merely sterilized water must affect the human body, and whether this kind
of sterilization may be applied permanently at all.


---


- 182 -
Especially those physicians who often devote their entire lives to the
study of cancer diseases, and are also financially well supported for this
purpose, ought to pose themselves the question of how this bacterial life
can arise in the human body, or indeed in any organically constructed
body at all. In any case it is not enough merely to register the existing
facts and to strive to destroy unwanted life that has already come into
being.
The very example that the development of bacterial life is favored when
water stands for a long time or flows slowly in the sun, or in poorly
sealed and illuminated wells, should show that certain connections exist
here which must be researched first and foremost in order to halt the
danger of disease associated with them.
If this path has still never been taken, the reason lies in the fact that
even our practicing physicians have largely lost the connection with
nature.
All attempts to disinfect water are, in the end, aimed at creating
unfavorable or impossible living conditions for the bacterial life that
forms in water under certain preconditions, in order to destroy it
thereby.
Once the water has been made "hygienically impeccable" in this way, one
is usually fully satisfied and believes that enough has thereby been
done. But no one considers that with the constant consumption of
sterilized water, sterilized milk, or other sterilized foodstuffs, man —
apart from other dangers connected with it, such as those posed by the
microorganic substances not removed by today's sterilization, which can
with full justification be called bacterial candidates — is also denied
certain material energies, whereby a diminishment of the mental and
sexual potency of man can occur, and in the weakened body an increase
of the very danger being fought must set in.

The Chlorination of Water and Its Consequences

In the supply of drinking and service water to cities and settlements,
which is becoming ever more difficult today, not only is its content of
solid substances paid too little attention, but the physical processes in
the water and its character are completely neglected.
As a rule one is content to obtain germ-free, clear and pure water.
There is hardly a larger city where the water is not disinfected by the
addition of


---


- 183 -
chlorine, by irradiation with the quartz lamp, or by the addition of
silver, and so on — that is, sterilized.
Through all these processes an oxygen in the status nascendi, or an
allotropic form of ordinary oxygen, is produced in the water, whereby
every living creature must perish. If water treated in this way is drunk
continually, the same processes must also play out in our body that we
desire in the sterilization process of the water.
Since it is not enough merely to point to this fact in general terms, the
processes that take place in a body that constantly absorbs chlorinated
water, or water treated according to the other water-purification
procedures customary today, shall be described in the following.
The devastating consequences that the constant consumption of such
water — in which only the above viewpoints are observed — can bring
in its train is perhaps best shown by the ever-widening spread of the
various disease phenomena that we summarize today under the name of
cancer. (In the year 1920, 2,400 people died of cancer in Vienna; in
1926 there were 3,700 fatal cancer cases; in 1931, 4,900 human lives
fell victim to this dreadful affliction. In the figures cited, the spread
of the disease stands clearly before our eyes.)
This terrible plague, which despite all previous efforts and arts of our
medical research institutes can be neither recognized nor effectively
combated, and whose spread claims ever more victims, is above all a
consequential effect of unhealthy or badly managed water, which is not
only involved in the construction of all foodstuffs and of our blood,
but also determines the quality of the air mixture immediately
surrounding the inner organisms.
A glance at the statistical tables shows us clearly that cancer is most
widespread in those regions where no good high-spring water is
available.
But even where the water at the spring is good and healthy, it becomes
bad again during transport through pipelines often hundreds of
kilometers long, so that the map of cancer spread indicates the length
of the supply line through which the drinking and service water flows to
the place of consumption.
Naturally it will immediately be replied that the water is subjected to
every conceivable examination and that its respective content of
dissolved and absorbed substances is kept precisely on record.
If we therefore drink merely sterilized water, we must also accept the
effects that must thereby occur. It is therefore also pointless to brace
ourselves against the physical and mental decay that is necessarily
connected with it.
But if we do not wish to go to ruin voluntarily, slowly in mind and
body,

- 184 -
then we must seek other ways and strive not to drive out the devil in
today's drinking water by means of Beelzebub.

The Substance Water


---


The right paths lead us of themselves back to nature, and thus to the
source of life, to the healthy water that, lifted by inner forces,
springs from Mother Earth all the healthier the higher it rises, when it
is ripe — that is, when it has its correct physical composition — and
must leave the earth.
In its absorbed air content, such water shows about 96 percent
gaseous, physically dissolved carbon substances, so that the "psyche,"
that is, the character of the water, must be described as very
elevated.
There are springs that exhibit such a high content of carbonic acid
(this expression is, however, incorrect) that small animals which inhale
the vapor precipitated in the surrounding atmosphere fall down dead
almost instantly. (The Dog Spring in Naples)
But such water also becomes dangerous for people when it is drawn
directly from the spring with the mouth and the rising gases are inhaled
along with it. The mountain dwellers call such springs "poisonous
waters." To this day one still finds springs that are shunned by people
and fenced off from the grazing livestock, because these springs, as
folk speech says, contain the so-called water-worm which, drunk along
with the water, brings death beyond rescue within a few days.
If such water is placed in the air in a metal vessel, an
disproportionately rapid warming of this water and a slight fizzing at
the surface become apparent. This phenomenon is incidentally also
sometimes found when drilling wells. Where these processes occur, the
tapped water level sinks rapidly and the well soon runs dry.
If such water is exposed to the air, a quick awakening of abundant
bacterial life can be observed after only a short time — life which is
organized at an ever lower level the warmer the water becomes.
If one pours warmed rainwater into such water, adds a few drops of oil
to it and seals the whole thing off, the contents of the vessel explode
after a short time.
What has taken place here?
The negative atmosphere contained in the high-quality spring water —
its psyche — oxidizes, that is, it equalizes with the strongly
oxygen-containing, warm, and therefore predominantly positively charged
air, and shatters the vessel when an obstacle is placed in the path of
this equalization


---


- 185 -
is placed, and a lower-organized carbon substance is present, such as
oil, for example, represents.
If water of this kind is drunk quickly while the body is heated, the same
phenomenon naturally occurs in that body. The person in question
feels a stabbing pain in the lungs and is a corpse within a few
days. The mountain dwellers call these rapidly appearing decay
phenomena the galloping lung consumption. If these
cases, formerly frequent, no longer occur so often today, the
reason lies only in the fact that water of such a high grade has by now become
very rare.
Through the equalization processes described above, in which the substance
"water" offers the resistance absolutely necessary for them,
energies are released or bound.
Essential factors here are the materially different
composition of the atmosphere and the influence of light, conditioned by
the season, acting differently according to the position of the sun.
The longer the water is exposed to the influence of light and comes
into contact with the air through flowing or mechanical movement (stirring etc.),
the more it will give up its original earth-sphere,
absorb atmosphere, and become warm and stale.
The more immaturely the water emerges from seepage springs etc. or is taken
from the earth, and the smaller the originally existing
contrasts were, the weaker the equalization processes become, the
more inferior the energy products become, and the more lowly
organized are the microorganisms that can form under these
conditions. The mental and physical decay of all
organisms set in operation by inferior water is the
necessary consequence.
If, on the one hand, the vital oxidation processes can no longer proceed
in their corresponding high-quality form in a water that has given up the
sphere it brought from the earth through excessive aeration or excessive
sun exposure, then, on the other hand, one can no longer
expect any high-quality properties or processes from a water that could not
preserve its inner maturity or has lost its original properties of maturity.
But then one should not
be surprised, either, if in such a water
various lowly organized creatures arise that eventually
become dangerous even for the life of human beings.
Here now begins the work of the scientists and experts,
whose one-sided way of thinking is to be described in the following.
If the supply of oxygen was, on the one hand, necessary for the origin and
development of living creatures, then, on the other hand, excessive
oxygen enrichment or excessive supply of lower-organized
oxygen must in turn endanger their existence again.
We find something similar in this respect in ourselves. If we want


---


- 186 -
to visit the sphere of oxygen, the stratosphere, we must
take along oxygen of the quality found in our own
sphere. The same applies to the provisioning with
fresh water supplies on sea voyages.
If we inject excessive quantities of oxygen into the water, neither
a bacterium nor a human being can endure it in the long run. The former,
having no other possibility of breathing, must perish immediately; the human being,
who after all can still gasp healthy air on the side, perishes over the course
of time.
Since the organic transformation processes in the body depend on a specific
composition of the basic substances present in the water — the carbon
and oxygen groups — a qualitatively high-grade
build-up of vegetation, no matter where it takes place, depends on
a specific ratio in which the quantities and qualities
of these substances are contained in the fundamental building substance "water."
These quantities and qualities produce in the organism that absorbs them
through breathing, through the consumption of food, or through the direct
intake of water, by way of the mutual
oxidation processes, a specific inner temperature proper
to the respective organism.
A specific inner temperature produces a specific body form, and
this in turn a specific immaterial energy, which confronts us in a more
or less highly organized form as character. Hence
the old proverb "mens sana in corpore sano" (a healthy mind in
a healthy body).
If the basic substances are altered in their composition, then
not only the metabolism underlying the further build-up of the body
must necessarily change, but, together with it, also the mental
structure and its further development.
Briefly summarized: healthy air, healthy food, and healthy
water produce not only a healthy body, but also good
traits of character.

The consequences of the water purification procedures used hitherto

Through the current water purification procedures under the influence of light,
the oxygen content is essentially altered both quantitatively and
qualitatively. The immediate consequence is metabolic disorders and, with them,
accumulations of oxygen that the water supersaturated with this substance
cannot process in the body.
As the first symptoms of illness, owing to the appearance of
pressure phenomena, swellings and tumor-like enlargements
of the tissues show up, particularly clearly in the so-called shade-tolerant timber species


---


- 187 -
become visible, which were exposed to the direct action of the sun in warm and
therefore strongly oxygen-containing
soils.
The excess of oxygen establishing itself in the enlarged cells
leads to strong acid formations and, as a further consequence, to inflammations.
These inflammations in turn call forth higher temperatures, the fever,
whereby the oxygen becomes ever more aggressive and, for lack of
other carbon substances, finally even enters into equalization
with the tissue substances. The consequence of this is the origin
of lowly organized microbes, which, given suitable conditions,
begin their vital activity and, in the absence of other nourishment, then
literally devour the macro-organism, the body. The pathogen is thus
the indirect product of incorrect equalization processes.
Science calls this meal cancer.
The only defensive means available to it so far are the
knife or radiation. But if our physicians were clear
about why the cancerous tumors literally swell up when the body is opened,
or if they understood the phenomena of combustion that
occur under excessive irradiation, they would no longer use these aids.
It is a remarkable fact that aqua destillata greedily snatches gaseous
substances from the surrounding air, so that it soon
reveals the smell of the substances surrounding it. Use is made
of the fact that such sterilized water withdraws solid and gaseous carbon
substances from its surroundings also in medicine, in the
de-sludging (chelation) of human blood. The consumption of
such water can bring about only a short-lasting improvement
in the general condition and, at best, act only as a whip-like stimulus.
In the end, however, such water must have a destructive
effect on the organism, since in the end it withdraws from it carbon substances
which in this case are not surplus slag but
highly necessary building substances.
Thus the effect of the complete sterilization of the water can only be a
very brief one, since in any case the surrounding medium, the body,
is deprived of highly necessary substances, which then form the breeding ground
for a new micro-life.
If sterilization is even attempted to be achieved by chlorination,
then after the germ-killing activity of the aggressive
oxygen, some of it will still remain contained, which, upon meeting
the corresponding carbon particles, now all the more gives rise
to the formation of micro-life.
The carbon substances in the water can be regarded as negative, the oxygens as
positive electrons, which, under the influence of
temperature, stand in an inversely lawful relation to one another.
If we take in good food, good air, and healthy — that is, mature —
water, then highly organized bacteria arise, which any possibly


---


- 188 -
arising lowly organized creatures devour. If we
take in bad basic substances — whether through inferior food or
through water poor in good carbon substances — then no
high-grade bacteria can arise, and the creatures built up from lowly organized
basic substances devour the body that was formerly
animated by high-grade bacteria.
Of decisive importance is the correct or incorrect composition of the blood,
conditioned by these transformation processes, and the energies
arising within it.
The decision whether we breed beasts of prey or
domestic animals in our own body thus lies entirely in our hands, or rather in
the hands and brains of the experts in the fields of
agriculture, forestry, and water management.
In nature a certain uniformity prevails, and therefore these
symptoms, as the other forms of vegetation also show us, appear
everywhere alike.
Mistakes that have been made must therefore have effects everywhere and hence
must also trigger an overall decay.
The inner substance content of the water is, however, also decisive for the level
of the groundwater table.
As the vertically rising mountain springs show us, in mature
water the inner energies become so great that they are able to overcome
the weight of the water mass itself, provided the conduit vessels
have a correct structure and cross-sectional forms that are not too large.
The experimental proof of this is, as the photographic
image in the first part shows, easy to produce.
The sinking of the groundwater table is above all a consequence of
metabolic disorders in the groundwater. Conforming to this phenomenon
is the disturbance of the blood circulation in our body and, naturally, also
the movement of sap in the plants.

On micro-life

In what follows, a few examples of previously unknown
developmental possibilities of bacteria are to give instructive insights.
In earlier times, floors were made of soft wood material,
such as spruce or fir. These floors were washed frequently,
yet despite the great quantity of water that penetrated
the fill material located beneath the floor, they lasted
for decades. With the development of domestic culture, people moved on to the
hard parquet floors, which, as is well known, are laid upon the subfloor
made of softwood. If such
parquet floors are washed, microorganisms occasionally form, which


---


- 189 -

then appear in such numbers that these floors decay within a few years.

Our experts take the view, in such cases, that the wood used was infected.

The true state of affairs is, however, essentially different.

The noble hardwood is, in its structure, of higher quality than the
lower-organized softwood. Fine wood possesses qualitatively superior
protein substances, which, given a normal supply of oxygen, are converted
only slowly.

If appropriate gaps remain between the subfloor and the parquet floor,
so that no sealed intermediate layer can form between the two
materially different wooden floors, then these floors will, given
corresponding wood quality, last for decades. If, however, the upper
floor becomes moistened, and the swelling of the wood causes the gaps
to close, then a damp, warm layer forms between the two floors, which,
given faulty insulation, receives its supply of air and oxygen from
the groundwater rising in the masonry, untouched by the sun.

The concentrated oxygen rising with the groundwater that never sees
the sun will expand in the damp, warm layer described above, and
thereby become aggressive. This oxygen — already relatively highly
organized and rendered aggressive by the warming — first enters into
equilibrium with the lower-organized protein substances of the
subfloor. The energies arising in these metabolic processes give rise
to the development of certain microorganisms, which, at the ambient
temperatures that suit them, take up their vital activity and eat
away at the parquet floor from the bottom to the top.

The different kinds of nourishment and the differently constituted
microclimate now produce different species of this minute life, which
in the end, after the decay of its point of origin, also infects the
wider surroundings. That ailing trees standing in the forest are then
also attacked — especially the shade-tolerant tree species that modern
forestry has brought into the light, and which therefore exhibit
very oxygen-rich saps as well as a loose structure — goes without
saying. These phenomena, however, are only secondary and subordinate
after-effects of the clear-cutting forestry practised for about
100 years. The severe damage primarily arising from it will be
discussed in a later chapter, "Forestry."

As long as water was regarded as lifeless matter, and the internal
metabolic processes in water were left unexamined, it was also
impossible to come to closer grips with the question of the origin
of the world of microbes with all its conditions.

It is always the water — that is, the constant process of
transformation taking place within it — that generates a particular
life, which then,


---


- 190 -

no matter whether useful or harmful to human beings, ultimately
serves the building-up of the whole.

A further instructive example is offered to us by the question of the
living conditions of the so-called cave salamander (olm).

If we examine the water occurring in subterranean lakes, sealed off
from every influence of light, we find within it a most peculiar
atmosphere and no world of microbes. Apart from the olms, which often
occur in great numbers in these waters, there are no living creatures
there.

What, then, does the olm live on?

The strongly concentrated oxygen content of such water needs only a
slight warming and an increase in its aggressiveness in order to
convert the highly organized carbon substances present there into
still higher-quality ones, which the olm then takes up together with
the atmosphere contained in this water.

Through the animal's respiratory process and its body heat, lively
phenomena of oxidation occur, and with them an increased development
of heat, which suffice to transform the aforementioned highly
organized carbon substances, within the animal's body, into precisely
that nourishment which the olm requires for the maintenance of its
life.

If the olm is brought into the light, and thereby into a
corresponding sphere of oxygen, the surface of its body begins to
change color, and the body dies.

If, for example, one places the olm — without bringing it into the
light — at the very spot where it was caught, into a vessel, and pours
warmed rainwater into it, the same phenomena appear as described
above.

Once again we find the same picture that also explains to us, for
example, the mountain trout standing motionless in rushing water.
This particularly interesting phenomenon is to be discussed in the
chapter "Energy Economy."

The examples described above, however, would still not suffice to
elucidate the nature of the fact of primordial generation (generatio
aequivoca), accepted in the Middle Ages yet rejected in modern
times.

A simple but instructive example brings us still closer:

Those places where the water, often shimmering quite dark, streams
out of subterranean mountain lakes are, not without reason, the
spawning grounds of fish. If we examine this water at the boundary of
light — that is, at the point where it is struck by the incoming
light — a peculiar change shows itself in the substances contained in
such water, and the beginning of bacterial life. The nearer we come
to the region sealed off from light, the more highly organized is the
bacterial life in the water. The longer the water flows in the light,
the more lowly organized it becomes.

If we observe the fish life present there, the same thing shows itself


---


- 191 -

picture. The nearer the fish has stood to the spring, the more
delicious it is. Every fisherman knows that the strong stationary
trout living near the spring scorns every bait. A further peculiarity
is that these fish can live for months in grottoes, into which they
migrate as the water sinks during the hot summer months.

The way these animals feed — living half in daylight, half
underground — is essentially different from that of the fish living in
the lower reaches of the rivers, and similar to the way of life of
the olms. The eating of these almost blind fish leads to high sexual
potency, a fact well known to high-mountain hunters.

A very interesting phenomenon also shows itself in the origin of
mealworms.

If one places a vessel containing only flour in a dry, warm spot,
only a few worms form, or none at all. To obtain larger quantities and
better qualities of worms, one puts into the flour an old woollen rag
or a bone and seals the pot. The reason that the desired effect now
occurs lies in the presence of the added third group of carbon
substances — the woollen rag or the bone — which derive from a more
highly organized vegetational group than the flour, for example.

Incidentally, a few other interesting experimental arrangements may be
sketched here.

If we pour onto a moist layer of gelatine a diluted solution of
potassium chromate, or of iron or copper sulphate, beautiful
liquefaction figures arise, which under the magnifying glass display
a strongly ramified, delicate system.

If one takes river water to prepare the gel and sets up the
experimental arrangement in the boundary zone between a positive and
a negative temperature gradient, then after some time various fungi,
algae, mosses, etc. can be detected under the microscope. If, on the
other hand, fresh seawater is used instead of fresh water, then a
different fauna and flora of this microbe world develops,
characterized by more worm-like and sinuous organisms. This microbe
world, once the corresponding preconditions are created, behaves just
like its brothers and sisters in the macro-world: it devours
everything around it, stands in mutual struggle for life, expels the
unusable again, and multiplies at an uncanny speed.

Particularly clear experimental results can be achieved when, for the
sake of maintaining a proper temperature gradient — in this case an
artificially created one — the experimental arrangement is carried
out in a well-sealed glass vessel, insulated outwardly against the
outflow of energy.

It always holds true that, alongside a corresponding atmosphere, for
the formation of the desired microorganisms or worms the presence of
a


---


- 192 -

third, more highly organized substance is necessary, in order to
trigger the energies and to create conditions which we can, for
example, establish in the presence of a drop of oil in water of
corresponding composition.

Whether the origin of these microorganisms comes about through their
own bodily energy or through the action of a suitable, artificially
created temperature gradient is immaterial.

The main thing, in both cases, is the accompanying climate, changing
in short periods, whereby in the boundary zone of the individual
climatic zones — that is, in the intersection of two mutually acting
temperature gradients — the energy that forms life becomes free. The
precondition for this, once again, is a proper ratio of the basic
substances — that is, of the oxygen and carbon groups contained in
the water — the corresponding sealing-off, and the fitting body-form,
in which the inner climate proper to the respective creature, and
therefore necessary for its life-activity, can form and be
maintained.

On this occasion reference should also be made to a natural
phenomenon which science has so far been unable to explain, but which
can be fathomed effortlessly if one observes the connections under
which these peculiar phenomena are brought about.

This is the worm-rain in Lapland, which occurs now and then in
spring. In it there rain down actual white worms about three
centimetres long. The explanation that the worms falling from the sky
under the blood-red light of the midnight sun had been seized by the
wind somewhere, gathered into a cloud of worms, and fall to earth by
the thousands at a particular spot, could not hold its ground.

A similarly peculiar phenomenon is the so-called season of rot
(Fäulniszeit), which occurs in Lapland toward the end of July. This
season of rot lasts about four weeks. No tree may be felled during
it, for after only a few days the mould fungus would appear in such
masses that all the work would be in vain. Even heavily salted
American bacon begins to show every colour. Any injury to the body is
extremely dangerous; the smallest wound festers and can only heal
once the season of rot is over. The same holds for animals, for here
too injuries are incurable during this time. Young animals born in
this time of rot are mostly crippled. After the season of rot begins
the great dying of the midges and other tormenting spirits.

A further proof that a particular, season-dependent climate, or
particular influences of light, favour the origin of a superabundance
of micro-life, are the epidemics that occur regularly under certain
preconditions, and that are known to be caused only by bacteria, and
that in truth represent the most effective self-protection of nature
when the organism "human being" intervenes nonsensically in the
living mechanism "nature."


---


- 193 -
That the intensity of sunlight, varying so differently across the individual
seasons, also plays a major role in growth is well known.
If, for example, one allows light to enter a room through specifically
colored windowpanes, the flies begin to perish; if one changes the color
of the windowpanes, they come back to life.
Nor is the decline of tuberculosis since that time when radio waves
have been oscillating through the ether any accident. That humans,
through the resulting excessive one-sided oxygen enrichment in the water
and in the air, became faster-living, more hectic, and by no means
cleverer, may well also find its explanation here.
The observations of earth rays and the cancerous decay phenomena
frequently associated with them are likewise attributable to locally
incorrect balancing processes in the interior of the Earth, badly affected
by displacements of the elemental groups, in which the groundwater
plays the mediator and they are therefore communicated to all of life
by way of the capillaries.
All these phenomena, so enigmatic to science, can be effortlessly
imitated or prevented when one understands the nature of the primal
substance of all life, the nature of water.

The Conveyance of Water

If we consider the water conduits of the ancient Romans, we see from
the excavated pieces that at the beginning of the founding of the cities,
efforts were made to deliver the necessary drinking water to the place of
consumption in wooden pipes and natural stone conduits.
Only later, as the water demand grew ever greater with the growth of
the cities, did people hit upon the unfortunate idea of conveying the
drinking and bathing water in metal channels.
With regard to the choice of material for the conduits, wherever wood
was not used, the coin metal thrown into springs for cultic reasons was
observed, and the kind that best resisted years of influence was chosen.
Depending on the nature of the water, some metals are literally
encrusted by it, while others are almost entirely dissolved.
When the water is delivered in long iron pipes, questionable
material transformation processes sometimes take place, which cannot
possibly be captured with our present instruments, but which are of
decisive significance for the character, or rather the psyche, of the water.
As is well known, electrolytic, that is to say energetic, processes take
part in the formation of rust, which, with a corresponding change in
temperature


---


- 194 -
and in the presence of oxygen, through the effect of released carbonic acid
etc., occur on the pipe wall. The carbonic acid released through mutual
thermal influences dissolves iron out of the conduit, forming ferrous
carbonates. If, as a result of excessive aeration of the water, a
corresponding proportion of oxygen is then added, the ferrous bicarbonate
is converted into iron hydroxide while electrolytic processes occur
simultaneously; this separates out of the water as iron ochre and causes
constrictions of the cross-section.
In this, it must be borne in mind that iron can form more than ten times
the quantity of moist iron rust.
Through these processes, a portion of the carbonic acid that was
contained in it as an essential part of the embodiment of the psyche of the
water has first of all been withdrawn from the water, and with it the psyche
of the water has been degraded..
The transformation processes occurring at certain temperatures, which
lead to the formation of iron ochre as the end product, have an already
artificially pre-treated iron as their starting product. As a result of the
smelting processes and the addition of various additives, this iron has
been stripped of almost every natural character, such as the ore still
resting in the depths of the Earth still possesses, with its capillaries.
When the solid components are deposited on the inner wall during the
formation of iron hydroxide, then with a negative temperature gradient,
transformation processes and subsequently re-transformation processes
also take place simultaneously, leading to the formation of a new,
inferior psyche, which to a certain extent appears bound to the iron ochre.
Thus the water, through its conveyance in iron pipes, has not only lost
its high-grade psyche, but has moreover absorbed a bad psyche into
itself.
A particular danger is formed by the tarring of the inner walls frequently
applied to iron water pipes to prevent rust formation. It is a fact known in
medicine that the hard-to-volatile hard coal distillation products give
rise to cancerous diseases in the body, which is why some water supply
institutions have prohibited the use of tarred pipes.
If, as frequently happens, water conveyed in this way is then still driven
through turbines, where it is physically shattered by the high rotational
speed of the guide vanes, and the water flowing out of the turbine is
perhaps also mixed with other water, then severe damage must
inevitably occur both in the organisms to which this water is supplied and
in the surrounding soil. This manner of treating the blood of the Earth
can be imagined roughly as if, in blood transfusions, some


---


- 195 -
drawn-off blood were first swirled about with a fizzy-mixer and then
randomly mixed with foreign blood before being injected into the body. A
person treated in such a way must become gravely ill and ultimately
insane.
The same must, after a longer time, also occur with the constant
consumption of water that has been treated in the manner described
above, because the blood is systematically destroyed. The physical and
moral decay of those people who are compelled always to drink such
water probably proves the correctness of the foregoing sufficiently. The
spread of venereal diseases, too, is attributable above all to the far
advanced state of weakness of the blood.
In what follows, it shall now be shown how water is to be treated and
conveyed. As a model of an ideal conveyance of water, the capillary can
be chosen, which shall be briefly described with regard to its material and
the functions connected with it.
If the aforementioned evils of the deterioration of the psyche of the
water are to be avoided, then one must choose above all a poor conductor
of heat as the pipe material, one that is organically correctly built. Good,
healthy wood is best suited for this. Artificial stone is almost as unsuitable
for the conveyance of noble drinking water as metal. The reason for this
lies in the fact mentioned before, that only materials left in their natural
state may be used for the conveyance of the blood of the Earth.
If the objection is now made that wood is unsuitable for the pipe network
of a large city, if only because of its low durability, it must be set against
this that good and properly treated wood can in this case even be more
resistant than iron.
In order to avoid, as far as possible, the destructive influences that
pipes laid in the ground experience, these pipes are, apart from special
treatment measures, to be surrounded with sandy, non-humus soil
material.
The poor thermal conductivity of the wooden walls prevents
unfavorable influences on the inner metabolic processes in the water,
whereby the cleavages occurring with a negative temperature gradient in
the water are greatly weakened and the quality of the flowing water is
preserved.
The hydraulic performance of wooden stave pipes is even somewhat
greater than that of iron or concrete pipes.
Nor is the circumstance cited by Gross to be underestimated, that the
construction costs for wooden pipelines are smaller. It must, however, be
emphasized here that the types of wood cultivated by modern forestry
are at present almost unusable for this purpose, because today's
artificial forests almost throughout deliver timbers that possess neither
the properties


---


- 196 -
nor the resistance that belong to wood left in its natural state. Although the
forests in which humans as foresters have not yet intervened disruptively
have already become very rare, there are still enough remote woodlands,
spared by contemporary forestry and therefore still valuable, to which the
greatest attention must be paid if good, healthy water is to be delivered to
people again.
With the selection of suitable wood, pipes can be produced that largely
meet the necessary requirements.
If individual countries, such as America and Norway, have decided to use
wooden pipes on a larger scale, the reason lies largely in the quality of the
wood necessary for this that still exists there.
But the water can only then preserve its conduit system if regard is paid
to the inner laws of the water, that is to say if the substances separated
from it, serving preservation and construction, are directed to their
appropriate purpose.
That with the general deterioration of the water a qualitative decline of
the other foodstuffs must also occur need not, after what has been said
above, be particularly emphasized.
What damage was caused by the rapid draining of the water into the seas
shall be discussed elsewhere.
The capillaries of the animal or plant body serve on the one hand for the
conveyance of the blood, or of the saps, and on the other hand for the
simultaneous and constant construction as well as the preservation of the
capillaries themselves.
Therefore the supply pipe of the drinking water must also be
correspondingly constituted, because otherwise unsuitable processes take
place, leading on the one hand to the destruction of capillaries of the pipe
wall, and on the other hand to incorrect metabolic processes in the water
itself. These then manifest themselves in the human organism or in other
bodies in the most unfavorable sense imaginable.
We find something similar in all river courses. Experience teaches us that
watercourses whose inner lawfulness has not been disturbed attack their
banks little. On the other hand, there are no artificial bank regulation
measures that could withstand, in the long run, the destructive power of a
water whose natural course has been impeded.
The causes for this lie in the wrong method customary today, which does
not influence the water itself, which is what matters above all, but instead
regulates its banks.
The constitution of the walls of our drinking water conduits must above
all correspond to the inner lawfulness of the conveyed substance, because
otherwise it leads primarily to the destruction of the water conduits, and
secondarily to the destruction of the blood vessel systems, and thereby to
the everywhere-occurring

- 197 -

dangerous metabolic disorders, with which the increase of
cancer diseases is connected.
If the spring catchments are located far from the place of consumption, it
succeeds only with the application of quite special measures, and then
also only partially, to preserve the character of the water. By no means
can this be achieved, however, by today's manner of water conveyance,
which is dictated only by reasons of purely external and superficially

become somewhat more cautious in the choice of the pipe material.
In addition, in order to obtain the necessary quantities of water, the
spring water is often mixed with immature groundwater, which does not yet
possess the corresponding content of noble carbon substances. If the
water now becomes warmer on its long way — in pipes which, unfortunately,
are nowadays mostly made of good heat conductors — the carbon substances
contained in the water, and likewise the oxygen, become more aggressive.
What effects these phenomena can exert can be recognized, among other

The oxygen content also causes the organic substances contained in the

there likewise brings about transformation processes with the substances
that suit it at suitable temperatures — but in this case these are not
building-up processes, but phenomena of decay. Under such preconditions,
the consumption of this water becomes one of the main causes of the plague
of the 20th century: cancer.

On the consequences of purely mechanical drinking-water extraction

The purely mechanical measures for obtaining drinking water also lead,
in many areas located near the sea, to unpleasant surprises.
The equilibrium relationships between the fresh groundwater and
the seawater were thoroughly studied by Badon Ghijbens and later by
Herzberg. In the present case it is a matter of a hydrostatic equilibrium
problem of two miscible liquids of different specific weight.
Wintgens writes about this in the "Beitrag zur Hydrologie von Nordholland"
(Contribution to the Hydrology of North Holland), 1911, as follows:
Let the specific weight of the liquids 1 and 2 be G1 and G2, and the
difference of the two liquid levels after the onset of the

- 198 -

state of equilibrium be Hm; in this case the
separating surface of the two liquids will

lie at a depth of h1 - (G2 / (G1 - G2)) = Hm

below the liquid level.


---


This formula yields, assuming a specific weight for
freshwater = 1, and for seawater = 1.024, a maximum groundwater depth
of h1 = 42 × Hm.
In the example of Norderney described by Keilhack, the
freshwater surface lies about 1 to 1.5 m above sea level. This
H of 1.5 m would correspond arithmetically to a groundwater depth of 42 × 1.5 =
63 m. The actually determined depth of the groundwater
was about 50 to 60 m.
If now, through immoderate groundwater extraction with large pumping
works, the freshwater level is lowered — thus H is reduced — then the
boundary layer between freshwater and seawater will rise higher, until
finally this boundary zone reaches the region of the pump's suction head,
whereupon the salinization, or rather the increase of the
chlorine content in the drinking water up to undrinkability, sets in.
The mechanical-physical processes are further supported by the
metabolic processes between fresh- and seawater. With
every new pumping hole entering the ground, the penetration of
atmospheric oxygen into the boundary region between these
two types of water is facilitated. The temperature-gradient relationships
between the freshwater surface and the underlying boundary surface
are also changed. These two components act in such a way that the
internal rising forces in the water, which would preserve it at a certain
horizon, are likewise diminished.
On this occasion, attention should also be drawn to the salinization of many
mountain lakes, which was ultimately caused by the measures of water and
energy engineers. First, our rivers were deprived — by the aimless
deforestation — of the sun and heat protection that the
canopy of leaves of the trees had provided. Later there came, in addition,
watercourse regulations carried out according to
purely mechanical viewpoints. Both circumstances brought about a high
oxygen enrichment in the water, which then sought from the channel walls
the corresponding coarse and fine carbon substances, and tore them partly
from the bank walls, partly dissolved them out of the soil. If
this river water now comes into deeper, cooler lakes, where the previously
aggressive oxygen becomes concentrated, and the water is unable
to hold the quantity of now-dispersing carbon substances, then
a sedimentation of salts occurs — from freshwater a

- 199 -

seawater results. The reverse case arises in great ocean depths, where
strong overloading of the water with highly organized carbon substances
occurs and the water becomes not only fresh but also highly negatively
charged, whereby under certain circumstances genuine
thunderstorm formations can occur in the deep sea.

Conveyance of the Earth's Blood — Conveyance of Blood


---


Before we proceed to the description of the correct construction of a
water conduit pipe, one more example shall be presented,
which is intended to make clear to us the principle of a correct
conveyance of water.
If one examines the blood vessels of a snail, one sees that this
animal possesses two kinds of blood vessel systems of different color, whereby
the outer vessel systems carry lighter, and the inner vessel systems darker,
blood.
The constitution of the blood of the outer system is characterized by a
greater oxygen content and is essentially different
from that of the inner system, which has a higher content of carbon
substances. Investigations further showed that the
suspended substances are located in the middle, while the dissolved substances
are located more at the periphery of the capillary cross-section. Also the
speed of movement, imagined as projected onto a straight line, is
smaller at the marginal zones than in the middle. It must be noted here,
however, that this difference in speed is only an apparent one, since the
speed of movement of the inner liquid particles appears greater
than that of the outer blood particles only
because the latter must describe a path that roughly corresponds to a
screw motion within a screw, whereas the
inner blood particles perform almost only a simple screw motion.
The double helical line described by the blood corpuscles of the inner
systems cannot be perceived for the reason that the second
helical line is only an energy path, which the eye can no longer
perceive. This fact, however, has a much higher
significance, since we are concerned here with psychic upgrading processes,
which influence the "character" of the blood and, in the course of further
development, the character, or rather the psyche, of the respective
living being.
Only slowly, in various branches of research, is the conviction
gaining ground that — in deviation from previous
investigations — every task must be broken down into sub-problems from
the viewpoint of attending to the very smallest things.


---


- 200 -

The "material transformations in water," discussed often in the course of the
present exposition, are outwardly characterized by the so-called
"pulsation" of the water. Of this, hydraulics knows only that it decreases
with increasing velocity, but rises with increasing roughness of the channel
walls.
The water, therefore, possesses a certain inner vitality and, when it
rises in capillaries, plays a decisive role in the supply of the necessary
building-up substances.
In various places I have already pointed out that the rising of sap in a
tree cannot be explained solely by the physical factors brought forward so
far, such as the effect of external air pressure, etc., but that it must find
its explanation in the metabolic processes taking place in constant
pulsation in every cell of the tree — it is thus a consequence of the vital
activity of the capillary tree cells. Professor Kurt Bergel of Berlin arrives
at similar conclusions regarding heart and blood activity in animal beings.
He rejects the previous view that the motor "heart" pumps the blood into all
parts of the body. Rather, this work is performed by the millions of
highly active capillaries that permeate the body.
This capillary force, however, would only be capable of lifting to a
certain height. An external aid is therefore necessary. Bergel demonstrates
this with a small experiment. At the head end of a bundle of capillary tubes
placed with their foot ends in water, he delivers uniform light taps,
whereby he achieves a continuous overflowing of the water over the
head ends of the capillaries.
Health and disease are, according to his exposition, primarily
dependent on the flawless, or respectively disturbed, activity of the
capillaries. The unequivocal proof of this was supplied by Professor Bergel
through his investigations on a bird's egg. On an egg incubated for a short
time, a small red point appears, which on closer examination proves to be a
drop of blood. If the egg is incubated further, one can already perceive on
the yolk membrane a network of veins. Even shortly before
cooling, one can detect rhythmic pulsations.

The Double-Spiral Pipe

The double-spiral pipe fulfills, both in cross-section and in longitudinal
section, the preconditions that a water conduit pipe must exhibit if it is
to deliver healthy water to the place of consumption.
The masses of water conveyed in a double-spiral pipe experience, through a
blade system made of precious metal arranged on the inner wooden pipe wall,
a movement of such a kind that the individual thread of water at the
periphery describes a path which, at a


---


- 201 -

screw movement within a screw results.
Through this arrangement, centrifugal and simultaneously centripetal
forces appear in the pipe cross-section, which guide bodies heavier than
water toward the center, but press bodies lighter than water outward
against the periphery.
The water masses guided in this way are slightly warmed by the
mechanical interplay of frictional forces occurring at the blade walls,
whereby, at the inner circumference of the pipe, a splitting-off of oxygen
takes place and, as a further consequence, an enrichment of the same
occurs at the pipe periphery.
Simultaneously with the dispersed oxygen, all bacteria will also migrate
toward the periphery of the pipe, since they do not find the suitable
living conditions in the middle of the cross-section. Along with the
bacteria, all particles contaminating the water also move toward the
periphery of the pipe, whereby the water can simultaneously be
effortlessly cleansed of suspended matter.
Once the bacteria have migrated to the marginal zones according to
their oxygen needs, they are surprised there, after a certain running
time, by a certain oxygen concentration in the water completely sealed
off from external influences. As a result, precisely those pathogenic
bacteria that are sensitive to excess oxygen are advantageously
destroyed, while the non-pathogenic bacteria — those that are not
harmful to human health but are in many cases even beneficial — are
partly preserved.
Simultaneously with the splitting-off of the absorbed oxygen contained
in the water from the carbon substances likewise contained in every
water, an advance of the inner water core sets in, describing only a
simple screw movement, because the water is "physically relaxed"
through the aforementioned splitting-off of oxygen with respect to the
carbon particles.
As a consequence of this physical relaxation, a mechanical acceleration
occurs, and thus also a self-cleansing and an energetic charging of the
water masses rushing along the center. This energy charging, however,
in turn leads to equalization processes between the heavy bodies rushing
centrally and the energy-rich water, whereby, with simultaneous cooling,
a detachment of solid matter takes place, which are now once again
guided to the periphery. There they equalize with the oxygen and, in the
form of energies, reunite with the water rushing along the center.
Those particles that no longer found the way to the center are pressed
against the pipe wall by the mechanical pressure prevailing in the pipe;
there they come into equilibrium with the elemental substances that
once built up the wood, and they clog the pores of the wood, which


---


- 202 -

in this way becomes more resistant than iron, for example.
Once again this is a natural process, which in principle we find in the
formation of all capillaries, which not only build themselves up, but also
protect themselves against harmful influences.
Through the particular acceleration of the total water masses in the
twist pipe, larger quantities of water are now conveyed on the one hand
than in an ordinary smooth-walled pipe; on the other hand, through the
effectiveness of the oxygen, a far-reaching self-cleansing and
self-sterilization of the water occurs, whereby, through the continuity of
the energy charging, the water also becomes qualitatively better and
better along its way.
The cause of this is as follows:
Simultaneously with the acceleration, a cooling of the centrally guided
water masses takes place, whereby, in the water axis exhibiting the lowest
temperatures, a concentration of the carbon gases occurs, which
decreases toward the periphery of the pipe. On the other hand, the
oxygen concentrates at the periphery of the pipe, its highest aggression
appearing at the warmed wall, whereby, from the edge toward the
interior of the pipe, reciprocal relationships arise, and thus the
above-mentioned equalization phenomena occur, which upgrade both the
water and at the same time the wood.
In the course of time, a certain state of equilibrium — and thus the
termination of these processes — will finally set in, both for the average
spatial distribution conditions of the water flowing in the pipe and for
the equalization processes at the pipe walls; that is to say, the water is
ripe and has become almost insensitive to harmful external influences.
At the same time, the wood itself also becomes immune to external
influences.
Thus, if the oxygen is located in the marginal zone of the pipe on the one
hand, then on the other hand the free carbonic acid particles must, as a
consequence of the water temperature prevailing there, be found in the
marginal zone of the inner water core. The carbon substances contained
in bound form must accumulate in the water axis, which, as described
above, is most saturated with carbon substances.
Through a special arrangement of built-in, specially shaped blades, the
aggressive oxygen particles at the boundary layer — that is, those at the
outer edge of the inner water core — come into constant, direct contact
with the most aggressive carbonic acid. From this result continuous

the center.
Accordingly, two circulations arise in the cross-section of the pipe,
namely
a) the mechanical circulation of the water, and the energy circulation,
directed oppositely to it, of those energies which, when the aggressive
oxygen parts meet the free

This energy circulation presents itself in the form of a continuous,

These twist pipes also convey bodies heavier than water in the center,
and at the same time refine the conveyed material, so that, for example,

way yield, after smelting, a higher-quality iron, because during transport
the oxygen of the ore was consumed for the formation of new carbon
substances, which then contribute to the materially higher structure of
the carbon substance "iron."
